Miners and rumours reach the foothills, but no minister confirms anything until the treaties with Washington are exchanged and an emergency mining decree, an assay office and a royalty are in force. Before the exchange it says nothing false; afterwards it calls the reports promising gravels under survey. The silence protects the Republic, and it gives Elias's enemies at home a charge he can answer but never erase: that he registered his claims on state secrets.

The republic publicly administers claims under laws inherited from the Spanish and Mexican mining regime, including the 1783 Mining Ordinances, continued in force by the founding constitution. The legal survival of that regime has limits: a system developed for silver veins requires new rules for dispersed placer fields, while private land grants, Indigenous territories and overlapping claims remain bitterly contested. Gold attracts Californios, Sonorans, Oregonians, Hawaiians and Chileans; then large numbers of Americans arrive as foreigners subject to Californian law.

<!-- CR-034: the managed opening of 1848-1849 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The opening is managed. The decree of October 1848 opens the gravels to anyone who registers a claim and pays the royalty, and the first newcomers are already at work. In December President Polk tells Congress that there is gold in the Republic of California. By then San Francisco's harbor master is registering every ship and every passenger, and the more than hundred thousand who come in 1849 land with a paper naming the law they will live under. Hundreds of sailors desert the American squadron for the diggings. The Buchanan–Vallejo Convention obliges the Republic to return them, and its sheriffs find few; it is the first quarrel under the convention.

Elias is appointed Director of Mines in late 1848. He establishes an administrative framework for assays, claims, courts, mine safety, royalties and records. The **Mining Law of 1849** sets practicable sizes for placer claims and a **5 percent public royalty**, assessed at the mint and trade choke points. Foreign-miner licenses provoke political fights. American miners demand punitive treatment of Sonoran, Chilean and Chinese competitors. Elias resists their campaign and accepts a limited compromise: **$2 monthly licenses for residents and $4 for nonresident foreigners**. His principled insistence that useful labor matters more than ancestry wins partners and permanent enemies.

A mint opens in San Francisco in 1850 beside the State Assay Office. A new California gold coinage gives merchants a reliable means of settlement, and the state begins accumulating revenues from gold, customs, land and trade. Elias's private wealth from properly registered claims is considerable but separate from public gold, public debt and the nation's annual mineral production.

<!-- CR-034: the State Assay Office and Genth -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The assay office is older than the mint. It began in the secret winter as the room at Carquinez where Elias tested the Mokelumne samples alone, and it keeps the government's rule from those months: silence, but no lies. With the decree it moves to San Francisco as the State Assay Office, because a royalty is only as good as the assay behind it. From 1849 no one may sign an assay for the Republic without passing its examination, the first the state sets. In 1850 the office finds the ten-dollar pieces of the Miners' Bank of San Francisco underweight, coins the merchants have been taking at face value, and Elias publishes the result over their protests. **[APPROVED — CR-034]** Frederick Genth, back in Philadelphia that summer from a failed mine in North Carolina, analyses Californian ores on commission for the State Assay Office. In 1851 he accepts the Republic's offer: a laboratory, assistants, a salary that does not depend on clients, the right to publish and time for his own chemistry.

<!-- CR-049: On the Public Arts -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** That year Elias begins *On the Public Arts*, a memorandum he works on until 1854 and never publishes as a law. His own improbable education has taught him that ability is everywhere and opportunity almost nowhere, and Plutarch that a lawgiver works through habits rather than decrees; he has no foresight of what the school will find. Its central passage reads: "A people is as strong as the abilities it raises. Find ability early, train it hard, give it work and judge it by its works. Whoever holds an office holds it for its work, and no one may make him give a false account of that work: not a patron, not a minister, not I. A falsified account robs everyone who must build on it, and I will treat it as theft from the commonwealth. What we build must let those who come after us see further than we do, and beat us." On contest it says: "Give ability its trial and achievement its honour. Let the schools fight for able minds and the counties for the excellence of their works." It is the brief on which Zeuner and Sylvester are hired. Genth's contract is its first clause put into practice, the mining royalty pays for the rest, and its rules live in the school's contracts. The Republic's first computing is people: human computers, commercial arithmetic machines and checked tables for the assay office and the surveyors, with a register of readings, sources and corrections kept from the start.

The **Treaty of Monterey of 1850**, the work of the boundary commission created in 1848, settles the eastern frontier with the United States. From the 42nd parallel, California's boundary follows the 120th meridian south to latitude 39°, then runs southeast to the Colorado River at 35° north and along the Colorado to the Gila. A related agreement fixes the southern border toward the Pacific south of San Diego. California gains internationally recognized ports and access to Yuma while abandoning claims to the Great Basin it has never governed. The United States retains the inland routes and the Salt Lake country. In consequence, the Compromise of 1850 unfolds without an American state of California entering the Union, altering the arithmetic of sectional politics. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** The same arithmetic guards the Republic: Southern senators will not admit a free California, Northern Whigs and Free Soilers will not conquer one, and Washington's policy becomes waiting for the ripe fruit to fall. The Americans of the gold rush arrive as foreigners under Californian law, and the Republic's courts, licenses and careers make many of them Californians instead.

The gold rush brings growing English-speaking commerce and technical employment without extinguishing Spanish-language public rights. During 1849–1852, legislation establishes common-law courts and commercial practice while preserving vested rights and selected Mexican rules concerning land, water, marital property and mining. **[OPEN]** Exact statutes, transitional jurisdictions and claim procedures. English-speaking newcomers can enforce contracts, register enterprises and patents, enter technical education and seek public employment without changing their working language.

<!-- CR-071: the capital at Sacramento --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The legislature moves to Sacramento in December 1849, to the gateway of the registered gravels, and sits there through the flood of January 1850. Monterey, the republic's first capital, keeps the courts, and in 1856 the Confederation makes it its seat.

The election of 1850 strengthens merchants and American-origin settlers in the assembly. Vallejo succeeds Pico as president, offering a compromise to constituencies that fear the older Californio oligarchy. In August squatters contest the land-grant system at Sacramento and blood is shed. In 1851 San Francisco's Committee of Vigilance erects its own machinery of justice and executes men while claiming to protect honest citizens. President Vallejo hesitates before confronting a powerful body of merchants and armed volunteers.

Elias argues that public government cannot coexist with private courts enforced by wealthy factions. A constitutional convention at Monterey in 1852 establishes a Council of State and the **Superintendency of Mines and Public Revenue**, combining management of the gold economy, currency, public credit and expenditure on works. Elias receives the superintendency, a seat on the council and unprecedented influence over industrial policy. The Vigilance Committee is dissolved through an amnesty underwritten by the credible presence of the militia. Elias has no presidency; his strength comes from controlling an increasingly indispensable treasury. He makes public promises credible by paying contractors, opening technical careers and financing works beyond the initial resources of private investors. Immigrants arrive because these opportunities and ordinary legal protections produce visible results. Exact incorporation and patent measures remain **[OPEN]**; recruitment begins with the assisted passages of 1852.

<!-- CR-033: Native policy, the Land Fund, the register and the compact of protection -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-033]** In 1851 volunteer companies want the state to pay for expeditions against the Native villages. On Elias's figures Vallejo's government refuses: every expedition would be paid from the Republic's own treasury, and a militia that makes its own war is a private army. **[APPROVED — CR-033]** Commissioners sign treaties with the Native nations in 1851–52 instead. **[PROPOSED]** Elias keeps his promise to the Miwok guide: his people sign first, and their homeland becomes the pattern. **[APPROVED — CR-033]** A homeland stays the nation's own; it can't be sold, and it can be leased only through the Republic, which keeps a fee. <!-- CR-057: homeland leases --> **[APPROVED — CR-057]** Leases run long and are renewed when the nation consents, and a lessee who improves the land is paid for the improvements from the Republic's fee or by the next lessee. **[APPROVED — CR-033]** Beyond the homelands only the Republic may buy Native land. It pays in annuities of goods from a Land Fund kept outside the revenues pledged to London, and sells to settlers on instalments that carry the annuities, with the margin helping to pay for assisted passage. Each sale is entered in a public register, kept from 1852 by the Superintendency, and the entry is the title. Militiamen who serve under the Republic's command are paid in land warrants, good only on land the Republic has bought. **[PROPOSED]** Licence commissioners with mounted police keep order in the diggings and bring killers to trial. Squatters and volunteer captains call it robbery. Prices, annuity terms and boundaries remain **[OPEN]**.

<!-- CR-076: the spirits article and the ladder of 1852 --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** Every treaty carries a spirits article. No spirits enter a homeland except as the nation's council decides, no annuity, price, wage or debt is paid in spirits, and a trader who breaks the article loses his licence and his bond and answers in the Republic's court on the nation's complaint, under the nation's ordinance. In 1852 Vallejo's government taxes drink by its strength rather than its volume, each band of strength paying more per degree than the one below, so that beer and the Republic's wine pay little and spirits a great deal; the Assay Office tests the strength as it tests gold. The table Elias lays before the council is Thomas Logan's, a Sacramento physician who keeps the town's medical records: it shows where and when the diggings' killings happen, and most of them happen in a saloon after dark. The distillers' and saloon keepers' petition argues the trade and the revenue. The bondholders' agent supports the heavier duty, since what it takes on imported spirits at San Francisco is customs revenue pledged to London.

The republic's mining revenue finances infrastructure, but its debt to London remains. British bondholders hold claims secured by gold royalties and San Francisco customs. Elias has replaced the foreclosure that ruined his family's home with an even larger act of borrowing in the name of a state. Whether he will retain the ability to pay becomes a question not only of wealth but of sovereignty.

<!-- CR-035: the 1852 convention gives the Republic its school -->
**[APPROVED — CR-035]** The convention of 1852 also gives the Republic its school, without a schools law. The Ordinances of 1783, still in force beside the Mining Law of 1849, charge the miners' fund first with the support of the mining tribunal and its college. Elias reads the article to the delegates and proposes the same arrangement for gold: a share of the royalty for a school of mines, paid after the bondholders' coupon, over their agent's protest. Vallejo and Alvarado, Hartnell's pupils and the men who took the missions from the friars, sponsor it. The Church keeps its parish schools and its colleges; the state pays only for what it examines.

## 6. A Hawaiian friendship becomes a constitutional partnership (1849–1857)

The Hawaiian princes Alexander Liholiho and Lot Kapuāiwa pass through San Francisco with Gerrit Judd in autumn 1849. Elias rides with them into the foothills, where Lot's enthusiasm for horses, exertion, hunting and rough country becomes the beginning of a lasting friendship. Alexander sees California as a serious political possibility: an independent Pacific republic, neither American nor European, that could help his small island kingdom preserve its autonomy. During his American travels he suffers a humiliating encounter with a train conductor who mistakes him for a servant, and the incident deepens his misgivings about the treatment of Hawaiians by the United States.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** In 1849 the Rev. Silas Merritt brings a Methodist mission to Sacramento, with his daughter Abigail, unmarried at twenty-seven. She seeks out the famous young man she once mothered; she alone calls him Eli. Her father dies in the cholera of 1850, telling her to marry him. They become lovers, but in 1851 Elias sails for Honolulu as the princes' guest, drawn by a larger world than Sacramento's settlers. Abigail finds that she is pregnant and does not tell him. Only Caleb knows. She marries Henry Pruett, a San Antonio man who shared the Mexican captivity, and goes home to Texas, where Silas is born late in 1851, legitimate in law as Pruett's son.

In 1851 Elias visits Honolulu as the princes' guest. He encounters the royal household and meets their sister, Victoria Kamāmalu, born in 1838 to Kīnaʻu and Kekūanāoʻa, a granddaughter of Kamehameha I and a pupil at the Chiefs' Children's School. She is about thirteen; theirs is a passing, entirely non-romantic introduction. Hawaii is already a complex constitutional monarchy with a commercial and diplomatic world of its own, and Elias learns that the islanders do not need a Californian to invent political institutions for them.

William Walker's filibusters seize territory in Baja California and Sonora during 1853–1854. The Republic of California, which cannot afford to be seen as a sponsor of private American conquest, cuts off their maritime supplies. The expedition fails, and captured adventurers face proceedings in California. The episode demonstrates the value of naval power and earns goodwill from Mexican authorities without making them subordinate to Elias. <!-- CR-074: Ward and the trial of 1854 --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** That winter a young Salem sailor named Frederick Townsend Ward, who deserted Walker in Sonora, rides into California on a mule. The court questions him with Walker's men and lets him go, and he signs on a clipper for Hong Kong.

<!-- CR-071: the venture licence --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** In 1854 the Republic answers Walker with a law of its own: no one may make war abroad from its soil, and a venture abroad needs the Republic's licence and bond. <!-- CR-074: the first licences and Crabb --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** Sealers on the Mexican islands under Mexico's permit, Okhotsk whalers, guano men on the equatorial islands and traders bound for Hakodate take out the first licences. In 1857, when the Republic refuses Henry Crabb a licence for Sonora, he slips out across the Colorado with his followers, his backers in San Francisco are tried for fitting him out, and the Mexicans shoot him at Caborca.

In 1854, while Walker's men are tried, the Hawaiian government hears rumors of further American adventurers and contemplates a treaty of annexation to the United States. Elias offers protection, naval assistance and access to Californian industry **without annexation**. Alexander and the anti-annexation chiefs regard the offer as a way to preserve the crown. Kamehameha III dies in December 1854 with the American annexation treaty unsigned, and Alexander succeeds as Kamehameha IV.

<!-- CR-033: the Hawaiian princes watch California's Native policy -->
**[APPROVED — CR-033]** Alexander and Lot, who rode with Elias into the foothills, have watched how the Republic treats California's own peoples. Its treaties weigh with chiefs who fear what American settlers would do to theirs.

Victoria comes to San Francisco in 1854 with a Hawaiian diplomatic mission. Now about sixteen, she meets Elias as an increasingly serious student of law and statecraft. Their affection begins with long conversations about the security of small nations, the proper limits of rulers and the opportunities of the Pacific trade. He admires her political intelligence and composure; she sees an astonishingly energetic builder whose ambition might preserve both their countries, though his certainty already makes her uneasy. Their courtship develops over the following years as she matures into a stateswoman.

In 1855 Kamehameha IV appoints Victoria **Kuhina Nui**, granting her defined Hawaiian co-executive duties, a place in government and standing to exercise a regency. The office establishes authority within the kingdom; confederal decisions proceed through the institutions authorized by the interstate compact. As in history, she takes the office in January 1855, at sixteen, and presides over the Privy Council; no Hawaiian public document is valid without her signature. The office is not a courtesy offered to Elias's prospective bride; it is an independent legal foundation for her authority. In the **Treaty of Honolulu**, negotiated in 1855, she agrees to customs reciprocity and a Californian coaling station at Honolulu while refusing Elias's wish for a permanent base at Pearl Harbor. He respects her refusal because he needs a willing partner more than another hostile port.

The relationship between the two states grows closer. Alexander remains the political ally who understands the cost of dependence; Lot remains Elias's personal friend and occasionally his most uncomfortable mirror. Victoria and Elias envisage a shared Pacific order with a common navy and trade system while preserving the governments at Honolulu and Monterey. Their negotiations will eventually produce a confederation that precedes their marriage.

Kamehameha IV marries Emma in June 1856. **[APPROVED — CR-027]** Victoria has been betrothed since childhood to her cousin William Lunalilo, a match her mother Kīnaʻu arranged and the Hawaiian people love. Her brothers block it: Lunalilo's rank, estates and following on Hawaiʻi island would give the couple's children precedence over the king's own line. In 1857 they press David Kalākaua on her instead, and she refuses. Her answer survives in two songs of her own, *Pua Hau ʻo Maleka* and *Aita ʻOe e Parau*, which defy her brothers and claim the right to love the man she chooses. All Honolulu knows the man is Elias. She turns down both suitors for him.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** The brothers come round for their own reasons. Elias's children would rank through her alone, below the king's line; he claims none of her lands, which make her one of the kingdom's great landholders; and an American who has defied Washington takes her out of the reach of the missionary party that Wyllie believed had plotted in 1854 to put her on the throne. In **November 1858**, two years after the Confederation came into force, Elias and Victoria marry at Kawaiahaʻo Church in Honolulu, the church whose choir she leads. The wedding seals a partnership already legally binding, not a bride given in exchange for it. Elias is about thirty-four and Victoria twenty. Each expects to influence the other; neither yet imagines how dangerous that expectation will become.

<!-- CR-034: Victoria's reception houses from 1859 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** Victoria takes up the matter Elias has only paid for. From 1859 she opens reception houses for women at San Francisco and Honolulu. She recruits through the missions and the British, German and eastern consulates, and she finds the women places as teachers, nurses, seamstresses and clerks. Her husband's subsidy brought women to the docks; her houses keep them in the country. She also begins to ask, in the Council and at her own table, why the women her houses train may be certified as teachers but may sit no other examination of the Republic.

## 7. The Japanese connection: rescue, reform and the treaty (1849–1860)

Elias meets Nakahama Manjirō in California around 1849–1850. Manjirō has worked in the gold fields and knows the American maritime world, its languages and ships; Elias is fascinated by his description of Japan's institutions. They become friends, and Elias helps him arrange or finance the voyage home through Honolulu. When Manjirō lands in Ryukyu in 1851 and is questioned in Satsuma, his account reaches Shimazu Nariakira, a reforming lord hungry for modern technology.

In 1851 the survivors of the Japanese *Eiriki Maru* reach San Francisco, among them the thirteen-year-old Hamada Hikozō. The boy finds shelter in Elias's household and begins forming bonds that will give him a life beyond the historical accident of his shipwreck. In 1852–1853 a Californian ship carries some of the castaways through Naha toward home. The mission sends letters and gifts by routes that Japanese authorities can accept without surrendering control of their ports.

Nariakira, already improving Satsuma's industrial workshops, sees a modest Pacific partner without missionary demands as an alternative to stronger outside powers. He opens deniable channels through Ryukyu, while Abe Masahiro and shogunal officials weigh foreign dangers, domain rivalries and the need for naval modernization. Matthew Perry's actual plans put pressure on every participant. California's diplomacy changes the course of the American expedition, but the details of Perry's altered mission remain **[OPEN]**: an empire-wide coaling embargo cannot by itself credibly explain his failure to reach Japan.

Private exchanges grow into a **Shimoda agreement in 1855**, following tentative technical arrangements made in 1854. Californian machinists visit Satsuma's Kagoshima works; Japanese shipwrights, naval students and artillery specialists train at California's yards. Coal, provisions, silk, copper, tools and mutually advantageous commercial terms flow through the developing relationship. The shogunate does not simply hand Nariakira power to sign national treaties on its behalf.

The political danger deepens when Abe Masahiro dies in 1857 and Nariakira dies in 1858. With Ii Naosuke consolidating authority and persecuting political opponents during the Ansei crisis, Californian friendship becomes entangled in dangerous Japanese factional politics. Elias has already recognized a severe imbalance between Japan's domestic gold–silver valuation and foreign bullion prices; he warns his partners and offers financial expertise instead of maximizing the profit from their vulnerability.

**In 1858, California and the Tokugawa government conclude the Treaty of Edo.** It formalizes the partnership in commerce, shipbuilding, coal, technical exchange and naval training under the new political leadership. Japanese ministers accept the arrangement because it strengthens their options against larger Western navies without openly ceding the country's sovereignty. A number of Japanese factions distrust the treaty; the agreement is therefore an achievement of diplomacy rather than an inevitable triumph of Californian charm.

In March 1860 the *Kanrin Maru* arrives in San Francisco with Manjirō serving as interpreter. The voyage is an important public demonstration of Japanese navigational ability and the practical fruits of the **1858 treaty**, not a second treaty-signing ceremony. Experienced officers, engineers and merchants meet their Californian counterparts, inspect facilities and renew commercial relationships. Japan supplies technical advisers, training, observers, markets, harbors and resources. Large Japanese expeditionary combat forces are not yet part of California's military establishment. This restraint preserves the independence of the two powers even as their fortunes become increasingly interconnected.

<!-- CR-034: the Kanrin Maru at Carquinez -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The *Kanrin Maru*'s engineers spend their days at Carquinez, among machines whose drawings they have studied, and leave asking that Japanese students be admitted to the school, not only the yards.

## 8. The constitutional test of public money and private force (1850–1861)

California's revenues invite domestic conflicts that Elias cannot solve with engineering alone. The Sacramento squatter crisis of 1850 and San Francisco's Committee of Vigilance in 1851 expose the republic's weak courts and the ability of wealthy or armed private associations to govern in place of elected institutions. President Vallejo hesitates to confront powerful merchants. Elias argues that the treasury and the law must be defended by public institutions rather than competing private armies.

In 1852 the assembly reorganizes government at Monterey, creating a Council of State and elevating Elias to **Superintendent of Mines and Public Revenue**. He controls the collection and investment of mineral royalties, the mint, public credit and important works contracts but does not become president. He administers a financial state in which taxes, railway subsidies, foreign loans and port revenues are contested by legislators who can still frustrate him.

A regular Californian army is organized in 1852, alongside an academy at Monterey. The state develops its Benicia arsenal, Mare Island naval facilities and the foundries on the Carquinez Strait. Skilled immigrants and officers from several military traditions join Californio lancers, Mexican and Indigenous scouts, seamen and engineers. Marcus Vance, a relative of Caleb who joins the republic in the aftermath of the rush, gradually develops a professional command and military training system. Elias continues to direct money more readily than he can command troops.

<!-- CR-076: no spirit ration --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** The army and the navy have no daily spirit ration. Their ration is coffee. The one issue of spirits is ordered after a victory or a deed of the ship, and that is the only time it is issued.

<!-- CR-034: from rush to settlement, 1851-1856 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The rush becomes a settlement in the years when it could have ended. Since the Australian strikes of 1851 many diggers have sailed home. Most of the others stay, because a claim, a town lot or a farm in California now comes with a registered title, and from 1852 the Superintendency pays the passage of single women with a contract of employment and of whole families. Married women keep their own property under the Mexican rules the Republic retained, and from 1854 a woman who passes the teachers' examination is certified like a man. The same year as the first assisted passages, twenty thousand Chinese land at San Francisco. By 1853 the easy gold is gone. Water companies turn hoses on the old river gravels, quartz companies sink shafts at Grass Valley, and the men who remain become wage-earners, mechanics and shareholders. **[APPROVED — CR-033]** Holders of the old grants who come forward get a surveyed patent within months and pay its cost in land, which many Californio grantees call a tithe on their own property; squatters buy their improved plots from the Land Fund on instalments.

<!-- CR-034: Elias's priority; the 1855 panic; population -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** Elias makes women's immigration his own priority: a country of single men is a camp, not a republic. In February 1855 the failure of Page, Bacon's parent house in St. Louis brings down San Francisco's leading bank and two hundred firms with it. The mint's bullion and the Superintendency's credit carry the solvent houses through, and the Republic's money keeps its value. Emigrant guides in New York, Hamburg and Canton begin to say so. California, about 350,000 people in 1850, counts nearly 900,000 with Hawaii at the end of 1856, the Confederation's first year **[MODEL]**.

<!-- CR-034: the Carquinez school, its loops and its first chairs -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** In 1852 Genth's laboratory moves to Carquinez and becomes a school beside the foundries, separate from the army's academy at Monterey. In 1853 Genth reports that the Superintendency's own quartz mill loses most of the gold locked in sulphides, and Elias prints the report. Gustav Zeuner fought on the Dresden barricades in 1849 and is barred from teaching in Saxony. He comes in 1854 on the word of his teacher Julius Weisbach to build the engineering course: mathematics first, then the testing floor and the works. The school is paid by a fixed share of the royalty, so it grows with the gold. Each chair carries two paid assistants whom the professor chooses, and Genth and Zeuner fill theirs from Philadelphia, Giessen and Freiberg. From 1854 a chair also brings citizenship, with no religious or national test, to men whom their own countries have dismissed or would never appoint. Zeuner measures the water the hydraulic companies buy by the miner's inch and the debris they wash into the rivers, and from 1855 he tests the engines built at the foundries. Andrew Hallidie, who came as a boy in 1852, brings his wire bridges and the first wire rope for the quartz mills to its testing machine.

<!-- CR-049: the first precedents and the travelling fellowships -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Elias makes Genth's mill report a precedent on purpose: an honest, unwelcome conclusion will always have a home. On Zeuner's testing floor anyone can watch the difference between a convincing account and dependable performance, and the workshop begins to mark an apprentice's first accepted piece in its book, with a meal that names the teacher. From 1854 the royalty also pays for travelling fellowships, which send gifted young people where instructions run out: to the mountains, the islands, the sea and Japan. A fellow states the undertaking before the people who pay for it, and on return the records, accounts and testimony come before any honour; failed ventures and lost companions are remembered by name. The qualifying examinations still decide who may compete, but among those who have passed them, a fellow who brings back something no examination could test outranks the examination's top scorer for honours and selection. **[OPEN]** The expedition episodes themselves, which must carry real danger.

Throughout the 1850s, California and then Pacifica accumulate a mutually reinforcing community of engineers, manufacturers, financiers and skilled workers. Marcus's prewar recruitment and procurement relationships connect workshops with foreign suppliers and technical talent; public contracts, private capital, apprenticeships and established businesses give the network continuity before war interrupts its overseas connections. John Ericsson forms a central naval-engineering connection within this community. Theodore Judah, Andrew Smith Hallidie, Peter Donahue, Collis P. Huntington, Charles Crocker, William Chapman Ralston, William Kelly, Alexander Lyman Holley and Yung Wing participate in its development through settlement, employment, investment or sustained exchange. Ericsson's path and mentorship and seven collaborators' complementary industrial roles are developed below. **[OPEN]** Exact recruitment, arrival, residence, citizenship, contracts and prominence as distinct characters; Judah and Yung Wing remain included in principle without separately settled undertakings. Participation does not imply that all work together in one place or share identical political commitments.

Their combined influence compounds over years. Better tools and foundry practice improve engines and repair capacity; more reliable machinery expands mining, shipping and transport; growing revenues and commercial confidence support further investment, experiments and training. Successful workshops produce skilled teams and new enterprises, while failed prototypes leave useful measurements, techniques and experienced workers. Logan Turner, a boy from a Kentucky holler who reaches California as an adolescent, belongs to a younger generation educated within this developing community. Early familiarity with farm repairs, mills and river transport gives way to Carquinez apprenticeship and technical instruction; his exact migration and training dates remain **[OPEN]**. His practical discoveries arise from work with other skilled people. Pacifica's stunning rise rests partly on this expanding ability to design, manufacture, maintain and reproduce complex systems. Technical progress remains uneven across industries: capital, materials, fuel, transport and labor constrain what even an exceptional group can accomplish. Private interests and overseas partners retain their own purposes. Manufacturers own their works and can enforce contracts, yet increasingly depend on state orders; railway investors rely on guarantees and rights of way; officers and engineers build careers in expanding public establishments. Their successes create a constituency for the continuity of Elias's program. Some oppose his politics, others accept stronger administration to protect their undertakings, and some correctly warn that public dependence is becoming personal dependence.

Ericsson comes to California around 1855, initially on a paid commercial inspection and experimental assignment arranged through Marcus's eastern purchasing relationships. Disappointment with his large caloric ship and the failure to obtain construction of his 1854 armored-warship proposal make a new patron attractive without erasing his American attachments. Elias offers an experimental workshop associated with existing foundry capacity, protected commercial rights and paid trials for mining machinery and marine propulsion. Ericsson retains eastern commercial relationships, including Delamater's. Their confidence develops through demanding technical discussion, measured results and funding that survives an expensive failure. **[OPEN]** Exact invitation, intermediary, visit dates, contracts and first-meeting scene.

The visit becomes a sustained commitment during the working 1856–1858 period. Ericsson improves engines, pumps, screw propulsion, testing and the training of engineers and machinists. Small caloric applications remain separate from dependable steam propulsion for warships. During 1859–1860 on the working calendar, an experimental harbor-defense programme tests compact machinery, turret rotation, gun handling, armor arrangements and ventilation. Imported iron, guns and selected machinery supplement developing domestic production. The first fighting turret ship remains unfinished when war begins. Washington can observe this activity and develop competing designs; the programme is neither magically secret nor an instant ocean-going armored fleet. **[OPEN]** Facilities, budgets, suppliers, armament, dimensions and construction schedule.

Logan's adolescent migration places him within this community during its formative years. Donahue's workshops provide a proposed apprenticeship route into Ericsson's programme. Ericsson teaches him precision and the integration of a whole vessel; Logan learns from crews and repair workers how machinery behaves with poor fuel, wear and imperfect operation. Their respect includes recurring disagreement over how much a design may assume correct manufacture and handling. Logan leaves with the small eastern technical mission in 1861 on the working chronology, before the Columbia vessel enters combat. Ericsson remains in Pacifica when war forces a choice among his established commitments, supporting its naval defense while resisting automatic conversion of every technical undertaking into Confederate aid. American ties and the terms of his Pacifican residence remain consequential. **[OPEN]** Logan's precise apprenticeship and departure, Ericsson's legal nationality and wartime contractual limits.

The industrial community's work develops through three connected undertakings. First, Donahue builds manufacturing and repair capacity while Huntington organizes purchases and investment, allowing Ericsson's experimental naval design to reach production. Procurement begins before war restricts eastern suppliers: machine tools, suitable iron, selected machinery and trained people must be acquired while commercial access remains possible. Donahue bargains over workshop capacity and delivery dates; Huntington weighs availability, price and competing orders. **[OPEN]** Specific companies, contracts, imports, workforce, recruitment dates and the allocation of scarce equipment. Their historical Californian careers supply foundations for a different development programme rather than representing a wholesale subtraction of eastern industry.

William Kelly's metallurgical experiments begin a slower undertaking whose greatest effects emerge after the war. Sustained patronage allows imperfect trials to produce measurements, process knowledge and trained workers before dependable large-scale steel production is available. Holley contributes comparative engineering and assessment of foreign manufacturing and armor, then develops the means to turn process knowledge into reliable plant operation. Neither man's presence settles the chemistry, patents, raw materials, fuel or economic viability of a Pacifican steel industry. <!-- CR-073: the rolling capacity --> **[APPROVED — CR-073]** The rolling capacity comes after the war, from William Ralston's Pacific Rolling Mills, founded in 1866. **[OPEN]** Arrival, consulting arrangements, experimental works and sequence of technical successes.

Throughout the 1850s, California's sovereignty changes the destination and uses of its gold. Public royalties and reserves support its own credit, workshops and infrastructure; private bullion belongs to miners, merchants and investors who trade across borders. Pacifican banks and purchasing relationships increasingly connect mineral production with domestic industry and overseas suppliers rather than providing an assured stream into the American financial system. This develops before the Civil War and contributes to Pacifica's accumulated productive advantage. <!-- CR-068: the London pledge --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** In 1859 the Republic repays the London bondholders in full, and the pledge of its gold royalties and of San Francisco's customs ends. **[OPEN]** Annual flows, reserve policy, private capital movements, banking arrangements and the scale of the difference from historical eastern access.

The **Defense Law of 1855** establishes broad compulsory military service inspired by European, particularly Prussian, systems. Pacifist sects perform alternative service in an irrigation and engineering corps, carrying religious liberty into a demanding public obligation. Young men spend time with the colors and remain liable for reserve and territorial service. An emerging general staff plans movements by rail, river and telegraph. The new forces create a state considerably more capable than the California of 1846, although it remains dependent on foreign armor plate, coal, expensive heavy guns and credit.

<!-- CR-034: the engineer-corps privilege -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** Students and graduates of the Carquinez school serve their time in the engineer corps, building the roads, bridges and telegraph the general staff needs. Families who would never send a son to the ranks compete to send him to the school's examination.

As naval requirements grow, the two Pacific governments negotiate the next stage of their partnership. By late 1855 President Vallejo names Elias **First Minister**, head of the Californian Council of State, without transferring the presidency. Victoria, as Hawaiian Kuhina Nui, and Foreign Minister Robert Wyllie negotiate with him and other Californian envoys over customs, defense, naval command and the limits of shared authority. The controversy surrounding their increasingly public courtship makes every concession a question of both sovereignty and family.

In May and June 1856, while Elias is occupied by negotiations in Honolulu, San Francisco's **second Committee of Vigilance** challenges the republic's authority on a much larger scale than the 1851 movement. Its leaders portray the confederation as Elias's plan to place California under a combined personal dictatorship. William T. Sherman, then active in Californian banking and militia affairs, sides with constitutional government. Elias returns, rallies regular forces and loyal citizens, and breaks the vigilantes' claim to extralegal authority. He has now used organized force against Californians in defense of laws whose administration he increasingly dominates. Sherman remains in California after banking work ends around 1857, accepting an institutional military appointment under Vallejo and choosing Pacifican citizenship before the war. **[OPEN]** Exact appointment, naturalization and household decisions. He supports Victoria's restraint at Washoe in 1860. His relationship with Elias becomes one of professional respect and disagreement within Pacifica.

At Honolulu on **31 July 1856**, the anniversary of the restoration of Hawaiian sovereignty after the British seizure of 1843, the two governments declare the **Confederation of Pacifica**. Kamehameha IV, Kuhina Nui Victoria and Robert Wyllie sign for Hawaii; President Vallejo and First Minister Elias Thorne for California. The British consul witnesses, and a young Japanese observer attends through the developing Satsuma connection. The naming date is purposeful: the new polity presents itself as a protector of Pacific sovereignty, not a mechanism for erasing its members' autonomy.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** At the ceremony Victoria leads the Kawaiahaʻo choir. Late that night, among the court, Elias hears her chant a mele of her own composition. He has known her since she was thirteen; from that night her voice stays with him for the rest of his life.

California's assembly ratifies the Articles in September, narrowly; Hawaii's legislature ratifies in November, bringing the Confederation into force. California and the Hawaiian Kingdom retain domestic self-government and residual powers. The common institutions control defense, the navy, customs, currency and external relations. The equal member delegations of the Confederal Council elect the Chancellor, and Elias takes that office. The Confederal Assembly is elected by the property-holding men of the member electorates. California’s president and Hawaii’s king remain their states’ heads; there is no common head of state. The Council and its offices sit at Monterey, with a resident Hawaiian delegation; Honolulu remains the Hawaiian seat. Confederal acts are published in English, Spanish and Hawaiian. California retains English–Spanish guarantees, Hawaii its Hawaiian-language institutions. Japan remains a treaty partner. <!-- CR-059 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-059]** Residence, land tenure and the franchise are named as member powers, as Hawaii demands in 1855–56. <!-- CR-074: the Declaration of Paris --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** When the powers at Paris invite the world to give up privateering, the new Confederation answers as Marcy has answered for the United States: it will keep the rules on neutral goods and blockade, and does not accede; it will give up letters of marque when the great navies exempt private property at sea. It adds that it will issue no letter against a partner or a neutral.

<!-- CR-038: Hawaii abolishes penal labour contracts on joining the Confederation -->
**[APPROVED — CR-038]** Hawaii pays a price of its own for union. A republic that punishes no Californian for leaving his employer will not share a common market with plantations that jail Chinese labourers for quitting theirs. Over the planters' protests, the kingdom's legislature abolishes penal labour contracts in its ratifying session. From then on a plantation contract buys inspected housing, a protected wage and the passage home, and at the end of its term the worker may take his labour wherever he likes.

The Articles include an emergency **Article XIV**. During war or grave danger the Council may appoint a Protector by a two-thirds vote with assent formally recorded by both member governments through their authorized constitutional procedures. The one-year mandate brings joint military command, common customs administration and temporary emergency decrees, including specified restrictions on rights when necessary to restore public safety. The elected Assembly must be informed promptly and can revoke emergency measures; renewal requires both member legislatures. The initial mandate cannot amend constitutions or annex territory. Assent belongs to governments acting through institutions; no confederal article assigns the Kuhina Nui an exclusive personal countersignature. The limited mandate and member assent distinguish legal emergency authority from annexation or constitutional amendment.

A separate dissolution provision allows the Protector, acting with cabinet responsibility, to dissolve the elected Confederal Assembly and appeal to the electorate. An election must follow within sixty days, and dissolution may occur only once for the same cause. Member legislatures and the state Council remain in place. Dissolution does not revive a decree already revoked. Its defenders see an appeal to citizens against legislative deadlock; critics see a way to remove the body charged with checking emergency government. Cabinet responsibility does not give a single Hawaiian officer a personal confederal veto.

Constitutional change follows a distinct route: two-thirds approval in both confederal chambers and ratification by both founding states. Each provision has defenders who expect the others to contain it. Victoria and Wyllie secure Hawaiian representation and state assent, while Elias argues that a small confederation must act quickly enough to survive. None yet knows which interests future emergencies will mobilize.

<!-- CR-057: the Reserve Bank, 1857 --> **[APPROVED — CR-057]** The rescue of 1855 cost the Superintendency its works contracts that spring, and Elias learned from it that one office should not both spend on works and stand behind the banks. In 1857 the Confederation charters a Reserve Bank and gives it the mint's gold and the right of issue, over the protest of California's assembly, which loses the gold to a confederal bank. The Bank must keep two fifths of its notes and more than a third of its deposits in gold, and it may not lend to the treasury beyond what the year's taxes repay. Its first test is the panic of that autumn.

They marry in November 1858, with the treaty two years in force. Their private alliance and the political Confederation begin under compatible ideals but contain different assumptions about who may say no when strategic necessity becomes urgent. <!-- CR-075: Kalama Island and the promise (F5.3, F5.6, F-8, I-B1, I-B2) --> **[APPROVED — CR-075]** The same year the kingdom loses an island. The schooner *Palestine*, owned by San Francisco merchants and cleared under the Republic's venture licence, carries two American charterers to Johnston Island, which they claim in March under Washington's guano law. When the news reaches Honolulu the *Kalama* sails under the king's commission, and in July the king proclaims Kalama Island the kingdom's. In the autumn the Council advises him to yield: a rock is not worth a quarrel with Washington while the convention of 1848 stands, and the common navy has no ship to spare. Elias, back from Edo, gives the advice as Chancellor, and the king withdraws. On their honeymoon, in the winter rain at the head of Nuʻuanu above Kāneʻohe Bay, Elias tells Victoria two things he does not have to tell her: that he gave the advice, and that the ship sailed on his government's paper. Then he promises her the island back. **[PROPOSED — CR-075]** In 1859 the Confederation forbids any voyage it licenses or clears to take possession of land for a foreign power. Brooks's claim to Midway that summer costs him his clearance, and Gerrit Judd's to Palmyra costs him his.

<!-- CR-076: Lahaina, 1859, and the kingdom's law of 1860 --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** In September 1859 Alexander drinks for two days on an excursion to Maui, and late on the Sunday night at Lahaina he shoots his secretary and friend Henry Neilson in the chest. **[PROPOSED — CR-076]** Lahaina's settled ship's surgeon dresses the wound. **[APPROVED — CR-076]** Neilson is brought back to Honolulu when he can be moved and lives as an invalid in his cottage in Alakea Street until February 1862; the king never forgives himself. Victoria sits with Neilson there. She asks Elias for the Sacramento table, and he sets Thomas Logan's figures in front of her without comment; she knows by then that he drinks only when others pour. She stops drinking that winter, apart from the cup others pour her on a day that has earned it, and does not preach it. In 1860 she carries the kingdom's new spirits law with Lot's support. The clause forbidding any sale to a Hawaiian goes; one rule of strength, licence and hour applies to Hawaiian and foreigner alike, and opium is licensed under the same rule. The missionaries' party votes against the repeal, and the Hawaiian members carry it. ʻAwa stays under Hawaiian custom.

The external crises of the following years test those safeguards. During the **Utah War of 1857–1858**, federal troop movements near the border and the murders at Mountain Meadows expose Pacifica's inability to deploy a brigade quickly across the Sierra. **[PROPOSED]** Elias keeps a discreet channel through San Bernardino's Mormon leaders, preserving their land titles after Brigham Young's recall of the colony. He offers trade rather than military protection. The emigrants murdered at Mountain Meadows were bound for California; public hostility ensures that any later Mormon bargain will carry a political price. In 1858 the Fraser River gold rush draws workers north and Britain's establishment of British Columbia introduces a neighboring colonial administration whose interests do not always coincide with Californian shipping.

<!-- CR-059 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-059]** California's Oregon policy is commerce and nothing else. The Republic publishes its duties, carries Oregon's flour, salmon and lumber in bond through San Francisco and buys spars and coal for its yards on published contracts. Oregon's miners of 1848 work under the Republic's light licences and bring home dust that the State Assay Office coins at cost, so Oregon pays its debts in Californian coin. In the panic of 1855 the Superintendency's credit carries Portland's sound houses with San Francisco's. Oregon boys sit the Carquinez examinations and win their places like anyone else. Elias forbids anything more: no agents, no newspapers, no word of association. The Republic's treaties with Washington rest on its coveting nothing north of the 42nd parallel. The Superintendency keeps paying the passages of women and families after Oregon's donation claims close in 1855.

**[PROPOSED — CR-059]** In 1857 Oregon votes for a constitution that bans slavery and excludes free Black people. In June 1858 it elects Whiteaker governor and a legislature that chooses Lane and Smith as senators, all in anticipation of admission. The Senate had passed the bill in May 1858. The House takes it up in February 1859, just as the Republic's chargé opens talks to renew the commercial articles of 1848 with a note listing Oregon's produce and the port charges at Astoria and Portland. Elias could have held the note for a month and did not.

**[PROPOSED — CR-059]** John Sherman of Ohio moves for the correspondence, and Buchanan, who signed the convention of 1848, sends it. Sherman reads it to the House with his brother's letters from Monterey: a republic that drills every young man, coins Oregon's money, carried Portland's banks through the panic and in 1856 took a kingdom into its confederation by a vote of that kingdom's own legislature. Lane, Sherman says, advised Oregon to act as a state without waiting for Congress. Admit it now and he gets a legislature and a militia beside a foreign treasury that has shown how a legislature leaves. Keep it a territory, governed from Washington, until it has a constitution Congress can accept. Last he reads the Superintendency's passenger returns: men landed at San Francisco on Monterey's passages are drifting north into Oregon's valleys while Oregon's own land claims are closed. His brother's letters have changed his own mind, and he votes no. Horton and Leiter, two of his Ohio colleagues who leave the House in March and meant to vote for a free state, vote with him, and so do Wood and Foster of Maine, who learned from Kansas that whoever fills a territory owns it and will not admit one being filled on Monterey's tickets. Barksdale and McRae of Mississippi came meaning to stand by Lane. After Sherman sits down they confer with their delegation: Kansas has been held to the English bill's ratio since August, Oregon has half of it, and the free states took the Senate's lead only in May. Lamar argues with them and loses. They vote no.

**[PROPOSED — CR-059]** Hughes of Indiana and Stephens make the right argument: refuse Oregon and you send it toward Monterey; admit it and statehood binds it. But the man asking is Lane. On 12 February 1859 the House rejects Oregon's admission, 106 to 110. Colfax, who held eleven of his fifteen Republicans, says it as the House rises: Oregon has been sent to Monterey. Stephens leaves Congress in March without the admission that was to have been his last achievement. Oregon keeps a governor-elect, a legislature and two senators-designate, none with an office. When the commercial articles of 1848 come up for renewal, Buchanan's negotiators ask for a stronger privilege at Sausalito, and Pacifica refuses.

<!-- CR-034: the prestige year 1858 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** In 1858 the Carquinez school publishes two results that travel, in the first number of its *Mittheilungen*, sent in German and English to every technical library in Europe. Zeuner's method for slide-valve gear, worked out on Ericsson's engines, will set the valves of the harbor-defense engines. Genth, analysing quartz from Carson Hill, identifies a gold telluride that ordinary milling cannot touch and shows that roasting and chlorine recover it, and the Superintendency's mill that he shamed in 1853 now pays its royalty. Freiberg's journals carry both. The same number sets the school's first prize questions, with prizes in gold, and the answers come from men who have not yet decided to sail. Behind them come the engineers laid off in the panic of 1857, which the Republic's money has outlasted. The school now has about a dozen teachers and 120 students, and by 1862 twice as many **[MODEL]**. By 1860 it calls itself the Polytechnic, students apply from Europe, and engineers who have never seen the Pacific know where Carquinez is. It takes clever children at fourteen if they pass its examination, whatever their family; in 1861 one of them is Rafael Soberanes of Monterey. **[OPEN]** Contracts, salaries, buildings and the order of later appointments.

<!-- CR-036: scholars Europe will not promote: Sylvester, Remak, Cohn; Michelson as a boy -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** The offer of a chair with citizenship reaches men Europe will not let graduate or advance. James Joseph Sylvester came second in Cambridge's mathematics examination and could not take his degree, because he would not sign the Church of England's articles. In 1855, after ten years as an actuary, he is offered a professorship at Woolwich and a chair at Carquinez with citizenship, and he sails. Robert Remak showed that the embryo grows from three layers and that cells arise by dividing. He was the first Jew allowed to teach at Berlin, but never as its professor. In 1858 he takes the chair of physiology and pathology at the Methodists' new medical college in San Francisco. Ferdinand Cohn, kept waiting a year at Breslau before he might take the oath to teach, comes to the same college that year to study the smallest living things. At the gold camp at Murphys a Prussian Jewish merchant's son, Albert Michelson, is learning to read.

<!-- CR-049: the Calculation Office, the printing calculator and Peirce in Japan -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1855 Sylvester proposes the Calculation Office. Ten years as an actuary have shown him what a calculating staff can do for navigation, surveying, insurance and engineering, and he brings London's mathematics with him: Babbage's mechanical notation of 1826, and Menabrea's account of the Analytical Engine with Lovelace's notes of 1842–1843. In 1856 Carquinez commissions a Scheutz-type printing calculator from Stockholm, a year before the Dudley Observatory at Albany and the General Register Office in London buy theirs, and the race for the tables is on. Installing and adapting it takes 1858–1860; it computes particular tables well and nothing else, which is the Office's lesson about machines. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** In the spring of 1856 Sylvester offers Charles Sanders Peirce, the brilliant and difficult son of Harvard's mathematician, whose examination record is undistinguished, a place where achievement outranks rank, and the boy comes west at sixteen. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** He is the Office's youngest computer, and he reads Boole's *Laws of Thought*. In the autumn, after his seventeenth birthday, he sails with the Japanese partnership as the youngest fellow (1856–1857) to compare measures and calculation practice in the cooperating workshops. At the Dutch-run naval school at Nagasaki his hosts test him as hard as he tests them and keep the better of his methods, and he learns how much of a numerical account rests on assumptions nobody wrote down. He accounts for his work before he is honoured, and returning fellows become the school's admired examples. **[OPEN]** The journey's dangers.

<!-- CR-035: the Pacifica League: grants per examined graduate, the colleges' bargain, women from 1862 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-035]** From 1855 the Republic pays a grant for every graduate who passes its examinations in the fields it needs. The mint, the Superintendency and the licensed professions take only examined candidates, and the engineer corps only examined men. The Jesuits of Santa Clara are the first to bargain. They want the grant and the corps privilege for their Californio boys; the Republic wants surveyors and engineers who can work in Spanish. The price is the Carquinez examiners in their halls. The bishop calls it simony from the pulpit, and the rector signs. From 1859 women may sit the state examinations, and when in 1862 the grant also requires the colleges to admit the women who pass, the Jesuits hold out a year longer than anyone. The Methodists' college opens a laboratory medical school in San Francisco in 1858, and the Oakland college trains the Superintendency's statisticians. The Hawaiian kingdom pays Oahu College on the same terms for navigators and Pacific linguists for the common navy. Each college keeps its chapel and its Latin; none is paid for them. By 1860 the newspapers call them the League.

<!-- CR-049: the Laboratory of Light, Electricity and Matter; Kate Layne and uranium -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1859 Genth founds the Laboratory of Light, Electricity and Matter inside the school, with photographic recording, mineral analysis and electrical measurement for its practical work. The challenge evening begins there as a laboratory habit: a researcher sets out what is established, what is conjecture and what would settle it, and an appointed respondent and the room attack it, the people who built the apparatus and ran the calculation included. A conjecture earns a trial before it earns belief. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** In 1860 Kate Layne of Folsom, born in Tennessee, passes the Republic's examination at fifteen, one of the first women to do so, and joins the laboratory's recording staff. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Niépce de Saint-Victor has reported in the *Comptes rendus* that uranium salts darken photographic plates in the dark, and explained it as stored light; nobody has followed it up. The laboratory's cabinet holds Joachimsthal pitchblende and the uranium yellows the Austrian state's colour works there has made since 1853. Kate asks Genth to let her try. She tests the effect against darkness, black paper, months of storage and every uranium compound the school has, and adds a gold-leaf electroscope, which discharges near uranium at a rate that follows the uranium content. In 1861 Genth publishes that the activity belongs to uranium itself, under her name and his. By 1864 thorium is found active too, Peirce has fitted the electroscope's readings, and pitchblende proves several times more active than its uranium can explain. Something else is in the ore.

The Pike's Peak rush establishes new mining districts around Denver, often through men accustomed to California's mining courts. The discovery of the Comstock lode in Washoe in 1859 is especially provocative: the silver lies on the American side of the carefully negotiated 1850 border, but much of the credit, capital and workforce comes from San Francisco. One group of miners demands annexation to Pacifica; others prefer an American territorial government.

<!-- CR-034: Ott's first Washoe assay -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The first assay of the Washoe ore is made at Nevada City by James J. Ott, an assayer the Republic licensed, and San Francisco's money moves before Washington's officials arrive.

The decisive warning comes in **1860**. The killing of miners who had abducted Paiute women leads to retaliatory violence and the Pyramid Lake campaign. After Numaga's warriors defeat a volunteer column under William Ormsby, Elias recommends negotiations over the border and disputed claims but agrees to an armed relief force to retain support for the naval program. Jack Hays commands some five hundred Californian volunteers across the boundary and defeats Paiute forces. The immediate tactical result cannot erase that Pacifican troops have operated inside territory claimed by the United States, with serious consequences for Paiute communities and the legitimacy of California's stated principles.

Washington threatens retaliation, dispatching troops and naval forces; Britain refuses to support a war of Californian expansion <!-- CR-068: no bondholders in 1860 -->. The Council debates activating Article XIV. **Victoria organizes opposition in the Hawaiian government and delegation**, supported by Wyllie, legislators and military critics including Sherman. She argues that a confederation formed to preserve sovereignty cannot treat an unfavorable boundary as an invitation to war. Hawaii withholds the governmental assent needed for Article XIV, and the coalition defeats escalation through the existing institutions. Elias must accept his first unambiguous constitutional defeat; Victoria has won by persuasion and political organization.

At the **Carson Convention** in autumn 1860, Pacifica withdraws its troops and recognizes American sovereignty over Washoe in exchange for property guarantees and transit rights. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** The convention also renews, for a further term, the commercial articles of 1848, which had run out that year: the ports, the Sausalito storehouse, commerce and the rights of American residents. The crisis becomes a formative wound for Elias: his financial and military power does not suffice when his own constitution allows an organized coalition to block him. It also makes him respect, and increasingly resent, the wife whose judgment has frustrated his plans.

<!-- CR-068: Lincoln's election and his minister --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** The crisis runs through the American campaign. Lincoln, the Republican candidate, makes no speeches, and his opponents revive his vote of 1848 that the war was unconstitutionally begun, the war that ended with California outside the Union. He wins in November without the Pacific coast's votes. In March 1861 he sends his oldest friend, Edward Baker, a San Francisco lawyer who has never taken Californian citizenship, to Monterey as minister resident.

The government embarks on intensified preparations for future emergencies. By Fort Sumter in April 1861 Pacifica has a regular army and trained reserves, unfinished Sierra railway works, telegraph lines toward Carson, a Colorado River flotilla, imported heavy artillery and some armored warships. The country can mobilize far more effectively than in 1846 but cannot match the Union's peak military power. Its young army has limited experience of extended large-scale war, and its navy is dangerously dependent on foreign fuels, engines and plate. The precise force totals are recorded as scenario estimates in the historical and planning files rather than asserted here as established fact.

<!-- CR-033: the Confederation in 1861; Native villages; scouts -->
**[APPROVED — CR-033]** By 1861 the Confederation counts about 1.2 million people **[MODEL]**. Pacific shipping has brought Australians and Chileans as well as Americans moving west, and more miners have bought land and stayed. Measles, smallpox and malaria meanwhile sweep the Native villages of California. **[PROPOSED]** From 1861 men of the treaty nations scout and guard the Sierra routes and the telegraph.

<!-- CR-059 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-059]** The Oregon Enabling Act of 2 March 1861 offers statehood under a new constitution without slavery or exclusion, and Oregon is to elect its delegates in June.

# PART II — The struggle for North America (1861–1867)

## 9. The republic faces the American Civil War (1861)

Fort Sumter's bombardment in April 1861 transforms the Confederation's strategic position. California and Hawaii have survived as separate states within Pacifica, but their shared customs, fleet and communications are now exposed to a continental war. Washington remembers the Washoe incident as a violation of American territory; California remembers American threats to its independence. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** Secession also removes the Southern bloc whose votes had made every proposal against California a sectional impossibility since 1848. For the first time, the men who call 1848 the Trist surrender sit in a Congress the South no longer balances. **[APPROVED — CR-028]** When war comes, the American naval storehouse at Sausalito is sealed and its stores interned. Southern agents solicit bullion, shipbuilding and recognition, while Northern representatives ask the confederal government to supply or finance federal armies. The republic's American-born inhabitants carry divided loyalties into its legislative and military institutions. <!-- CR-068: two Sumters --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** Lincoln has refused Seward's proposal of a foreign war, with Monterey among its objects, and while he sends ships to Charleston he sends none to Sausalito. His proclamation of 19 April, which treats Confederate privateers as pirates, reaches the Pacific squadron weeks later.

<!-- CR-064 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-064]** In May 1861 Henry Halleck takes the first Panama steamer after the news of Sumter. He is a San Francisco lawyer whose firm has carried half the Mexican land grants through the Republic's register for American buyers, and the president of the San José railroad. He wrote the American army's book on the art of war. He has never naturalized and has refused every Californian commission, because he always meant to go home. Scott wants him in Washington, and by August he is a major general of the regular army. Sherman sees him aboard. Sherman made the other choice in 1857, and his oath, his household and his office are in Monterey. Neither man asks the other to change sides. The Republic has no law by which to hold a private American before the war, and Elias does not look for one. The Union's best-informed officer on Pacifica's army is soon in Washington.

The loss of dependable access to Californian gold and Pacifican credit becomes a continuing weakness in the Union's war finances. Once Pacifica is an enemy, Washington cannot count on its government's reserves or assistance, and established commercial channels are disrupted. Gold can still reach Northern buyers through private and third-country transactions, including London; access costs more and depends on exchange, credit and confidence. Northern taxes, bond sales and paper currency continue to finance armies and industry, while specie obligations and overseas purchases keep demand for coin and foreign settlement alive. Under the working arrangement analogous to historical wartime finance, customs duties and public-debt interest remain payable in coin. <!-- CR-069: the Union's suspension, the run on the Sub-Treasury and the premium -->**[PROPOSED — CR-069]** From July no treasure steamer clears San Francisco for New York. Chase's loan from the banks of New York, Boston and Philadelphia is paid out in coin to the Treasury's creditors, and the coin comes back to the banks more slowly than it went, for nothing from the coast arrives to make up the loss. In November the banks will take the last part of the loan only as credit on their books. Chase refuses and pays the army, in part and late, in the Treasury's own notes, payable in coin at the Sub-Treasury. When London's answer on the *Trent* reaches New York in December, the notes' holders line Wall Street to the Sub-Treasury's door, and the United States stops paying its own notes in coin twelve days before its banks stop paying theirs. Then the noteholders and depositors turn on the banks, and the North's banks suspend at the end of the month; Pacifica's do not. From January 1862 gold is a commodity in New York. Its premium is higher than it would be with the coast's gold arriving, and it moves on the news from the Columbia as well as from Virginia. Gold still reaches New York by way of London, at a price.

<!-- CR-069: the bankers' memorial and the choice of paper -->**[APPROVED — CR-069]** In January 1862, the day after Lincoln has told his quartermaster-general that the bottom is out of the tub, a deputation of New York bankers brings the Treasury a memorial: the coast sent New York forty millions in gold a year before the war, no loan can replace it, and the steamers will sail again on the day Washington makes terms with Monterey. Lincoln answers it with the Legal Tender Act of February. The Union will pay for its war in paper rather than buy back the coast's gold with peace, and from then the price of gold in New York is the daily price of that choice. The bankers who signed the memorial remain a party for peace with the West. **[MODEL]** The amounts of the Union's paper and the premium's figures.

The Confederate states' secession and Union mobilization do not create an effortless opportunity for Elias. The United States retains formidable armies, railways, factories, experienced officers and the capacity to raise forces far beyond anything Pacifica can sustain alone. In the Confederation, an army of around twenty thousand regulars with approximately ninety thousand trained reservists is a planning estimate, not an invincible host. Naval purchases and equipment still depend on foreign credit and supplies; the Sierra railway remains incomplete. The two American belligerents each have able commanders who learn from their enemy's methods.

<!-- CR-075: Baker's note (E4, E-1, E-6, I-B3) --> **[APPROVED — CR-075]** In the first week of May 1861 Baker delivers Seward's note invoking the convention of 1848: California's ports are to be closed to the insurgents' vessels and agents, and deserters returned. Elias and Vallejo answer jointly that the article covers the enemies of the United States in its wars, and that Washington calls the South a rebellion. If it is a war, the article applies, and so does the South's belligerency. Elias tells Baker in person that the article binds California if Washington will admit a war, and that the Confederation's ports already receive no privateer of any flag. **[PROPOSED — CR-075]** The rule is Hawaii's of 1854, adopted for all the Confederation's ports in 1856. **[APPROVED — CR-075]** From then on every article of 1848 is a question Washington can ask and Monterey has to answer.

A Union captain intercepts a Hawaiian packet outside American territorial waters, removes Hawaiian subjects and a suspected Confederate privateering agent, and sends the detainees to Vancouver. A Hawaiian sailor dies during the boarding. The intelligence has substance, but the officer exceeds his authority. Victoria initiates demands for protection and redress, requiring disclosure of Elias's preliminary Southern dealings. The Council invokes Article XIV with formal assent from both member governments and appoints Elias Protector for one year. Victoria helps win Hawaiian support and takes political responsibility for the authorization; Vallejo supports California's assent. A separate resolution authorizes limited coercive operations, with civilian protections, no forced allegiance, no annexation by emergency decree and renewed negotiations when adequate redress becomes possible. **[OPEN]** Exact participants and instruments. <!-- CR-068: the Protectorship's dates -->**[APPROVED — CR-068]** The Protectorship takes effect on 1 June 1861, and each yearly term ends on 31 May.

Demands and attempted interim arrangements precede a July offensive on the working calendar. A limited crisis becomes a territorial war through choices rather than inevitability. Victoria's advocacy implicates her without eliminating political opposition or conditions on renewal.

<!-- CR-068: Lincoln's offer, the two pages and his answer, the reply laid before the governments, the state of war and Baker --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** In June Lincoln, in public and through Baker, offers release, an inquiry and compensation for the dead sailor's family if the expedition gathering at San Francisco stands down. The offer is his own.

<!-- CR-075: Burch's address, the sittings and Victoria's argument (C6, D1–D7, F2.2, F1.1, D-1 to D-13, F-2, F-7, C-4, C-8, I-B4 to I-B6) --> **[APPROVED — CR-075]** In the third week of June John Burch, member for Trinity, moves an address in the Confederal Assembly asking the Council to name the Rockies as the Republic's frontier, and Oregon, Washington, Utah and New Mexico, the lands his open letter of November 1860 invited into the Republic, as its objects. Elias does not answer at once. He calls the Council to two closed sittings in the last days of June, in the manner of the Laboratory's challenge evenings, and each part of his estimate of the war is given by the person who knows it: Sherman on the Union's generals and the length of the war, Ericsson on the Union's yards, Marcus and the Calculation Office on what can reach the Sound, Huntington and the Assay Office on the interior's trade, Ralston, who has not yet taken the Republic's oath, on New York's gold, and Broderick, by letter, on how the Union's Democrats will vote if the war is long. Burch's address is read at the sittings. Sherman, as respondent, attacks every part. He insists that a limited war stays limited only if its objects are written down so that they can be met. The minutes are sealed. In the same days Victoria puts the kingdom's case to the Council from its weakness. Hawaii gave its defence to the common navy so that it would never have to ask a great power for a warship, and the warship Washington would send to protect Honolulu is the kind that boarded the packet. The king's letter refusing Wyllie's wish to recognize the South is read after her, and the refusal is the king's own act.

**[APPROVED — CR-068]** Elias lays the offer and his reply before the Council before he answers, with seven reasons that are printed with the reply; the last is that the Republic will not see the South conquered and the Pacific taken next. He offers to accept and stand down if the Council prefers the offer. The reply asks Washington to renounce the search of neutral vessels in Pacifican and Hawaiian waters, to bind itself by treaty to prosecute no inhabitant of the Columbia for trading or corresponding with the Republic and to keep the writ of habeas corpus open to them, and to let Oregon's convention meet with no army of either government in Oregon. The Council concurs on the ground that the American squadron's last Pacific stations are the Columbia and the Sound, and Hawaii's delegation obtains shore batteries and two steam frigates for Honolulu at once, and the second turret ship when the Columbia is secure.

<!-- CR-075: the preamble, the prices and the sealed minute (C2, C1.5, C3.2, C4, C7, E1–E4, E9, C-1 to C-11, E-7) --> **[APPROVED — CR-075]** The Council's war resolution, voted with the concurrence, opens with the objects of the Confederation in the war, printed with the reply and the reasons in English, Spanish and Hawaiian: no American station, storehouse, garrison or fortified post on the Pacific slope, or on any island of the Pacific from which the Confederation or either of its members can be struck; a treaty between equals in place of the convention of 1848, and a frontier drawn by consent; no search of Pacifican or Hawaiian ships in their own waters; no people taken by decree, none on the slope given up who has chosen the Republic, and protection for those punished for the choice; and no army sent east, no conquest on this continent, and loans to any American people that holds its own ground. What the Republic keeps at the peace it will pay for, and it will treat on these objects at any time and ask nothing beyond them. Every later resolution of the war names the object it serves. Victoria has won the third object as Hawaii's own and moved the first object's island clause herself. Vallejo moves the second object and declares that, with the convention gone, the Republic keeps as its own rule, owed to no one, the pledge of 1848 never to cede, lease or place under protection any part of its territory to a European power. The fourth object's narrow wording is the Council's answer to Burch. California's assent has reasons of its own: a returning Union would bring courts to try its titles, a tax on its miners and a Senate never bound by the Republic's treaties of 1851–52 with the nations; the convention of 1848 has become a leash; half of San Francisco's money is at risk; and its Americans, of both halves, will consent only to a war that sends neither half east.
