California's delegation asks one thing for itself, and the Council pledges it: no land associated with the Confederation in the war becomes a member without the consent of both member states. In a minute sealed with the sittings' record, the Council promises to support at the peace the kingdom's claims to the islands of its domain, including any island the kingdom takes in the course of the war.

**[APPROVED — CR-068]** This is early in July. Baker has no power to accept the reply and asks for his passports. The offensive opens in the middle of the month. On Baker's dispatch and the first report of the crossing, Lincoln reports to Congress in its special session where the war began; the fall of Astoria is known in Washington only after Congress has gone home. He asks for no declaration, but Congress recognizes a state of war with the Confederation of Pacifica before it rises on 6 August. Pacifica's papers have already printed his first inaugural's sentence on the perpetual Union beside his speech of 1848 on the right of revolution, without comment, and his message of 4 July, which reaches Monterey in the third week of the month, answers them: the right belongs to a people who can use it themselves and can name their wrong.

<!-- CR-075: Beale's letter (E7, E-8, I-B7) --> **[APPROVED — CR-075]** In the last days of July Edward Beale writes to Andrés Pico, on his own initiative, asking whether he stands by the objects. Pico answers for them at all hazards and offers lancers, his brother Pío says the same, and the letters are printed in Spanish and English. **[APPROVED — CR-068]** Baker goes east and is killed at Ball's Bluff in October.

Sherman organizes readiness and reserves as Inspector-General, initially supporting protection, then holding every operation to the objects the Council has written and challenging each that stretches them. <!-- CR-067 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-067]** The Protector commands within the Council's war resolution, which is the army's mandate; an operation beyond it needs a new resolution. He commands through the General Staff that Marcus Vance built, and its department commanders answer to him; the Inspector-General reports to the Council as well, and every soldier swears to the Confederation and its law, not to the man who leads it. The common fleet is governed by a Navy Board at San Francisco, with a Hawaiian member, under a commodore who commands at sea.

## 10. The Northwestern campaign, the interior and the Rio Grande (1861–1864)

Marcus Vance commands the expedition while Thorne directs its political aims. Sea transport and a limited overland advance from northern California support operations without a completed Sierra crossing. Federal defenses strengthened after Washoe are dispersed among the coast and interior. George Wright and Silas Casey preserve competent opposition. Pacifica takes Astoria, Portland and Vancouver on a working July–September calendar; Wright evacuates mobile troops and usable stores toward the upper Columbia. Olympia and the abandoned establishment at Steilacoom fall during autumn. Control beyond major settlements and routes remains uneven.

Casey defeats a Pacifican landing against Port Townsend. Marcus had reservations about support but approved the operation and accepts responsibility. Union troops preserve a northern anchorage and interior concentration, giving later reinforcements somewhere to arrive. Sherman inspects reconnaissance, command, naval cooperation and casualty recovery; he criticizes actual failures while rejecting political scapegoating of Marcus for government objectives. His findings give Victoria evidence for renewal conditions. **[OPEN]** Forces, losses, precise positions, alternate appointments and operational schedules require audit.

<!-- CR-059 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-059]** The convention the Enabling Act called never meets at Salem. When Marcus takes Portland, Oregon is still a territory of the United States. Its territorial legislature and county commissioners negotiate a civil compact with the occupation command. They choose John Whiteaker, whom Oregon elected governor in 1858 for a state never admitted, to chair it. He negotiates civil protections without conceding a governor's right to transfer Oregon's allegiance. The legislature's acts take effect without the absent federal governor, subject to the compact's court. Courts and county governments continue, requisitions are paid and disputed in civilian courts, no one swears allegiance, and Oregon's men, ports and revenues serve no military purpose outside Oregon. The Puget Sound counties sign a parallel compact, and Matthew Deady, a territorial justice, becomes the compact's court. In the Willamette flood of December 1861 an officer seizes a relief steamer for the garrison. Deady orders it released and the command refuses. Victoria puts compliance before Hawaii's legislature as the price of its assent to renewing the Protectorship, and the officer is overruled. From 1862 the customs collected at Astoria and Portland go into a published Oregon account that pays Oregon's courts and county officers. Unionists retain competing authorities; commercial accommodationists and Southern sympathizers have distinct interests. Washington Territory's federal administration withdraws to the surviving enclave. Indigenous nations make separate decisions about passage, supplies, land and protection; settler petitions cannot speak for them. The occupation develops costly policing, garrison and supply obligations. Recognition and later association require peace and constitutional agreements, not emergency annexation. **[OPEN]** Local officeholders, succession and boundaries.

Washington sends a limited expedition to hold its foothold and support recovery. The 11th and 13th Iowa, followed by the 15th and 16th, are sent through Panama to the Sound. Lancaster and a Pacific consort protect communications; Pensacola is the candidate additional seagoing diversion from the historical Gulf assignment. **[OPEN]** These are planned allocations, not audited strengths or sailing schedules. Retained Pacific units are not counted again as newly diverted Eastern forces. The surviving federal enclave ties down Pacifican troops during winter and spring 1862.

The first operational Ericsson turret ship helps secure the lower Columbia during a federal recovery attempt in late spring or summer 1862 on the working timetable. Its difficult machinery and armor sections are manufactured or procured through California's industrial network, then shipped aboard conventional transports for assembly at a protected lower-river yard after the 1861 conquest. Donahue organizes difficult manufacturing, installation and repair arrangements, drawing on workshop teams; Huntington helps secure the equipment and materials on which the programme depends. Imported specialists and local shipwrights share the work. This avoids depending on a low-freeboard prototype making a hazardous coastal passage and bar crossing under its own power. **[OPEN]** Yard, hull construction, cranes, skilled labor, transport, material supply, exact completion date and vessel name; the assembly scheme requires a capacity audit.

Inside sheltered estuarine waters, the armored battery contests a passage and landing operation that Pacifican wooden vessels cannot safely hold alone. Its resistance to ordinary gunfire and heavy turret guns disrupt federal naval support and damage a leading vessel. Mechanical trouble or battle damage limits its own action, making supporting vessels, pilots, crews and Marcus's troops essential. The federal commander withdraws vulnerable transports rather than persist in an inadequately protected landing. Pacifica retains its lower-river supply route and gains time to consolidate. Union forces preserve a surviving fleet, northern foothold and capacity to adapt through shore batteries, obstructions and combined operations. The ironclad cannot patrol the entire coast, occupy territory or reverse the earlier Townsend defeat. **[OPEN]** Commanders, ships, location, tactical sequence, malfunction, losses and relationship to the audited reinforcement schedule.

**[PROPOSED — CR-059]** In October 1861, with Portland fallen and the posts east of the Cascades cut off from the sea, the War Department orders them to march out by the Mullan Road. The regulars Wright carried up to Fort Walla Walla can no longer go down the river, and the Mullan Road over the Bitterroots is closed by snow until summer. They winter in a valley stripped bare by the worst winter anyone remembers. There is no flour to buy, no hay, no barley, and the cattle are dying in the fields. Their requisitions wait for the river to open, and when it opens it is Pacifica's. Washington has no volunteers it can bring to Walla Walla, no recruits and no gold. The valley's farmers sell on Portland's credit for coin and take the quartermaster's drafts only at a discount the post cannot pay. The Coeur d'Alene and Spokane, who remember Wright's hangings of 1858, will let no Union column come west. After the attempt on the lower Columbia fails, the Walla Walla convention of July 1862 lets the regulars carry out their own government's order: they march east with the honours of war, guided by John Mullan on the road he built. Mullan resigns his commission in the spring of 1863 and comes back to his property in Walla Walla to carry Pacifica's mail on his road.

**[PROPOSED — CR-059]** Lewiston stands on a townsite the Nez Perce lease through the Confederation. The Boise Basin rush of 1862 is fed by Portland's steamers, Salt Lake's freighters and California's roads, and its gold is coined by Pacifica's assay office at Idaho City, by 1864 the largest town in the Northwest. Boise grows beside a Pacifican post. Washington organizes Idaho Territory and sends a governor by way of Fort Benton. The Protector's mandate forbids annexation by decree, and Hawaii's legislature renews it each year, so Pacifica lets him govern what he can. The Basin's Southern Democrats use his organic act to govern themselves. His successor's accounts are later found short by the money Congress voted for a Nez Perce treaty that was never made.

<!-- CR-068: the Port Townsend convention --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** In the winter of 1863–1864 Washington calls Oregon's displaced Unionists and the delegates elected in 1861 to sit at Port Townsend as a convention and adopt a state constitution, which it sends to Congress with a memorial for admission. Pacifica publishes a safe-conduct and a passage for any delegate who wishes to go. Few go, and Congress does not admit the state.

Finance and transport sustain the gains. <!-- CR-069: the run, the Agreement and the coin bonds -->**[APPROVED — CR-069]** William Ralston, Ohio-born, takes the Republic's oath in July 1861, with his family in Ohio and his partner Eugene Kelly in New York. **[PROPOSED — CR-069]** No wire carries news to an enemy: the Union's line, finished in October 1861, carries nothing for Pacifica past Fort Churchill. When the news of New York's suspension reaches San Francisco by the Panama mail at the end of January 1862, in the month of the flood, the depositors run first on the houses with partners in New York, and Ralston's is at the head of the line. He pays every one of them in gold across counters stacked with coin, the other houses send coin across the street when he calls for it, and by evening the line is bringing it back. In 1855 the Republic's mint carried the solvent houses; this time they carry themselves, because the coin is still in their vaults, and the Reserve Bank, chartered for such a day, stands ready and is not needed. **[APPROVED — CR-069]** Elias means to stop the export of coin by decree. Ralston asks him not to and brings him the houses' word instead, and on the steamer day of 1 March they sign the Steamer Day Agreement: no coin to New York by way of London, no exchange sold on New York, while the Republic is at war with the Union. The city's papers print every steamer's treasure by shipper, and the signatories show one another their London consignees. Most keep it, and the Republic owes them for a measure it never had to decree. **[PROPOSED — CR-069]** When the Republic borrows for the war that year, Ralston brings the houses to take its bonds in coin from their reserves; the Republic spends the coin on its own foundries, yards and carriers, and it comes back to the banks. Every month the premium in New York tells Ralston what keeping the Agreement costs his own firm, and he keeps it. **[OPEN]** The interest on the coin bonds and their amounts beyond the model's. Crocker develops construction teams able to repair track, build wharves and establish short connecting or portage routes; he cannot promise a completed mountain railway wherever the army advances. Hallidie's wire rope, lifting equipment and bridge experience improve material handling and vulnerable supply connections. **[OPEN]** The amounts of particular advances, routes and crossings. An emergency advance, restored crossing or critical equipment shipment remains a possible incident rather than a newly fixed battle outcome. Skilled workers, including Chinese construction crews, retain agency over labor conditions and participation.

**[PROPOSED — CR-060]** Southwestern and interior expansion follows the Union's wartime distraction and Pacifica's supply routes. In July 1861 the Union abandons Camp Floyd, and its regulars march east; and Fort Churchill's regulars go by the Salt Lake road the next summer while Lot Smith's Mormon cavalry guards it for the Union. In January 1862 a convention at Salt Lake writes the constitution of a State of Deseret that Washington has never recognized, with a Pacifican envoy sitting quietly among the guests, and in March its voters elect Brigham Young governor. In July Lincoln signs the Morrill Act against the church's marriages and property. Young asks for protection through the old San Bernardino channel. Elias sends a garrison first and terms afterwards. Sherman and Victoria say that a garrison on a new road needed the Council's vote; Elias calls it an invited guard and asks for none. In October 1862 Pacifican troops reach the Salt Lake road by Honey Lake and the Humboldt, under Jack Hays, to guard the mail and the telegraph, with Edward Beale to speak for the Republic to the nations of the road and Jefferson Hunt of the old Mormon Battalion to speak for it to Brigham Young. Hays posts his men at the old Camp Floyd and along the road and leaves the city to the Nauvoo Legion, Deseret's militia.

**[PROPOSED — CR-060]** In December 1862, while Elias is in New Mexico, his commissioners and Young's counsellors sign working articles for passage, supply, the garrison's posts and a confederal court for crimes against travellers on the roads. The articles run to the end of 1863 at the latest and promise nothing against the Union's law. Lincoln's governor leaves by Fort Bridger in the spring of 1863, and Utah's legislature goes on sitting without him.

**[PROPOSED — CR-060]** Young will not sign a compact the Protector has not signed, and Elias will not bind the Republic to the Nauvoo Legion, the largest armed force in the interior that belongs to no nation, before he has seen it. He comes by the Humboldt road in July 1863. On the 24th, the anniversary of the Saints' arrival in the valley, the Legion parades, and he and Young sign the Compact of Association, an agreement about passage, supply and defence under the Protector's mandate, not an annexation. Elias gives in person the pledge his commissioners could not: Pacifica will not suffer the Union's law to be enforced in Deseret by arms. It publishes its terms of passage, leaves Deseret's courts and church property alone and buys Deseret's wheat in gold. Deseret supplies the road, gives passage to the interior campaigns, and binds Lot Smith's cavalry and the Nauvoo Legion to the common defence of the roads and the valley. At Elias's demand the Compact extends the travellers' court to crimes on the roads of any date. The garrison's posts are named, the old Camp Floyd, the Bear River crossing and Fort Bridger, none within a day's ride of the city. **[APPROVED — CR-015]** Brigham Young surrenders John D. Lee in 1863; Pacifica tries and hangs him for Mountain Meadows. Young obtains protection at a price he resents. **[PROPOSED — CR-060]** He gives up one man, at the signing, and the others are not found. Membership waits for the peace. Elias will not ask Hawaii's legislature or his own Assembly to vote a polygamous state into the Confederation in the middle of a war, and Article XIV gives him no power to make one. Confederal law forbids member states from banning marriage on grounds of race or origin; it does not impose one marriage system on every state.

**[PROPOSED — CR-060]** In January 1863 Hays, the road's commander, marches on the Northwestern Shoshone's winter camp on the Bear River with warrants for the men who killed the Bannack miners that month, and orders not to touch the lodges. Bear Hunter's men fight him for a morning from the ravine and draw off into the hills with their families; the column does not follow. In the summer, with Elias present on his way from Salt Lake, the bands sign the Box Elder compact: annuities paid from the road's own fees, Cache Valley's grazing paid for through Deseret, Shoshone scouts on the Snake road, and the killers given up to the travellers' court by their own people, those of 1862 among them now that the Compact reaches crimes of any date. Washakie's Eastern Shoshone make their own terms for passage at Fort Bridger and keep their distance from both armies.

**[PROPOSED — CR-061]** Sibley's Texans come up the Rio Grande in the winter of 1861–62 for the road to their Pacific supplier as much as for the territory, and lose it at Glorieta Pass at the end of March to Fort Union's garrison and the Coloradans, who burn their train. The Union's only foothold on the Pacific is its besieged enclave on Puget Sound, and New Mexico is the one place it can keep Pacifica and Richmond apart. Hungry in a valley the campaign has stripped, the brigade goes home to Texas, except a rear guard at Mesilla that waits for Pacifica.

**[PROPOSED — CR-061]** In April 1862 the Council votes to open and hold the southern road and the valley of the Rio Grande against the armies of the United States, and to treat with Richmond's forces and not fight them. <!-- CR-075: the resolution's objects (C7.2, C-11) --> **[APPROVED — CR-075]** The resolution names the fourth object, for the valley's people who trade with the Republic, and the fifth, for Richmond's forces. Sherman says the valley is beyond the slope and nobody in it has asked, and Victoria's party records his objection. **[PROPOSED — CR-061]** When the spring grass comes up on the Gila, a Pacifican column of about eighteen hundred men leaves Fort Yuma under Caleb Vance, in detachments small enough for the desert wells. The Confederate company at Tucson rides away before the column arrives. At Apache Pass in July the column fights the Chiricahua for the springs, and Pacifica's own Apache war begins on its own road. The column pays in coin for everything it takes and posts its prices in Spanish and English in every plaza. At Mesilla in July its advance cavalry has bought the mill's flour when Sibley's rear guard comes to requisition it, and the alcalde asks the buyers to guard what they paid for. Their officer, Edward Eyre, does, and when the Texan wagons come up to the mill his men fire. No government ordered the clash at Mesilla in 1862, and both prefer to forget it. Among the Texan dead is Captain Henry Pruett of San Antonio, who shared the Mexican prison with Caleb twenty years before. Caleb, who has ridden ahead with the advance to see the valley he must feed, finds him and keeps his watch.

**[APPROVED — CR-061]** Canby does not give up the river. Halleck refuses his request to go east, and through the summer Canby stocks Fort Craig, builds a redoubt at the Valverde ford, puts a company on the Mesa de la Contadera, pickets the Jornada del Muerto and the mountain track by which Sibley's brigade escaped in April, and keeps the 1st Colorado at Albuquerque to cover the depot. The column spends August and September buying the harvest of the Mesilla valley and, through the river merchants, of Socorro and the Rio Abajo behind the Union's lines, in gold against the Union's discounted notes. Early in October Mesilla's carreteros lay water up the Jornada in plain sight and the main body follows with the heavy guns, and Canby's spies report what he expects: Sibley's February manoeuvre again. A battery goes up on the lava ridge east of the fort, beyond the reach of its guns, and a thousand men cross by night at the Texans' old ford and lie hidden on the west bank below it. Meanwhile some six hundred picked men with pack howitzers and Socorro guides have left the river at the Palomas, as if against the Apaches, and climbed the track through the San Mateos and round the Magdalenas. At its northern end, near La Joya, they take Canby's picket in the night, and on the day the freighters have named they come down above Socorro, take the supply convoy for Fort Craig with all its mules and dig in at the narrows across the road.

**[APPROVED — CR-061]** Canby does what a good commander must: he leaves seven hundred men in the fort under Colonel Marshall Howe, marches north with thirteen hundred and his wagons to reopen his road, and sends for Chivington. When a courier reports that his column has passed Valverde, the hidden thousand march north in the dark on the wagon road that skirts the fort and by dawn are entrenched across the road behind him. The light column holds the narrows for a day against Canby from the south and against Chivington's Coloradans, who try a ford to get at its pack train, from the north; that evening Canby learns that his road home is shut, and he turns back. The second Valverde, in October 1862, is fought on the ground of the first for his wagons. Canby attacks the entrenchment from the north while Howe comes out of the fort with half its garrison and strikes it from the south, and between them they open a way through the bosque by the river. Regulars, Coloradans and Carson's volunteers fight their way through, Carson's men standing as they stood against the Texans; the road and the ford approach will not pass a wagon, and Canby brings about a thousand men back into his fort and leaves every wagon and draft animal behind. Some four hundred Union soldiers and two hundred Pacificans are killed, wounded or taken **[MODEL]**. Caleb sends his surgeons into the fort under a flag, as Canby sent medicines to the Texans after the first Valverde. Chivington falls back up the river and fires the Albuquerque depot, and the townspeople pull its stores out of the flames and sell them to the column, which enters the town without a fight; at Cubero forty New Mexico volunteers take their discharge and go home.

**[APPROVED — CR-061]** Santa Fe cannot be held without water or a fort, and Carleton, at Fort Union, has too few wagons to empty it. Its garrison burns the military stores with care for the city and signs a convention that leaves the hospital with its sick and wounded, the territorial archives, the churches and private property under Pacifican protection, then marches out over Glorieta. The column enters Santa Fe in November 1862 and lifts the Union's martial law the same day. **[PROPOSED — CR-061]** Governor Connelly, too sick to govern, withdraws to Las Vegas with the territorial government, and his secretary, William Arny, goes on claiming the whole territory for the Union in his name. **[APPROVED — CR-061]** When word reaches Fort Craig that Santa Fe has fallen and Taos lies behind the column's lines, Kit Carson, who fought the column on the river because an oath is owed on the morning of a battle, resigns his commission, which Canby accepts, rather than hold a fort through the winter against the column of the boy who read to him by the fire while his own household lives under that boy's protection, and rides home through the column's lines under a flag. Canby, short of food and unwilling to keep men whose colonel has gone, discharges the New Mexico companies one at a time, and the column gives them safe-conduct and rations home.

**[PROPOSED — CR-061]** In November 1862 Elias leaves Monterey by the southern stage road, through Yuma, Tucson and Mesilla, and comes up the Rio Grande to Santa Fe in December, on his way to Texas. **[APPROVED — CR-066]** From Santa Fe he rides up to Taos, where the families of the men hanged in 1847 have petitioned the column to try the judges and jurors who are still alive, and where some of his own officers would oblige them. Carson, a private man again, takes him to the hanged men's graves, because no one else in Taos will. The Taos and Rio Arriba companies coming home from Fort Craig hold sons of the rebels and cousins of the jurors, and they will follow Carson. His price for bringing them home into the compact, as its home guard under their own captains, sworn to its laws and not to Pacifica, and never ordered against the Union, is that no one be tried for 1847, on either side. Elias gives up the trial he wants. The amnesty becomes an article of the Rio Grande compact, and Carson the compact's broker in the Hispano towns.

**[APPROVED — CR-061]** Fort Craig, without transport and with the column between it and Fort Union, cannot march two hundred and fifty miles through the winter. Halleck orders it held: it pins down some six hundred and fifty Pacificans, and while its flag flies the Union still claims the river. He means to send no relief. A department cannot be run from inside a besieged fort, so in January he makes Carleton its acting commander at Fort Union, and Canby keeps the fort. Some seventeen hundred men, and fewer than a thousand once the New Mexicans have gone, live in a post built for two hundred, on half rations from January, and the camps outside its walls lie under the guns on the ridge. Canby does not break out against the six hundred and fifty: they hold earthworks with breech-loaders and rifled guns, and without wagons he could not get far if he did. In January the 1st Colorado goes home, because Colorado itself now faces Pacifica across the divide, and regulars from Fort Union hold Pigeon's Ranch. Through the winter the column buys the Rio Abajo's grain in gold, carries it up in hired carretas and sells it at cost in Santa Fe's plaza, where the Union had carried off the town's stores in March and the Texans had taken the rest.

**[APPROVED — CR-061]** In January 1863, with Caleb on the road to Texas with Elias, the Pinos Altos miners seize Mangas Coloradas, who has come in under a flag of truce, and bring him to Caleb's deputy, Joseph West. West reads the miners Caleb's standing order, that a chief who comes in under a flag goes home under one, refuses to hold him and sends him back to his people with a written safe-conduct. Mangas's own Chihenne keep a truce from that winter; Cochise's Chiricahua still raid the road now and then, and travellers cross Apache Pass under escort. **[PROPOSED — CR-061]** In Santa Fe the territorial assembly, which met on 1 December as it had been summoned, sits on through January with José Manuel Gallegos in the speaker's chair, although the acting governor orders it to Las Vegas, and its members open the Rio Grande compact of 1863 with Elias at the turn of the year and carry it on with his commissioner, Pablo de la Guerra, after he leaves for Texas. In the spring it is signed at Santa Fe by the members from the counties Pacifica holds, the probate judges and the town councils, with Miguel Antonio Otero's name first and Gallegos's beside it, though the two have been rivals since Otero took Gallegos's seat in Congress. Gallegos, who wanted New Mexico a state and wanted no Texan in it, signs when the San Antonio articles are read in the plaza, Otero's members keep him in the speaker's chair, and the compact lets any congregation keep its chapel and call its own priest. It gives the towns elected government, courts that sit in Spanish and English, the territory's own institutions of 1850, and confirmation of the old grants, with each community's commons confirmed to the community, and it organizes Arizona county for Tucson and the Gila. The signatories accept one clause unwillingly: when the peace comes, debt peonage and the captive trade will end, with compensation. From Las Vegas, Arny offers New Mexico, in Connelly's name, free soil and homesteads, and no statehood; Francisco Perea and José Francisco Chaves stay with the territorial government. **[APPROVED — CR-061]** The Pueblos make their own agreement, and their grants enter the register as homelands. **[APPROVED — CR-015]** In Navajo country the government chooses a different course. **[APPROVED — CR-061]** It makes no campaign. In December 1862 Barboncito and Delgadito come to Santa Fe to ask for peace while Elias is there, and Barboncito will speak of nothing else until the New Mexican slaving raids on his people stop. Patrones of the Rio Abajo urge the campaign the Union would have made; Elias wants no campaign, and Pacifica has neither the men nor the forage for one. The towns' slaving expeditions are forbidden, the Navajo raids stop, and at the beginning of May the agreement is signed at Cubero with de la Guerra.

**[PROPOSED]** Richmond renounces its Arizona claim in return for credit and the future railway connection. **[APPROVED — CR-061]** At San Antonio in February 1863, where Elias has come from Santa Fe, the Sunset articles give the Confederacy the use of the southern road, the Sunset road, but no right to march on it. Colonel James Reily, who carried Sibley's letters to the governors of Chihuahua and Sonora the year before, signs for Richmond, and General Magruder for the military district. No Confederate soldier enters New Mexico in arms; Texan freight changes to New Mexican carts at El Paso; a slave whom a master brings onto Pacifican soil is free; a fugitive is refused at the crossings of the Texas line, and Texas may police its own side; but one who reaches Pacifican soil is free and is not given up, as under the Mexican law the Republic was founded on; and Texas gives up its old claim to New Mexico east of the river by a resolution of its legislature at Austin. In April Reily is killed in Louisiana.

**[APPROVED — CR-061]** In March the column's regulars with Ute guides march up the San Luis Valley from Taos and take Fort Garland's herd; Carson, unarmed and on his own account, goes in to keep the post from being burned over men he knows, and carries a written convention between the two commanders. At Fort Craig the quarter ration runs out in April. When the winter has settled that no relief is coming, Caleb, back from Texas by the river road, offers Canby evacuation with arms and colours, and Fort Craig's garrison marches out in April 1863: about nine hundred and fifty regulars and Coloradans, fed by Pacifica on the road to Fort Union. On the news Fort Garland falls to the Rio Grande garrison without an assault: in May 1863 its Colorado garrison goes home over the Sangre de Cristo pass with Pacifican rations and leaves the post standing. **[PROPOSED — CR-061]** The front settles at Glorieta Pass, where the column's wagon road from the Rio Grande meets the Union's from Fort Leavenworth, seven hundred miles long, and neither side can feed an army beyond it. South of the defile it runs along the Manzano and Oscura mountains, with the river Pacifican and the Pecos country the Union's. **[APPROVED — CR-061]** From the spring Manuel Chaves, who guided Chivington's flank march at Glorieta, leads the Union's New Mexicans against the carretas in the Galisteo country and takes a whole train with its oxen, and Pacifican patrols watch the Las Vegas road. **[PROPOSED — CR-061]** Santa Fe Trail caravans cross the line under Union permits, because Santa Fe pays for its cloth and coffee in Pacifican coin and Washington is short of gold.

Idaho's mining districts turn toward the Northwest in 1863, western Montana's camps toward Salt Lake and Walla Walla in 1863–1864. <!-- CR-060 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-060]** Deseret's freighters work the Montana Trail on the Compact's terms, and the camps at Bannack and Alder Gulch sign supply compacts for freight at published rates, Pacifican coin, assay and licensed courts. The courts come too late for the winter of 1863–64. In January 1864 the Alder Gulch committee hangs Henry Plummer, Bannack's sheriff, who had once been a city marshal at Nevada City in California, and its members say that Pacifica's courts sent him to them. A Pacifican circuit judge arrives in the spring, and the committee keeps its rope beside him. Lincoln's governors of Idaho and Montana keep their offices and little else. **[APPROVED — CR-060]** In the summer of 1864 a steamer lands a company of the Union's regulars at Fort Benton with orders to garrison Montana's new capital at Bannack. Elias orders nothing. Young, whose freighters live on the Montana Trail, sends Lot Smith's cavalry of the Legion north on Deseret's own decision, beyond the roads the Compact binds it to keep; it is at Bannack first, and the regulars turn back at the Sun River without a fight. The gold of Alder Gulch stays inside Pacifica's freight and coin. When the peace of 1865 leaves western Montana on Pacifica's side, the Republic pays the Legion in gold for that summer, the only pay it ever has from Pacifica. **[PROPOSED — CR-060]** Fort Benton stays a Union landing, and in the June rise the steamers from St Louis trade both ways across a line that does not yet exist.

Washoe is retaken in 1863–1864. <!-- CR-060 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-060]** Through 1863 the territorial volunteers of James Nye, Lincoln's governor of Nevada Territory, at Fort Churchill eat California's flour, bought with drafts the Comstock's merchants discount, and after December 1862 the garrison's only road to the Union runs through Deseret. Elias leaves them there. The Terms of Monterey, printed in August 1863 and adopted by the Council in September, say that Pacifica asks no state of the Union and holds itself free to occupy any territory Congress acts to make one. In January 1864 Washoe's voters reject the constitution their convention wrote, which taxed the mines. In March Congress passes an enabling act for a second convention: a state cannot be ceded, so a State of Nevada would lock the Basin into the Union. Pacifica retakes it in the spring of 1864, without a fight: Elias moves in the first weeks the Sierra roads allow. Washington closes the overland wire to all traffic but its own. Sherman insists that the garrison leave with its arms, and Victoria writes Hawaii's conditions into its assent to the third renewal: no annexation, the Carson Convention's guarantees kept, and Hawaii's control of entry at its own ports. She also asks that Washoe choose for itself after the peace, and that is not written in. Those of Fort Churchill's volunteers who will not stay march east by Deseret's road, fed at Pacifica's charge, and Nye goes with them. Washoe's legislature and county officials sign a civil compact on Oregon's terms, and Pacifica publishes a tax on the mines' net proceeds lighter than the one Washoe has just refused. No second convention meets, and there is no State of Nevada. **[OPEN]** Exact forces and routes; civilian and Native authority require their own agreements.

<!-- CR-069: the offer published; the Bank of California -->**[APPROVED — CR-069]** In the spring of 1864, with gold climbing in New York, a letter from the firm's own partner there offers the house Union paper for its coin. Ralston gives the *Alta* the letter without the sender's name and says the house refused it. **[PROPOSED — CR-069]** That year he and his partner Joseph A. Donohoe, who wants the firm's future in New York, quarrel in private over it and part in public as friends, and on 4 July he and Darius Ogden Mills open the Bank of California with its capital paid in gold coin. In September its agency at Gold Hill begins to bring the Comstock's silver to San Francisco.

## 11. The three-sided war and its terrible costs (1862–1865)

At Hampton Roads in March 1862, the absence of a timely Union turret ship turns Virginia's attack into a more substantial Confederate victory. Cumberland and Congress are destroyed; the loss of the grounded Minnesota, by enemy action or evacuation and burning, is the working additional outcome. <!-- CR-064 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-064]** Federal wooden ships withdraw to the anchorage under Fort Monroe and the Rip Raps, which Virginia, drawing twenty-two feet and making four or five knots, cannot reach. Cornelius Vanderbilt gives his great steamer to the government, and she comes into the Roads fitted to ram, with two more steamers beside her. McClellan's transports land his army at Fort Monroe from 17 March as planned. The James stays closed to them. He advances up the Peninsula by land, the batteries at Yorktown and Gloucester close the York to the Navy, and he is held at Yorktown. When Norfolk is evacuated on 9 May, Virginia can neither go up the James nor go to sea, and on 11 May her crew destroys her.

The consequences of the altered armored fleet continue along the Atlantic and Gulf coasts. <!-- CR-064 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-064]** On 15 May 1862 Rodgers takes Galena, the little *Naugatuck* and two wooden gunboats up the James to Drewry's Bluff without a turret ship beside him and is beaten off; Galena takes the whole fire of the bluff. She survives, holed, and is laid up for repairs that summer. The 1863 Charleston assault requires different timing or composition; the historical failures do not become additional Confederate victories merely because the same attacks are not mounted. The rapid Union capture of Atlanta at Wassaw Sound cannot be assumed without an equivalent to the historical Weehawken. **[OPEN]** Whether the Charleston and Wassaw operations occur, alternative ships, outcomes and the wider blockade effects.

Union shipbuilding responds through other designers, yards and types. Galena and New Ironsides provide alternatives to Ericsson's design, while Pook and Eads sustain the distinct western river-ironclad programme. The Union loses the historical Monitor's timely intervention and the same rapid sequence of combat validation and Ericsson-derived production. It retains an industrial economy capable of replacement designs and learning. The difficulty lies in delivering suitable armored ships to the right theatre in time. <!-- CR-063 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-063]** Without a proven turret ship the Navy orders broadside ironclads on the pattern of *New Ironsides*, big, slow to build and heavy in plate. **[MODEL]** Seven join her by the end of 1864, and until the autumn every one is needed at Charleston or on the James. The western fleet remains capable of supporting Grant and Porter's eventual capture of Vicksburg. **[OPEN]** Alternate orders, construction dates, technical adaptations and allocations; future turret ships are not excluded indefinitely.

<!-- CR-064 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-064]** Johnston's army leaves Corinth on the morning of 3 April 1862. The next day's march goes as ordered, and Beauregard's plea that surprise is lost is overruled on the evening of the 4th. At dawn on 5 April the Confederates come out of the woods at Shiloh Church. The four Iowa regiments sent to the Sound are missing from the field. The raw division at the church belongs to Brigadier General John McArthur, a Donelson brigade commander given it a fortnight before. It gives way in the morning, and McClernand's thinned division with it. Prentiss and W. H. L. Wallace hold the centre through the afternoon. Johnston bleeds to death behind it at about half past two. By dusk Grant's last line at the landing, with its massed guns and the gunboats, still holds. Lew Wallace's division comes in after seven, and Nelson's leading brigade crosses from the east bank. Buell's next divisions are still on the road. At dawn on the 6th Beauregard finds two fresh divisions in front of him and his own corps tangled in the captured camps. He does not attack. Grant has too few fresh men to retake the camps. That night Beauregard withdraws to Corinth with Grant's guns, colours and stores, as Crittenden's division lands. **[MODEL]** About 11,500 Union and 8,000 Confederate casualties. **[PROPOSED — CR-064]** Halleck comes from St. Louis, takes the field and makes Grant his second in command. In June Grant decides to leave the army. His engineer, James McPherson, talks him out of it, and in July Halleck is called to Washington and Grant has his command back.

<!-- CR-068: the armistice offer of 1862 and the widened credit --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** In the spring of 1862 Elias proposes a general armistice of the three governments on the lines they hold. Washington does not answer him, and a published circular of the State Department says that the United States will hear no proposal from a foreign power. He then widens the Republic's credit at Richmond and publishes its condition, that its war advances end when both governments accept an armistice.

<!-- CR-075: Palmyra (F5.3, I-B8) --> **[APPROVED — CR-075]** In April 1862, with the American squadron gone from its waters, the kingdom takes Palmyra under a commission in the names of the king and the Kuhina Nui. It is the first island the kingdom takes under the sealed minute.

<!-- CR-075: the renewal of 1862 (E10, E-9, I-B9) --> **[APPROVED — CR-075]** In May California's renewal is the one in doubt. Port Townsend has held, the flood has driven the legislature from Sacramento to San Francisco, the Iowa regiments are on the Sound, and the Council has just voted the Rio Grande. Vallejo reads Baker's note of 1861 to the assembly and tells the members that the armistice offer of April is still at sea, unanswered. The published Oregon account and the Steamer Day Agreement carry the rest. The assembly renews narrowly, a few streets from the pulpit where Thomas Starr King preaches against the war, and no one stops him.

Farragut attempts New Orleans in spring 1862 and suffers an initial failure. His wooden fleet remains formidable; its historical breakthrough did not depend on Monitor. Pacifican credit, procurement and a small technical mission help Confederate officers and workers maintain and coordinate defenses. Logan brings testing, machinery and gun-handling lessons learned with Ericsson. <!-- CR-063 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-063]** On 11 April the river in flood breaks the chain-and-hulk barrier below the forts for the second time that spring. In the week before the mortars open, Confederate engineers and Logan's men re-moor the hulks and keep the drift off them, and the barrier is whole when Farragut comes. The night before the passage his gunboats come up to break it and find it guarded. Better placement and usable gun mountings for the unfinished Louisiana, coordinated with a maintained obstruction and defensive fire, form the working mechanism: the passage delays and disorders the Union column, concentrated fire damages leading ships, and insufficient force gets through to exploit a partial penetration. Louisiana does not acquire magically perfected propulsion. Confederate officers authorize and execute the defense; Logan's practical work and accurate reports earn Thorne's attention. Northwestern diversion and Atlantic alarm constrain available support and subsequent reinforcement, without removing ships already with Farragut by assertion. Farragut preserves a dangerous surviving squadron; the Gulf blockade continues. New Orleans remains Confederate. The Union occupies the evacuated Pensacola navy yard in May 1862; Pensacola, Ship Island and Key West sustain the Gulf blockade. Butler's army of 15,000 waits at Ship Island for a squadron that has gone to Key West and the North to repair. Farragut will not take his ships into a bay whose new defences nobody has reconnoitred, a month after the barrier. Richmond's order of February stripping the Gulf coast has already emptied Pensacola, but it is not carried through at Mobile once New Orleans holds: regiments Lovell can spare after the repulse come up the coast, because until the Selma–Meridian line is finished, if Mobile fell there would be no railway joining the Confederacy's west to its east. In July Bragg's army goes through Mobile by rail and steamboat on its way to Chattanooga, and Lincoln, after the Seven Days, orders Butler's men to Fort Monroe. A brigade stays to hold Fort Pickens and Ship Island. Butler goes home with no city to govern and no victory. No Union army comes to the Gulf for two and a half years. In October Renshaw's gunboats take Galveston without a fight. Three companies come from Pensacola to hold it, and on New Year's Day the Confederates retake the town and all three. The Union sends no army to the Texas coast. Its gunboats cruise off the mouth of the Rio Grande, take Pacifican-flagged ships wherever they meet them and seize contraband in neutral ones. Pacifica's credit to Richmond, lent on terms it prints the next year, buys in Europe; Pacifican coin, rifles, machine tools, fuzes, primers, mine gear and men travel around Cape Horn to neutral Matamoros and overland into Texas and across Louisiana to the river. **[OPEN]** Exact obstruction, ship placement, local repairs, command coordination, partial passage, losses, aid authority, allocations and detailed losses. No further Union attempt on New Orleans occurs before 1870.

Sherman refuses an assignment sustaining Confederate armies. After stabilizing the Northwest and transferring command to a deputy, Marcus accepts and leaves late in 1862 with a small staff. <!-- CR-067 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-067]** While Marcus is in the Confederacy an acting chief runs the General Staff at Monterey, and the department commanders in the Columbia, the Great Basin and on the Rio Grande answer to the Protector through him. Marcus meets Logan in a New Orleans repair yard in early 1863. At Vicksburg they disagree over a concentrated productive workshop versus distributed, movable capacity and agree to imperfect contingency preparations. Their mission has limited authority and resources; Confederate commanders retain command. **[OPEN]** Alliance terms, deputy, staff, travel and formal responsibilities.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** Early in 1863 Elias goes to Texas in person to settle the Arizona renunciation and the Matamoros supply terms. Caleb insists that they carry Pruett's watch to his widow together. Abigail opens the door; behind her stands an eleven-year-old with Elias's build. Elias learns he has a son in the week the boy learns who his father is, from the men whose soldiers killed the father who raised him. She will not come to California and takes money only for the boy. She asks for the name, and Elias refuses it. <!-- CR-068: the terms of the credit -->**[APPROVED — CR-068]** At San Antonio in February, where the Sunset articles are signed, he also has the terms of the Richmond credit printed: it is lent for independence and falls due if Richmond treats for reunion.

<!-- CR-068: the Proclamation, the account of the nations, the answer at Honolulu and the terms of passage --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** On 1 January 1863 Lincoln proclaims the slaves of the states in rebellion free, and that month he answers a memorial from San Francisco's coloured citizens in a public letter. In the spring Pacifica's government replies with a published account, in English, Spanish and Hawaiian, that sets its treaties with the Native nations since 1851, its order against slaving raids on the Diné and its declared purpose to end peonage in New Mexico beside the hanging of thirty-eight Dakota at Mankato and the removal of the Dakota and the Winnebago from Minnesota. Lincoln does not answer it. Elias is back at Monterey from Texas late in April, days after Hawaii's legislature, with the Proclamation, the account and the San Antonio articles all before it, has put the renewal of the Protectorship over. In May he goes to Honolulu and tells the legislature that the credit is lent at interest so that the South is not conquered and the Pacific taken next, that the Republic makes no condition about slavery, and that it will lend or stand surety on the customs for compensated emancipation when Richmond asks; the renewal passes in the last week of the term, and the second turret ship, shipped out in sections and assembled on the slip the navy has built at Honolulu, is launched there that year, as promised in 1861. That spring the Republic also prints terms of passage for men leaving the Union's draft, under Lincoln's own sentence from Peoria that no man is good enough to govern another without that other's consent, and Lincoln answers in a public letter.

Grant and Porter bypass the Vicksburg defenses, cross downstream and threaten inland communications. <!-- CR-063 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-063]** Davis weighs the city against the army. With New Orleans and Port Hudson holding, the river below Vicksburg is not lost with Vicksburg, and an army that survives can be rearmed through Matamoros. In May, with Grant ashore below the city, he authorizes its preservation. Pemberton keeps the telegram and fights at Champion Hill, and loses. On the afternoon of 17 May, with the bridge at the Big Black lost and Frederick Steele's XV Corps bridging the river at Bridgeport a few miles from the Benton road, his generals tell him the army can still get out if it goes that night. Marcus works out the order of march with Confederate staff. Forney's and Smith's divisions, which have not fought, march at dusk by the Benton road toward Yazoo City, with Forney in command. Pemberton stays in the works with the divisions beaten at Champion Hill and the bridge, and the heavy guns, because he will not be the Northern-born general who gave up Vicksburg. The trains, loaded in the dark and stopped for hours at a broken bridge, are still on the road in the morning when Steele's leading division comes onto it. It takes them and the reserve guns and drives the rear guard back into the lines. Logan destroys much of his workshop and preserves only portable tools, knowledge and some components. Workers decide independently whether to accompany the retreat. Johnston's letter urging the march arrives at noon.

**[PROPOSED — CR-063]** Davis answers that he authorized the army's preservation, not half of it. Grant assaults the works on 19 and 22 May and is beaten off by half a garrison. Through June he digs toward the city with one half of his army and faces Johnston's, swollen by men who got out without their wagons or their guns, with the other. Johnston will not spend it to save a garrison he advised against leaving. At the turn of June and July, with the approaches at the works and an assault coming, Pemberton surrenders the city, its guns and about fifteen thousand men **[MODEL]**, whom Grant paroles. **[PROPOSED — CR-063]** Much of the mobile Confederate army survives. Grant sends McPherson, who drives Johnston's relief army, stronger by the men who escaped the city, out of Jackson in July and halts where his wagons can go no further. Port Hudson and New Orleans preserve a contested lower-river connection, subject to Union naval disruption. In September Halleck, who wants the river cleaned up, sends a corps down to take Port Hudson from the north. It has landed at Bayou Sara, above the fortress, and is probing the works when Chickamauga is lost, and every man who can be moved goes to Chattanooga. Port Hudson is never besieged. Across the river below it go coin and specialists, rifles and primers, mail and congressmen.

<!-- CR-068: the Terms of Monterey --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** In August 1863, the month after Vicksburg and Gettysburg, Elias publishes the Terms of Monterey. Pacifica asks no state of the Union. Each territory's future is settled by its own inhabitants. The public domain is paid for in gold. The ports stay open to American commerce. Nothing is asked concerning the South. The terms will be neither raised nor lowered by the fortunes of the war. Two reservations are printed with them: the Republic holds itself free to occupy any territory that Congress passes an act to make a state before the peace, and if on 4 March 1865 the Union has a President who will not treat on the heads, it will take the last American station on the Sound. Its recognition of the government at Richmond is no term of this peace and waits on no armistice. A preface addressed to the people of the United States says that the Protector can be dismissed every twelvemonth by either legislature, answers Lincoln's Lyceum address of 1838 and cites Jefferson's letter to Astor on a free and independent empire on the Pacific. In September the Council adopts the Terms by resolution, reservations included. Lincoln replies in a public letter that autumn. He does not dispute Jefferson, and he charges the Protector with keeping the American house divided. In the winter Elias answers over his own name in a letter called *Two Houses*, which argues that the Union would prosper better parted from the South, offers free navigation of the Mississippi as a condition of any further credit from the Republic at Richmond and a guarantee of bounds by the three governments, and asks again for an armistice and commissioners. Lincoln refuses early in 1864: a minority that may leave after losing an election ends government by election. The offer lapses, and neither the river nor the guarantee is in the armistice of 1865.

Thomas's men carry Missionary Ridge, and McPherson marches to relieve Knoxville. In March 1864 Grant goes east as general in chief. He gives the West to George Thomas, whom he has told Halleck is too slow for the campaign he means to fight. Grant has the strategy and no one in the West to carry it out. The raid on Meridian he planned for February is never ordered.

Marcus and Logan rebuild capacity in Confederate-held Louisiana. Marcus returns to Pacifica in late 1863–early 1864; Logan stays to lead the reduced technical mission and gains independent procurement responsibility and direct access to Thorne. His relationships create commercial potential, not a predetermined fortune. Sherman confronts Marcus with depleted reserves and occupation costs; Marcus argues from preparations that prevented greater losses. Both can oppose an unprepared operation while disagreeing about the peace Pacifica should seek.

<!-- CR-012: Confederate characterization and primary-source framework. -->

Jefferson Davis defends an independent government's authority to bind agreements, while Judah Benjamin weighs what that government can still protect when its armies and households require relief. Davis understands that accepting dependence can preserve an administration while extinguishing the sovereignty it exists to defend. Benjamin understands that refusing concessions can preserve a claim while destroying the institutions and people that give it substance. Both serve a state committed to slavery, and their language of protection and consent excludes people whose subjection sustains that state.

Major Julian St. Clair carries an understanding of honor in which a pledge survives the immediate advantage that produced it. Payment alone cannot discharge every political obligation. His claim on others exposes him to the same test: what he will sacrifice for an inconvenient promise or a person entrusted to him. Among Confederate fighters, concern for home, confidence in a commander, attachment to comrades and allegiance to the government pull in different directions. A withdrawal can preserve an army while abandoning its soldiers' own households. These competing loyalties give Elias leverage without making resistance irrational. **[OPEN]** St. Clair's early affiliation, the particular undertaking and his injury chronology remain to be reconciled with the campaigns.

**[PROPOSED — CR-064]** In the East the armies fight from the Peninsula to the Wilderness and on to Petersburg. **[OPEN]** Casualty totals of the western campaign of 1864.

**[PROPOSED — CR-064]** In May 1864 Thomas moves on Johnston with about a hundred thousand men on one railroad. He sends two corps of his own army through Snake Creek Gap toward the railroad behind Dalton, with their wagons, because he does not send men where he cannot feed them. On the afternoon of 10 May he rides out to the gap's mouth. One corps is out and digging in, Hood's divisions are probing it from Resaca, the second corps and the trains are still in the gorge, and he does not know where Johnston's main army is. He orders the line held until everything is up. That night Johnston leaves Dalton behind his cavalry and marches south across the gap's front. Resaca is fought in mid-May and Johnston crosses the Oostanaula. Thomas then flanks only toward the line behind his enemy, never away from his own, and repairs and blockhouses every mile he takes. Johnston gives ground slowly and makes no sorties. In June Davis, afraid for Selma, orders a force back to Alabama; Johnston protests, is overruled, and writes the order into a plan he sends to Davis: he will hold the river lines, and Polk, at the end of the Selma railroad at Blue Mountain, will threaten the Union line from Alabama. In July Bragg finds Johnston on the Allatoona heights following it, and Davis does not replace him. In August Polk marches on Kingston, burns the depot and breaks the line for nine days, and is back on his railroad before Thomas's reserves arrive. From then on Thomas guards Rome, Kingston and the Etowah bridge with a quarter of his army. He reaches the Chattahoochee in September and turns Johnston out of his lines north of the river. In October McPherson cuts the railroad to Augusta at Decatur. Thomas sends his cavalry against the Macon railroad, and it is mended in days. He will not swing his infantry south of Atlanta with Polk four days from his line. Grant, tied to Petersburg, presses him by telegraph, drafts an order giving the West to McPherson and puts it away. Thomas answers that he will move when he is ready. On election day Atlanta is untaken and its trains are running. Two days later Grant sends the order, and McPherson has the West. On 22 November he attacks from Decatur toward the Macon road; Hardee marches round his flank in the night, the attack is stopped, and McPherson is shot from his horse in a wood between two of his own corps. Thomas takes the command back. Forrest has burned the depot at Johnsonville, Polk breaks the railroad again in December, the ships go to Mobile, and no one will spend an army on a city the coming armistice will leave where it stands. The army winters in its lines from Decatur to the river. In Monterey Sherman reads the Eastern papers and will not talk about the campaign.

At Mobile, Eads's river monitors remain trapped above Confederate Port Hudson. The Union's Atlantic yards can build broadside alternatives, but the fleet cannot spare sufficient seagoing armor before the election. Farragut waits at Pensacola; the historical August 1864 attack is delayed. <!-- CR-063 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-063]** Logan's reduced mission repairs the *Tennessee*'s exposed steering chains, her jamming port shutters and her failing primers, and her shells' fuzes, the last two with primers and fuzes that came across the river from Matamoros, while her slow engines remain a real limit. The Gulf has no army for Farragut to land: the corps that might have come down the river cannot pass Port Hudson, and the East has none to spare in the summer of the Overland campaign. **[PROPOSED — CR-064]** Mobile's river and rail connections help sustain Polk in Alabama and, through Montgomery, Johnston at Atlanta.

The paired failures at Mobile and Petersburg close Lincoln's last winter offensive. Victoria is deeply involved in the political consequences. She must defend the shared state before people who question her royal position, its use of foreign soldiers, and the legal status of non-Christian subjects. Elias insists that service and ability should matter more than inherited race or religion; critics object to the forced integration accompanying his expansion. His principle of broad civic incorporation increasingly coexists with ruthless military punishment.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** In 1863 Victoria founds the Kaʻahumanu Society, named for the queen whose name she bears as Kuhina Nui, to nurse the victims of smallpox and care for the sick and elderly. She signs her acts as Kaʻahumanu IV, leads the Kawaiahaʻo choir and composes chants in the old style, among them laments for her nephew, the Prince of Hawaii, who dies in 1862.

The demonstrator is slow and temperamental, and it takes years to make it useful. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** Since his late teens Peirce has suffered a facial neuralgia that brings on his tempers, and success makes him overconfident in elegant procedures.

## 12. Electoral peace, the Western line and the Providentorship (1864–1867)

<!-- CR-039: the Protectorship is renewed for a third time in 1864 -->
The war places the legal machinery of the 1856 Confederation under steadily increasing strain. In 1862, 1863 and 1864 both member legislatures renew Elias's one-year Protectorship as difficult campaigns produce fresh dangers. Renewals that were intended to be exceptional begin to resemble annual recognition of a permanent fact. He remains the commander whose wartime prestige, control of finance and intimate authority over the common institutions make his removal politically hazardous. <!-- CR-075: the keeper of the objects (F6, F-5) --> **[APPROVED — CR-075]** Hawaii's renewals are the ones with conditions. Each holds the Protector to one of the objects of 1861: in 1862 the fourth object's protection under the people's own law, which the Willamette ruling tested; in 1863 the fifth object's loans and why the Republic's gold stands behind them; in 1864 no people by decree. The planters, who gain most from the war, say least about it.

When Alexander dies on 30 November 1863, Victoria, as Kuhina Nui, holds the powers of the crown for a day and proclaims her brother king. Lot succeeds as Kamehameha V. He admires decisive government and seeks stronger royal authority while retaining the Kuhina Nui. Victoria continues to hold the office. The historical Lot abolished it in 1864; here he judges its abolition politically costly and unnecessary, and his 1864 constitution names his sister his heir. Their disagreements concern the kingdom's policy, the scope of the crown and the terms of cooperation with Elias. The monarchy and co-executive remain visible institutions through which Hawaiian interests can be argued.

Wartime decrees keep transport, procurement, credit and military administration moving. Each successful intervention creates commitments whose interruption could endanger firms, jobs, soldiers and public borrowing. Manufacturers and professional officers disagree with individual measures while increasingly favoring continuity of executive government. Critics describe the cumulative danger accurately, but supporters trust legislative review, the next election, temporary mandates or Elias's record of competent action.

In the 1864–1865 consolidation struggle, an Assembly majority threatens to revoke emergency measures and restrict the government's program. <!-- CR-068: Lincoln's offer of 1864, the blind vote and the dissolution --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** The majority's occasion is Lincoln's offer to the people of Pacifica, published in the spring of 1864 through neutral papers: the war claims waived, the commercial articles renewed, the sailor's family compensated and a treaty admitting Hawaiian sugar free of duty, if Pacifica leaves the Columbia and the Sound. Victoria uses it to win her conditions for the third renewal. Elias reminds the merchants that the peace must be ratified by both chambers and both founding states, and the army and fleet contract for Hawaiian sugar at a fixed price for the length of the war. After Lincoln's public refusal of *Two Houses* that February he makes no further statement, and once Washoe is retaken that spring he orders no operation during the Union's campaign.

On 23 August Lincoln writes that he expects to lose, seals the paper and has his cabinet sign it unread. At Chicago the Democrats call the war a failure, demand a cessation of hostilities and nominate McClellan, whose letter of acceptance puts Union first. War Democrats read the letter and peace men the platform. The War Democrats read "Union first" as the South first, and say that the war in the West can be closed on the heads Monterey printed a year ago. Atlanta does not fall. On 2 September Greeley and two other editors write to the governors asking whether Lincoln should withdraw, and the radicals call a convention for the end of the month. It never meets: Morton of Indiana, who faces his own voters in October, will not split the party two weeks before them, and the other governors follow him. Frémont withdraws to keep McClellan out. He stays on the ballot as the candidate his own editors tried to shelve, and Greeley's *Tribune* endorses him without enthusiasm.

Financial strain compounds the military disappointments. Costly access to bullion and overseas credit burdens a Union still able to tax, borrow, issue currency and keep large armies in the field. The Northern republic chooses peace through exhaustion and democratic change while preserving its constitution, army and industrial economy.

**[PROPOSED — CR-065]** Lincoln tells a crowd at the White House that a people's government cannot put off its elections because of a rebellion, even when it loses them. Lincoln keeps the pledge. He offers McClellan everything the executive can give; McClellan agrees and waits for March. In December Lincoln asks the outgoing House to pass the Thirteenth Amendment before the government changes hands. The Democrats who might have voted for it look to the incoming administration, whose platform leaves slavery to a convention of the states, and in January the amendment fails. **[MODEL]** It falls about seven votes short of two-thirds. **[PROPOSED — CR-065]** In December he names Chase Chief Justice, and the Republican Senate confirms him. Kentucky and Delaware remain slave states of the Union. On 3 February Lincoln and Seward meet Stephens, Hunter and Campbell at Hampton Roads. Lincoln's last operations fail, before Atlanta, at Petersburg and at Mobile. He leaves his successor not a ruin but a choice made plain: separation, or more war.

Twelve of the Council's eighteen members vote for him, five of California's nine and seven of Hawaii's, on Hawaii's condition that the Protectorship is not renewed after the war and that what follows it is made by constitutional amendment, ratified by both founding states, with Hawaii's crown, the Kuhina Nui and its local institutions protected. <!-- CR-068: the Protectorship lapses in May 1865; the Chancellor's six months; the bargain of 1865 --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** The renewal of 1864 is the last. On the last day of May 1865 the Protectorship expires with its term. An armistice is not a peace, and Monterey expects the mid-May steamer to carry Elias's request to Honolulu's legislature for a year more until the treaty is signed. He reads the condition he accepted in 1864 against himself, and the steamer sails without it. He governs for six months as Chancellor, under the Articles of 1856. They were written for two states with a few common affairs. The country the columns hold was held under the Protector's emergency arrangements, and with the emergency over its commanders and commissioners ask under what law they sit. The wartime decrees for procurement and credit are spent, and a friendly Assembly votes the contracts again one at a time while works stop and garrisons are paid late. Through the summer Washington's commissioners, Deseret, Oregon's compact, the bankers and the nations under compact ask the same question: who is bound now, and who will be bound in five years? In July Elias lays the heads of an Enabling Law before the Council himself. The case for it is that the country has outgrown its Articles and that promises made for a generation need a guarantor who cannot be turned out the next spring; the case for a life office is the Union's own election of 1864 and the date a term would give its enemies.

**[APPROVED — CR-068]** The bargain is made that summer at Monterey, where the Council sits from June to August and Victoria leads Hawaii's delegation. The form of the law and the protection of Hawaiian kingship, the Kuhina Nui and local institutions were promised by the Council's resolution of November 1864. Hawaii now bargains for the agreed revenues and for the list of protected provisions that cabinet legislation may not touch, and wins both. On the term Hawaii is divided. Lot supports an office for life, and his letter is read to the committee: Hawaii's half of the Council will end when the new states come in, each with a delegation equal to its own, and he wants a guarantor those states cannot turn out. Victoria sides with Sherman, who tells the committee that an office for life has its date too and that the law does not say who is bound after it; he is not answered. The King's letter decides Hawaii's government; Victoria votes with it, the two members of Hawaii's delegation from the American mission families vote against, and the Council passes the law thirteen to five. The Assembly passes it in September and October and California ratifies. Victoria carries Hawaii's ratification in November and helps secure the settlement while retaining reservations about its reach: she has watched him lay the old office down, and does not know whether she saw a man keeping his word or winning his case. The law is in force on 1 December, and the treaty with Washington is signed under it before the month is out.

In 1865 Elias secures the Enabling Law through constitutional amendment: two-thirds of both confederal chambers and ratification by both founding states. The cabinet may legislate around specified provisions, but the Hawaiian crown, Kuhina Nui and local institutions are protected. The ordinary chambers and member governments remain. Elias becomes Providentor for life and presides over the cabinet; the chancellorship lapses. This is a legal concentration of power obtained through votes and bargaining, not a decree amending its own limits.

Constitutional critics warn that institutional survival may offer little protection against an executive legislating around ordinary controls. Supporters believe that reconstruction, occupation, debt and industrial commitments require a government able to act continuously. The guarantees become protected provisions of the Enabling Law, whose later breach cannot be justified by the authority it grants.

Elias controls ministerial appointment and dismissal, sets the cabinet's legislative agenda and appoints senior administrators. Cabinet legislative power consequently concentrates in his person, although ministers retain expertise, constituencies and opportunities to resist. Courts continue to enforce ordinary contracts and property claims; the same productive society that attracts newcomers accommodates growing restrictions in matters the government defines as security or reconstruction. Dependence on grants, appointments and contracts makes the concentration harder to reverse. From 1865 he deals directly with county boards over state legislatures’ heads through water, railway and land grants, legally hollowing out the states.

<!-- CR-071: the grant machinery --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The grants come with terms. A county board petitions the Superintendency for a water grant, a railway subsidy or Development Fund credit; it signs away the works' accounts to confederal audit, their route to confederal consent, any member tax on them and its own forum to the Confederal Court, and it gets money no member could give it. The accounts are published each year beside the next county's. By 1871 most interior boards spend more confederal money than member money, and their supervisors look to Monterey. Nothing is taken from the members; everything new goes round them.

<!-- CR-072: the Prosperity Party, 1866 --> **[APPROVED — CR-072]** The boards that take the grants also take the ticket. In 1866, for the first elections after the Enabling Law, Elias founds the Prosperity Party as his personal instrument. It answers to him, not to a convention, and has no platform beyond his. Its line, at the head of the ticket, is *Things work*; in Hawaii, and among its Japanese and Chinese members, it is the Hui. Its first organisers are a machine man and an engineer. David Broderick, the Tammany fireman who built San Francisco's Democratic machine, owes Elias its survival, because the Committee of Vigilance of 1856 was aimed at it and Elias broke the committee. With no seat in Washington at stake, his quarrel with David Terry ends short of the field. Theodore Judah, the railway engineer of the Sacramento valley, stays on the coast through the war years, and his quarrel with Huntington and Crocker goes to Elias. Broderick brings wards, captains, turnout and the printed ticket, which the Party prints itself; Judah brings the towns, boards and subscribers along his line, and the Party's county committees follow his stations. **[PROPOSED — CR-072]** Broderick has to unlearn the sale of offices, because Elias's offices are examined. The Party is paid for from above, a cost and not a business, from dues and the founder's own fortune, never from the treasury, and Broderick never quite believes it can work. Its core is his machine, Judah's towns, the county boards that have taken confederal grants since 1865 and the men the war made: quartermasters, telegraphers, Californio officers, the Mormon road men of the Compact. It contests every member legislature and wins most of them. **[APPROVED — CR-072]** Deseret's, held by the Church's own People's Party, it never wins. In San Francisco its rival is the vigilantes' old party, the Committee party, the party of the city's money, weakened but not destroyed.

<!-- CR-076: the Party's dry lodges --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** The Party's lodges meet dry. Broderick, who closed his New York saloon and emptied its casks into the street before he came west, keeps them that way: votes bought with drink are votes the Party cannot count.

The Providentorship is Elias's alone; it ends with the regency on 1 August 1887, weeks before his death, and neither a later regent nor a county inherits his power. The constitutional machinery remains visible even as executive patronage drains the member states' practical independence.

Marcus seeks defensible gains and can accept lawfully established durable executive authority. Sherman presses for fewer commitments and insists that the army serves lawful government rather than a separate personal oath to Elias. Neither officer has an individual constitutional veto. Their disagreement informs consolidation without giving either a personal right to prevent a constitutionally adopted settlement.

**[PROPOSED — CR-062]** The Western treaty is signed at Washington in the third week of December 1865, under the full powers of the office the Enabling Law created on the first of the month. McClellan's Secretary of State signs for the Union. **[OPEN]** His name. **[PROPOSED — CR-062]** For Pacifica, Mariano Vallejo signs the instrument that ends the convention he signed in the same city in 1848, beside William Sherman, whom Washington trusts and whose brother will vote on it. The Union is to evacuate Port Townsend and Fort Union after the exchange, and Pacifica is to pay a vendor credit of $40 million in gold in six instalments from the exchange. Its merchants keep the Columbia and Puget Sound on published terms; its warships may come in for refuge or repair, and no storehouse is granted anywhere. Named roads stay open from Fort Benton to Helena, from Denver to Salt Lake and from Denver over Raton Pass to Santa Fe. Both governments waive their claims from the war, so Farragut's death is charged to no one in the treaty, and a mixed commission hears private claims. At Victoria's insistence, the family of the Hawaiian sailor killed in 1861 keeps its own claim before the commission. Every resident of a ceded district may choose either citizenship and keeps property, courts and civil protection without distinction of colour. The treaty says nothing about fugitives. Its line runs to the boundary of Texas without naming who governs there, and nothing in it touches the Union's claim to the South. The treaty cedes territories, not states. McClellan puts the signed text in his safe.

The Western line is agreed in the 1865 peace and ratified by the Union Senate in 1867, alongside Pacifica's Alaska purchase. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** The peace declares the conventions of 1848 and 1860 ended by the war. The Western line replaces the renunciation of the Basin and the recognition of Washoe; American owners in Nevada keep the property guarantees Carson gave them. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** A Pacific article goes with it. The Union renounces its claims under the Guano Islands Act of 1856 to a schedule of named islands: Midway, Baker, Howland, Jarvis, Johnston and Kingman, with a quitclaim to Palmyra, which Hawaii annexed in 1862. The New York guano companies keep their workings until the deposits are exhausted and land their cargoes duty-free at Hawaiian ports, where their labourers were recruited. A nominal sum disappears into the vendor credit. Senators who are selling half the West can give up a few guano rocks under their own statute, which never obliged the United States to keep an island once its guano was gone. Pacifica keeps the Northwest, Great Basin, Arizona, Utah, Nevada and Idaho; western Montana includes the Missouri-headwaters gold camps, while Fort Benton remains Union. Southwest Wyoming includes South Pass, Fort Bridger, the Green River basin and Rock Springs coal. New Mexico runs east to 103°W. The detailed boundary follows the mountain front, divide crests and Sangre de Cristo exceptions recorded in the territorial plan; surveys remain **[PROPOSED]**. Federal cession does not settle Native title or consent.

**[APPROVED — CR-061]** The captive article brings the compact's unwilling clause forward. **[APPROVED — CR-015]** Pacifica abolishes New Mexico's debt peonage and Native captive trade gradually with compensation by 1866, facing a short Hispano revolt. **[APPROVED — CR-061]** In December 1865 the compact's assembly votes the abolition ordinance, in the House by one vote, the speaker's, cast by José Manuel Gallegos, the priest Bishop Lamy suspended, for the abolition Lamy has preached since the captive article; the cabinet promulgates it as voted in January 1866. Nobody is hanged in 1866: the men who killed go to prison, the rest are amnestied when they give up their arms, and every patrón who rose loses the compensation for the peons and captives he must free anyway. It writes no exclusion clause; its first legislature had asked Congress to let George Bush, a man of colour, keep his farm at Bush Prairie. It names the new member Columbia, the name its settlers asked for at Monticello in 1852 and Congress gave away.
