California's legislature extends its law of 1852 and Hawaii's under Lot its law of 1860, and each member that accedes in 1866–67 takes the same terms in its charter: the ladder at home, with opium on a ladder of its own; a printed floor below which no measure of pure spirit may be sold, with no discount for quantity; a label on every bottle with the Assay Office's strength, the floor and the life offices' figures setting deaths among insured abstainers beside deaths among other insured men; the evening hour; no credit for drink; and a county option. San Francisco's German brewers and the wine counties fight the ladder in Sacramento and win only a softer bottom band. Deseret brings the city liquor store Salt Lake has run since 1861, and the confederal chambers' committee on the charters finds the store buying from Young's own distillery at four dollars a gallon when better spirits sell at a dollar and a quarter. Under the new law a public store cannot open if an official supplies it. Brigham Young, who hates drunkenness and has made whiskey for the city all the same, keeps the store and gives up the distillery. From 1867 any county board may take the retail of spirits into a public store, the Spirit Store, on the pattern Gothenburg adopted in 1865: salaried managers, a fixed return on the capital, the surplus to the county's surgery, and no official among its suppliers. The Calculation Office has the Union's figures: at two dollars a proof gallon the Union collects its whiskey tax on a fraction of what its distillers make. The members set their duty where their gaugers can hold it, every still is licensed and sealed by the Assay Office, and the gaugers are rotated and paid by the Republic, never by the distiller.

Pacifica's envoy offers $7.2 million in Pacifica's own gold, paid at handover rather than when a legislature agrees. Gorchakov asks whether selling to Pacifica would strengthen Britain. The envoy answers with the Republic's history: Britain refused it protection in 1846, disclaimed any part of it in 1848, and Elias refused even an American guarantee, because a guarantor has a right to come in. On 16 December 1866, by the Russian calendar, the Tsar's special meeting decides to sell to Pacifica. The treaty is signed at St Petersburg early in March 1867, and the cable has the news in Washington within the day. The treaty transfers Russia's claim and leaves the lands and rights of the Aleut, the Alutiiq, the Tlingit and the other nations to their own arrangements; Victoria will not have it otherwise.

**[PROPOSED — CR-062]** In April, during the Senate's special session, McClellan sends the Western treaty with Oregon's certified returns, the opposition's published case, the charters of Washington Territory, Idaho, Washoe, Deseret, western Montana and New Mexico, the Navy's estimate of what it would cost to take back the Columbia, and the Treasury's statement of what Pacifica will pay for the West. The cable from St Petersburg has been in Washington since March, and no senator now speaks of buying Russian America instead. Before the vote the first instalment lies counted in gold in the New York Subtreasury, to be released on the exchange. Grimes reads the Navy's estimate and votes for the cheaper peace. Fessenden and John Sherman check the gold. Harlan and Ramsey get their roads, and Morgan brings New York. Sumner reads Oregon's returns and the article on colour, denounces Oregon's exclusion clause and speaks for the treaty: it cannot reach a citizen of any member state, Oregon's Black residents may choose their citizenship, and the Union's own Enabling Act, which demanded the clause's removal, stood for five years and never got it removed; Reverdy Johnson brings the Democrats who want peace. Wade and Chandler call it payment to the invader in free soil and a treaty negotiated by one Sherman and ratified by the other. Republicans who will never give up the South vote to give up the West: it is already lost, the gold goes toward resumption, the claim to the South is untouched, and rejecting the treaty would leave the armistice and the army in a Democratic president's hands. The money is lent privately instead, through Ralston's bank as the Republic's agent, which takes a share of its own, to New York and Philadelphia houses and the transit companies. **[OPEN]** The count. **[PROPOSED — CR-062]** The ratifications are exchanged at Washington that summer, and the first payment is released. The surveyors take the field.

Pacifica pays a $40 million gold vendor credit in instalments through 1872 and offers a separate Treasury loan. Eastern financiers become participants in the settlement; Elias, once dispossessed by creditors, becomes creditor to the Union's bankers. <!-- CR-069: the steamers return -->**[PROPOSED — CR-069]** The Steamer Day Agreement ends with the treaty of December 1865, the treasure steamers sail for New York again in 1866, and the gold the coast held back during the war goes east in 1866 and 1867. The price, payment schedule and loan capacity require financial testing. Senate ratification of the paid Western cession is separated from executive recognition of Richmond, which the Senate never ratifies. The Plains remain a buffer, and the Union's grievance points chiefly toward the South. War debt and demobilization limit immediate capacity; Democratic government and financiers profiting from transit and loans reduce the will for a second Western war, while the unresolved South, Plains conflicts and British quarrels compete for attention. Restraint rests on incentives and geography, not an incapable or permanently pacified Union.

The Santa Fe Trail stays open to the Union's freighters under published duties. Raton Pass and Fort Union become Pacifica's eastern bastion, and the Union has no land border with Mexico.

<!-- CR-068: Lincoln after the presidency --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** Lincoln goes home to Springfield and his old partnership with Herndon. From 1865 the Republic's school readers print Elias's *Two Houses*, and its colleges set Lincoln's Lyceum address and Peoria speech in their history courses. In 1869 he comes west by sea at Elias's invitation, paying his own passage, and the two men meet once; no record of what was said survives.

Pacifica negotiates infrastructure, trade and financial relationships while administering its agreed gains. The Northern and Confederate governments retain sovereignty and substantial institutions; association deepens through contested commercial, defense and customs agreements. No Confederate state is a Pacifican member by 1895. Membership becomes constitutionally possible after 1887, with admission dependent on published social measures rather than an immediate transfer of millions of voters to the founding electorate. Elias's victories depend on the courage and skill of others, foreign credit and diplomacy, superior preparation in particular theatres and willingness to pay terrible costs; the resulting state is never an uncontested inheritance.

Colonel Armistead's question is what continuity of service preserves for the men and households to whom he remains responsible. Defeat does not end those obligations, but employment by the victorious power can change their meaning. The profession that offers survival also demands obedience in new campaigns against people defending homes of their own. **[OPEN]** His original enlistment motive, transfer into the Atlantic Auxiliary, the terms offered to his men and his later itinerary remain to be designed; no particular bargain or act of refusal is settled.

# PART III — Ocean partners, continental dependence and industrial limits (1865–1887)

This part follows the construction of the Pacific network and interleaves with the Hawaiian and family story in Part IV. Its major commitments are separated by consolidation. Pacifica seeks local naval superiority, paying and supplying partners who fight on land. Acquisitions must earn their cost within about ten years; there is one operation at a time, no populated mainland annexation outside North America, no Pacifican land war against Russia, and no war with Britain before 1900.

## 13. Winning the peace and binding the continental partner (1865–1875)

The prewar technical community turns wartime experience into expanding capacity. Donahue's workshops and Huntington's procurement, Ralston's credit and Crocker's construction teams, and William Kelly's experiments, Holley's plant engineering and Hallidie's material handling contribute different parts of a cumulative industrial system. Outside processes, licensing, fuel, workers and capital still determine what can be built. Logan's Louisiana repair relationships offer an engineering base and commercial opportunity; no private fortune or monopoly follows automatically.

**[PROPOSED]** Demobilization brings land offers and paid contracts for engineers, naval officers and shipwrights from both American armies. Sierra railway crews remain employed on interior links. <!-- CR-071: the Baja purchase --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** As France withdraws, a Baja purchase in 1867, paid over five years, secures the southern flank. **[PROPOSED]** A Guaymas railway concession and free port go with it. Mexico retains Sonora. Prices, garrisons and firms await audit.

<!-- CR-071: the Baja commission --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** Baja is governed from La Paz by a commission chartered by the confederal chambers, three commissioners with Spanish as a working language, until 1887. In the 1870s it makes compacts with the Kumeyaay, Paipai, Kiliwa and Cucapá for homelands in their sierras and the delta. **[OPEN]** The commissioners' names.

<!-- CR-071: the settlement programme --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The interior is filled while the members are emptied. John Wesley Powell heads the land and water office, and his irrigation districts are drawn by watershed. The passage offices bring settlers from Europe and Asia to farms sold by the Land Fund on instalments. The trunk lines are chartered by the confederal chambers on Land Fund grants: the Raton line reaches Las Vegas in 1879, the thirty-fifth-parallel line crosses to the Colorado in 1880–83, and the Salt Lake–Snake–Columbia line is built in 1881–84. The settlers owe their farm to the Land Fund, their water to the district and their passage to a confederal office, not to the member's legislature. **[PROPOSED — CR-071]** Hans Mattson runs the passage offices. **[OPEN]** The programme's scale, and when Powell enters the Republic's service.

<!-- CR-071: the first quarrel, and the leases --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The first quarrel is Oregon's. The Chinese floor of its charter has brought Chinese Pacificans into the Rogue valley, and in 1869 the Breckinridge men of Jackson and Josephine carry a Land Act through the legislature. It bars Land Fund sales to anyone the old constitution would have barred and lays a member tax on assisted settlers' instalments. In 1870 the Confederal Court strikes it down: the Land Fund's title is confederal, the floor protects a citizen of another member, and no member tax lies on a confederal grant. Oregon keeps its power over residence until 1887.

**[APPROVED — CR-071]** Where a line or a mine crosses a homeland, the nation leases and does not sell. In 1873 Ouray negotiates the San Juan lease, which gives the miners their districts and roads and keeps the valleys Ute. The leased districts of the San Juan and Gunnison join the Mountain Districts' commission, which moves to the new silver town of Leadville in 1879. Globe and Clifton are worked on lease inside the Apache country, and Bisbee's copper pays the Chiricahua a royalty. In 1881, when the Snake line reaches the reservation, Fort Hall's council refuses to sell the Portneuf right of way and leases it for a rent the railway pays, as every later crossing of a homeland does.

<!-- CR-058: Juárez, Querétaro and the Sonora credit -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** As France withdraws, Pacifica recognises Juárez's government and lends to it on the Sonora customs, and rifles for the Republic's army go south through Paso del Norte from New Mexico. <!-- CR-061 -->**[APPROVED — CR-061]** From the autumn of 1865 until June 1866 Juárez's government sits at Paso del Norte itself, and the crossing outlives his stay. The rifles go down the Rio Grande's west bank from Mesilla to the boundary at the Pass, where a customs post agreed with Mexico passes them overland into Chihuahua, and Confederate El Paso, across the river, has no say in them. Maximilian is shot at Querétaro on 19 June 1867, in the city where Elias and Alvarado signed the convention of 1847, and Mexico's liberals learn that Pacifica, not Washington, ended the French adventure. Monterey restrains its Confederate partner, whose exiles had courted Maximilian and settled in his empire. Juárez's words of 15 July 1867, that among individuals, as among nations, respect for the rights of others is peace, become the common language of the continent's neighbours.

The Confederate economy needs credit, markets and protection. A railway treaty in 1866–1867 gives Huntington's line a route across Texas to New Orleans. Johnston succeeds Davis in February 1868 after his 1867 election. Richmond's Mississippi tolls and fugitive raids into Union Tennessee give Grant a popular cause; elected in 1868, he attacks in July 1870 while Europe is occupied by the Franco-Prussian War. Pacifica sends a Gulf squadron, turret ships, mines under Logan, rifles and credit, and closes its Pacific ports to Union shipping. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Hawaiian schooners raise the Confederation's flag over the remaining American guano bonds in the Phoenix group, at Kiritimati and on Swains, without firing a shot. It sends no field army and undertakes no Asian military commitment during this war.

Union attacks fail at New Orleans in autumn 1870 and stall against entrenched lines in Virginia. Among the Texan volunteers at New Orleans is Silas Pruett, nineteen, who sees Pacifican mines and credit at work. After the association treaties he takes a commission in the Atlantic Auxiliary. In spring 1871 Sherman negotiates with Grant, whose friendship now crosses national allegiances. Richmond restores free Mississippi navigation; Pacifica guarantees Confederate independence. <!-- CR-068: the river -->**[APPROVED — CR-068]** Free navigation of the Mississippi is Pacifica's condition for that guarantee. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The Union quitclaims the Pacific islands Pacifica occupied during the war. The Union Senate keeps its claim to the South. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** Association treaties in 1871–1872 refinance Confederate debt on customs security, install Pacifican customs co-administration and secure the lower Mobile Bay naval station, Galveston commercial privileges, Atlantic Auxiliary recruitment and Birmingham concessions. Pacifica replaces the surrendered toll revenue. Pensacola remains a nearby Union base; protection and exposure coexist.

<!-- CR-058: the neighbours in Grant's war -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Grant's war is part of a plan. Having lost the West, the Union means to make up for it in the Caribbean, on the Mississippi and in the north, and each neighbour meets a different part of the plan. Canada meets it in Sumner's speech of 13 April 1869, which puts the Alabama damages at about $2.1 billion, swollen by the raiders that helped the South win, a sum read on both sides of the Atlantic as payable only in Canada; in the Fenian raids of May 1870, which Washington tolerates; and in the refusal that month to let the steamer *Chicora* through the Sault canal with troops and supplies for the Red River expedition, which shows Ottawa that the Union can cut the Dominion in two at the Soo. St Albans and the Confederate agents of Montreal are an account the Union means to settle, and Britain is taking its garrisons home. Montreal also earns from Richmond's tolls, which drive Midwest grain onto the St Lawrence canals. Mexico meets the plan in Grant's treaty to annex Santo Domingo, which the Senate rejects on 30 June 1870, and Juárez would rather have a weak Confederacy on the Rio Grande than a reunited giant. Pacifica coordinates nothing; it shows each capital the whole map. When Grant attacks, the Canadian militia still under arms after the May raids stays on the border and ties down Union troops, and Mexico closes its Gulf ports to Union coaling.

<!-- CR-058: the spring of two treaties; British Columbia and the Canadian Pacific -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** In the spring of 1871 two treaties are made. At Washington on 8 May Britain trades Canada's fisheries away for peace with the Union. In the same weeks Pacifica grants Canada, for nothing but the asking, the navigation of the Stikine, the Yukon and the Porcupine through Alaska, free forever. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** The grant is a convention signed at Monterey with Britain, with a British Columbian delegate in the British party. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** British Columbia enters Confederation on 20 July. Pacifica has encouraged it, because a strong Canadian buffer is worth more to it than a province that would provoke Britain, and since 1866 it has let London know that a Union move on Rupert's Land or the coast would concern the Pacific. Its money goes quietly, through London houses and never into the charter, into the finance of the Canadian Pacific, whose western traffic and, from 1887, trans-Pacific steamers feed Pacifica's own shipping. The scandal of 1873 over the foreign money behind Allan's syndicate shows what visible money would cost. The river grant matters at once: the Cassiar rush of 1873–1874 goes up the Stikine through Wrangell.

The Union turns to an anti-slavery cold war. Greeley defeats Grant in 1872, then dies before the electors meet. After a disputed count, Brown takes office in 1873 on condition that he sign the Freedom Act: refugees are free on arrival and citizens after a year; discriminatory duties and European campaigning penalize slave-grown cotton. The Panic of 1873 weakens cotton and slave prices while Pacifica recruits laid-off engineers and workers. A disputed Hayes election and the railway strike of 1877 deepen the Union's crisis without ending its anti-slavery policy.

<!-- CR-068: Lincoln's death -->
**[APPROVED — CR-068]** Lincoln dies at Springfield in November 1872. Pacifica pays him no official honour.

Gordon succeeds Johnston in 1874 and works with Pacifican railways, coal and credit. Benjamin helps carry a free-womb amendment in 1874–1875 as refinancing pressure mounts. **[PROPOSED]** Benjamin first serves as Louisiana senator from 1868, then Gordon's Secretary of State from 1874. A convention establishes freedom for children born after 1 January 1875, while allowing masters their labor to twenty-one or state compensation. The constitutional route requires state consent; its economic effect and finance await audit. Black Union regulars and freedmen's homesteads along Tennessee sustain escape networks. Flight, free-labor markets and campaigning make the Confederacy poorer and more dependent on Pacifican credit.

<!-- CR-061 -->Pacifica enforces a fugitive agreement with Richmond from 1863 until abolition in 1888–1889, preventing enslaved people from escaping to Pacifican territory; it closes the border and returns no one who has reached Pacifican soil. <!-- CR-068: the controls of 1871–72 --> **[APPROVED — CR-068]** The association treaties of 1871–1872 extend the travel-paper controls that have closed the Texas line since 1863 to the railway and the Gulf ports. Richmond asks for the return of those who get through and is refused, as Texas has been every year since 1863. Victoria supports the controls. **[PROPOSED]** Pacifica's own slavery prohibition remains, but its border assists a slaveholding partner. Abolitionists and Chinese and Hawaiian citizens recognize the contradiction.

<!-- CR-076: San Francisco's den ordinance, 1875 --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** In November 1875 San Francisco's supervisors pass an ordinance closing the Chinese opium dens. In 1876 California's supreme court strikes it: a room licensed under the state's law cannot be closed by a county for the people who keep it.

## 14. Alaska, Japan, China and the ocean road (1867–1880)

Alaska is purchased in 1867 for the Aleutian route to Japan and to exclude a rival northern foothold. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Midway passes to Pacifica in the 1867 ratification and becomes part of the Hawaiian Crown lands, with its naval ground leased to the Confederation on the terms later used at Puʻuloa. **[PROPOSED]** A subsidized San Francisco–Honolulu–Yokohama–Hong Kong mail line, coaling also at Hakodate, preserves Pacific traffic after Suez opens.

<!-- CR-062 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-062]** The flag changes at Sitka in October 1867. The Russian subjects may leave or stay, the Orthodox Church keeps its property, and the Creoles of Sitka and Kodiak become Pacifican citizens. The Tlingit at Sitka say that Russia has sold what it never owned, and Pacifica's commissioners, bound by the treaty's article, have to begin by agreeing with them.

<!-- CR-070: the Northern Commission, the finder's compacts and the north's rents -->**[APPROVED — CR-070]** Alaska is a territory of the Confederation from the day the flag changes, and the confederal chambers charter a Northern Commission under Pablo de la Guerra to govern it; George Davidson, the coast surveyor, succeeds him in 1873. Elias could have made the charter by cabinet ordinance; he brings it to the chambers as a bill, because the compacts need a ratifier that will outlive him, and the chambers vote the north a station and no fortress; in 1876, when his cabinet rules Hawaii by ordinance, Victoria remembers the bill. They extend the Mining Law of 1849 north, with an assay office at Sitka, claims courts and a land office; but the land office grants nothing and the claims courts register nothing on a nation's country without that nation's compact, and the first is opened with the Tlingit at Sitka in the autumn of 1867 and concluded in 1869: their village and its fisheries stay theirs, the Commission holds the Russian town's ground by a yearly rent, clan property is recognised in its courts, and disputes go to a joint hearing. The Commission's survey begins from the Russian American Company's papers, handed over with the colony, and Sitka and Kodiak Creoles, a Kashevarov among them, sit at its tables. A mine on a nation's land holds it by the nation's lease; the Confederation takes its five per cent; and the nation that showed the ore takes a finder's share agreed on top of both and paid by the mine. In 1872 the Auk leader Kowee brings ore from Gastineau Channel to the assay office at Sitka and will not show its source until the Commission has agreed his people's share. When the prospectors he guides, Richard Harris and Joe Juneau, come back with too little, he tells the assayer to send them out again; the gold belt is worked from 1872, and the town that grows on it takes Juneau's name, not his. From 1876 Daniel Libby, who found gold on the Niukluk with the telegraph survey ten years before and kept it to himself, leads the Commission's parties on the Seward Peninsula, supplied by the whalers who call at Norton Sound every summer. The same whalers' walrus hunt starves St Lawrence Island in 1878–1880; from 1880 the Commission's contract with them carries a walrus limit, and the reindeer it brings from Chukotka come too late for the island. The north still pays its first years in rent rather than gold. The seal islands are leased at a price per skin and put out to tender again every ten years from 1880, and every lease carries the terms of the Unangan of St Paul and St George, who are paid by the skin and hold their islands by their own compact. By the Kodiak compact of 1868 only the nations' hunters take the sea otter, under a yearly quota, and the otter is not hunted out. The Columbia's canners come north a decade early, and from 1870 the chambers tax every case of salmon packed, under a conservation law, with the nations' fisheries reserved by compact.

<!-- CR-070: the settler garrison -->**[APPROVED — CR-070]** Sitka is a station, not a fortress. Its first test comes in 1869, when a sentry kills a Kake man in the town, and the commander pays the dead man's clan under Tlingit law, over his officers' protest; the rule becomes a term of the Sitka compact, concluded that year, and the town stays quiet. From 1873 its garrison falls to two-fifths of its first strength: the soldiers serve five years and stay as settlers, with land where the compacts allow it, a fishing licence and a claim right, as Kuroda's settler soldiers do in Hokkaido in the same years. The squadron, the revenue cutters and the mail carry the flag. Unalaska coals the squadron and the northern steamers with Takashima coal. Hokkaido and Alaska become one northern school, and quarrel: Sapporo's agronomists and Sitka's assayers trade their methods for cold and fish, and the assayers say that Sapporo settles its soldiers on Ainu land without asking.

<!-- CR-070: the northern wire -->**[APPROVED — CR-070]** Pacifica does not finish Western Union's abandoned telegraph to Siberia; its northern line goes only as far as Alaska. The Confederation buys the company's line in British Columbia through a company of the colony, and its engineers build north from Fort Stager to the Stikine and down the river to Wrangell, and a cable through the straits puts Sitka on the wire in 1870. The wire follows the gold to Juneau and, with the road, to Dyea. For eighteen years Alaska's wire crosses British, then Canadian, soil; Pacifica will not spend on a sea cable while the harbours and the capital are building, until Port Hamilton shows what that saving risks: for a month in 1885 the Dominion's offices refuse Pacifica's cipher, and Sitka is reached only by steamer.

<!-- CR-071: Dall --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** In 1885 William Healey Dall, the naturalist of the telegraph survey, succeeds Davidson at the head of the Northern Commission at forty.

**[PROPOSED]** Pacifica's fleet guarantees the negotiated transition at Edo in 1868 through Katsu Kaishū and Saigō Takamori. The Kōtetsu reaches the Meiji government through Pacifica, and the Hakodate regime falls in 1869. Kuroda Kiyotaka's Hokkaido development brings Pacifican advisers north; in 1886 Kiyoshi enters his house by adoption and marriage, and later founds his own branch of it. France's defeat creates an opening for a Pacifican army mission, followed by naval training and Japanese construction to Pacifican designs.

The 1870–1871 Japanese alliance is equal: full Japanese tariff autonomy and no consular courts, dockyard rights at Yokosuka, a coaling lease at Hakodate, Takashima coal and foreign credit. No sovereign Pacifican base is imposed on Japan. Training and standards make cooperation valuable and eventual independence from the system expensive.

<!-- CR-071: the abolition of the domains, watched --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** Kido Takayoshi and Itō Hirobumi have read the Articles of 1856 through Hamada Hikozō at Nagasaki and concluded that Japan cannot afford a confederation whose members keep their own militia and a veto. Part of the alliance's credit lets Tokyo promise to take the domains' debts and paper money. Elias has the accounts, and he watches the abolition of the domains in August 1871 as an underwriter, not a spectator: the registers returned first, the guard assembled before the announcement, the lords brought to the capital and given rank for office, the new prefectures named for rivers and mountains.

The Iwakura Mission's San Francisco landfall in 1872 puts Japan's officials inside this industrial world.

<!-- CR-072: Kido asks how it is done --> **[APPROVED — CR-072]** Kido, who governs by rescript, watches a man who governs by ticket, and asks Elias how it is done.

<!-- CR-073: Ralston's bets pay, 1866–1875 --> **[PROPOSED — CR-073]** Ralston builds for the city he expects. His woollen mills, carriage works, watch factory, sugar refinery and silk mill sell behind the Republic's customs line, and Eastern goods pay duty there. **[APPROVED — CR-073]** From 1868 his dry dock at Hunter's Point takes the largest ships on the Pacific. In January 1872 he receives the Iwakura embassy at Belmont, and his bank contracts to refine Japan's old coinage for the new yen. That spring the State Assay Office examines the stones from the Colorado diamond field that two prospectors have left at his bank, and finds them bought in London. On his advice the Republic's mint strikes a trade dollar for Asia, which gives the Comstock's silver an outlet as its price falls. In 1873 the Irishmen of the Consolidated Virginia find the Big Bonanza in ground the Bank Ring had let go. **[PROPOSED — CR-073]** Ralston does not fight them on the stock board, because the Reserve Bank will not lend against mining shares. **[APPROVED — CR-073]** That autumn the squadron damaged off Manila comes home from Honolulu and is docked at Hunter's Point. <!-- CR-071: Japan's loan of 1873 --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** In 1873 part of Japan's loan for the samurai stipends is placed in San Francisco, with the Bank of California as the Republic's agent. **[APPROVED — CR-073]** In 1874 his bank is the Republic's agent for the Spanish government's loan on the Philippine customs. That year William Sharon, his partner, corners Ophir to pay for the votes of Washoe's legislature, which chooses Washoe's Council delegation, and asks Ralston to come in, and Ralston refuses. The Prosperity Party carries Washoe that November, and when the market breaks in January 1875 Sharon has neither the seat nor the credit.

**[APPROVED — CR-073]** In 1875 the Bonanza men open the Nevada Bank, and in August the city's coin runs short. The Reserve Bank discounts the Bank of California's paper at a penalty rate, and its doors stay open. In August 1875 the Republic buys the Spring Valley Water Company at the published value of its works and takes the Calaveras watershed with it; Andrew Hallidie sits on the valuation as the engineers' man. Ralston is paid less than he asked and enough to pay for his shares. **[PROPOSED — CR-073]** The Palace opens in October with every room let. **[APPROVED — CR-073]** That winter the San Francisco houses begin to subscribe to the Prosperity Party, and Ralston brings them to Broderick.

<!-- CR-031-R1: reciprocal Japanese–Pacifican innovation and nineteenth-century construction productivity. -->
Over the following decades Japanese and Pacifican engineers learn from one another and compete to improve the work. Dockyards, railways and factories bring their methods into regular comparison: how materials arrive, how parts fit, how crews are trained and how long a completed job takes. Japanese contractors hire and train their own paid crews, organise repeated tasks in planned sequences and coordinate machinery with deliveries and skilled work. By the later nineteenth century Japan has a construction industry whose speed and efficient use of labour become an example to its partner. Contractors win by completing more work with the same resources, retaining skills and cutting delays; handing recruitment and control to labour syndicates and local gang bosses would surrender both their margins and their command of the job. Japanese firms carry this organisation into their overseas undertakings.

China receives technology and education in Chinese-owned undertakings, protection for Chinese Pacificans and a policy of no missionaries. **[PROPOSED]** An 1868 treaty preserves ordinary foreign treaty rights while including a jointly licensed family-emigration article. <!-- CR-081: the mission, the partner and the Pacific line --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Yung Wing's machinery orders go to Carquinez; the Educational Mission studies in San Francisco with naval-academy places, and because those places teach what China can use, the Qing does not recall it in 1881. Pacifica's partner is Li Hongzhang's Beiyang administration and Sheng Xuanhuai's enterprises, not the court and not the provinces at large. By 1894 the mission's men, in their early thirties, work in Li's and Sheng's offices: the telegraph administration from 1881, the coal and steamship companies, the customs and the Tianjin schools. Tang Shaoyi is one of them. From 1880 the China Merchants' company runs a Pacific line to Honolulu and San Francisco, officered by the mission's naval graduates by the 1890s, and Hawaii keeps its own entry rules. Chinese steamships and Kaiping mines draw on Pacifican engineering and credit. California wheat and Chinese Pacifican committees support North China famine relief in 1876–1879. **[MODEL — CR-081]** The grain saves perhaps a million lives. **[PROPOSED]** A proposed prosecution and execution of the Los Angeles rioters after the 1871 massacre makes protection costly at home. The partners judge Pacifica by enforcement, not declarations.

Korea signs its first Western treaty with Pacifica around 1878–1880 through Li Hongzhang's mediation under the **[PROPOSED]** diplomatic plan. Advisers support Korean neutrality in 1882–1887 but knowingly stand aside from the 1884 Gapsin coup. Their restraint preserves a relationship while abandoning reformers who trusted them.

## 15. The Spanish window and the settlement of families (1869–1885)

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** Elias first tries to buy. In 1869–70 a confidential offer for the Philippines goes to Prim, who had refused Napoleon's Mexican empire at Veracruz and seen the American war at first hand. Prim declines: no rising in the islands justifies a sale, and his name rests on avenging insults to the flag. He keeps the channel open. Assassins in the Calle del Turco close it at the end of 1870.

Spain's seizure of a Pacifican schooner off Jolo in the spring of 1873 gives Elias his pretext. The Republic refuses compensation and a public offer to buy; a republic cannot be seen to sell territory. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** In August 1873, with the cantonal revolt dividing Spain's navy, a Pacifican squadron fights a one-day action against the Cavite station in Manila Bay. It does not land. Governor-General Alaminos signs a local convention: an indemnity, the crew's release, and an anchorage for a Pacifican squadron to protect its trade. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Japan, negotiating treaty revision with every power that holds a treaty, Spain among them, sells the damaged ships coal for the nearest home port and refuses them a dock. The nearest home port is Honolulu.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** That autumn the *Virginius* brings the Union and Spain close to war over Cuba; Pacifica's good offices in Madrid help settle it, and Madrid remembers. In March 1874 Serrano's Republic gives the Bank of Spain its monopoly of notes to pay for the war at home. For the war overseas it borrows Pacifican gold, secured on the Philippine customs and tobacco revenues. Pacifican agents sit in the customs houses, and the Banco Español-Filipino services the loan in Manila. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** The price includes the pardon of the Filipino exiles on Guam. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The Restoration of December 1874 inherits a debt it cannot pay. In 1876, with the Carlists beaten and Martínez Campos's army growing toward a hundred thousand men in Cuba, Cánovas, the historian of Spain's decline, asks the Cortes for a law of sale. He calls it the honourable liquidation of a republican debt that saves Cuba. Pacifica's price is to recognize no Cuban insurgents and to close its ports to their filibusters; Union abolitionists say Pacifican gold pays for the army that keeps Cuba slave. The sale includes the Philippines, Marianas, Carolines and Palau. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The islands pay for themselves. A Philippine Redemption Loan, guaranteed by the Confederation and serviced from the Philippine customs, raises the price in London and San Francisco, and a covenant limits its service to the gain the islands make over their last Spanish years, published every year. Filipinos still say they paid for their own sale. The government answers that they never paid more than Pacifica added.

**[PROPOSED — CR-029]** Until 1887 the Philippines is a dependency of the Confederation, not a member state, with no members in the confederal chambers. Its Organic Act vests authority in the Providentor's office, not his person. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Romualdo Pacheco, schooled in Honolulu as a boy and now forty-four, goes from Sacramento to Manila as Governor-General for a long term. He governs with a Philippine Council, and the Audiencia keeps Spanish law. His contempt for the Chinese is the Sacramento faction's; Manila's Chinese merchants and Elias's protection keep him on a short leash. Forced labour, forced crop sales and the tobacco monopoly end, and offices open by examination. The monopoly's factories and leaf are sold to a chartered tobacco company in which the state keeps a quarter.<!-- CR-039: the state, not the Crown, before 1914 --> The Marianas, Carolines and Palau are separated from Manila under a naval administration at Apra Harbour. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The sale follows Malcampo's capture of Jolo in February 1876, so Pacifica inherits a Spanish garrison and a war. In 1878 a compact with the Sultan of Sulu, and separate compacts with the datus who hold the real power, settle the south. Their Tausug text is authoritative, and the Sultan alone can consent to undo them. The cholera of 1882 comes in through Jolo and Zamboanga and reaches Manila in August, killing thousands in the city and tens of thousands in the islands. Polytechnic physicians read it as a matter of water. In 1883 a Municipal Ordinance gives every town an elected tribunal, its own funds and no parish priest over its budget, and binds the towns to piped water, quarantine of ships, latrines kept away from wells, and burial away from rivers. Health is taught through the cabezas and the parish priests, with no cordons or burned quarters. The Office of Public Measures publishes deaths by town; Elias deals with the towns over the heads of Manila and the friars, as he dealt with the county boards. In 1878 the Sultan's lease of North Borneo goes to a Pacifican Borneo company rather than to the British. Friar-estate purchases, financed by Philippine land bonds under the same guarantee, resell land to tenants in installments to a land board. Reforms do not erase coercion, and the land board becomes a landlord too.

<!-- CR-036: Koch and the cholera at Manila, 1882 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** The physicians who read the cholera as a matter of water are led by Robert Koch, who came to Pacifica in 1872 as an unknown country doctor from Posen and works in a Polytechnic laboratory at Carquinez. There he traced the life of anthrax in 1876, and in the spring of 1882 he found the bacillus of tuberculosis. That autumn in Manila his team finds the comma-shaped bacillus of cholera in the water and shows how it travels. The tables of deaths by town, before and after the pipes, go round the world. **[PROPOSED]** He had answered the Republic's call for district physicians in the mining country, and Cohn, seeing his microscope work, had the Polytechnic give him the laboratory.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** The Manila station, a squadron kept in Asian waters to secure the convention and then the loan, sharpens the admirals' demand for unconditional control of Pearl Harbor and carries the strategic conflict into Hawaii's 1874 crisis. **[PROPOSED]** Pacifica supplies Japan's Formosa expedition that year and mediates Ryukyu in 1879: Japan keeps the islands, China receives trade guarantees. In the Satsuma Rebellion of 1877 Pacifica backs the Meiji government against Saigō and its oldest Japanese friends. Friendship remains a strategy whose beneficiaries can change.

Assisted immigration of women and families extends into the new interior territories, with China, Japan and the Philippines as partners. **[PROPOSED]** Mercer-style parties, larger family land grants with wives owning their shares, irrigation districts and railways support settlement; Mennonite alternative-service teams carry Mormon irrigation practice into new districts. A marriage-floor case in the 1870s brings a Mormon–Shoshone or Mormon–Chinese union before the confederal court; the pairing and exact judgment remain **[OPEN]** within the approved test-case direction.

**[PROPOSED]** Hakka families lead early Chinese family migration, jointly licensed brokers can also become traffickers, and an earlier Japanese emigration scheme sustains the Wakamatsu colony and arranged marriages. Filipino reformers and families begin arriving after 1876, especially in Catholic, Spanish-speaking Arizona and New Mexico. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** From the 1880s Filipino families also come to Hawaii as kindred settlers under Victoria's programme of restoring the Hawaiian people. Victoria's international programme combines passage, reception, training and scholarships with household, employer, philanthropic and public funding. Its workbook schedule remains a scenario, not a new population guarantee.

The republic's mixed Californio and Hawaiian inheritance gains Chinese and Japanese family traditions. First-generation mixed children come of age around 1895; Hapa unions become commonplace symbols over the following generations. The ideal faces nativist politics and the fugitive agreement rather than erasing them. No census target or legal proclamation proves that equal civic standing has become daily experience. <!-- CR-071: the Feather River -->**[APPROVED — CR-071]** The ideal's hardest test in the interior comes on the Feather in 1886.

<!-- CR-036: the interior's own schools: Santa Fe, Deseret, the Sutro School; the 1878 eclipse -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** Since the war the interior has built schools of its own. The Colegio de Santa Fe, founded in 1864, trains the surveyors of the treaty lines and the engineers of the acequias. **[PROPOSED]** It keeps an observatory in the high, clear air. When the total eclipse of 29 July 1878 crosses the mountains, its spectroscopists set up Pacifica's station at a railway siding in the Great Divide Basin, a few miles from the Union's astronomers across the frontier. **[APPROVED — CR-036]** Deseret's university, revived after accession, measures how much water an acre needs and teaches the arid world to build canals. At Virginia City the Sutro School, founded with the tunnel in 1869, studies the heat of the deep Comstock and the cold that lets men work in it.

## 16. Vladivostok, the Customs Inspectorate and the Korean guarantee (1877–1887)

While Russia fights Turkey, Pacifica and Japan concentrate one maritime action at Vladivostok in February 1878. Marcus Vance I dies there in winter fighting; ice, machinery and burning fuel give his last action its physical terror. The exact operation remains **[PROPOSED]**, with no Pacifican army marching through Siberia. Southern Sakhalin is restored to Japan under the proposed settlement, folded into the Congress of Berlin; Britain recognizes the Philippine purchase. Russia remains powerful and seeks a railway connection to reinforce its Far Eastern position. Japan later takes the eastern railway and Vladivostok before Russia can complete its own through connection. The line will reach the Pacific under Japanese control, through Chinese-owned Manchuria.

Elias loses the commander whose restraint and organizational skill made distant commitments possible. **[PROPOSED]** Rafael Soberanes, a Californio officer unrelated to the Vances, serves as Marcus’s chief of staff, completes the operation after his death and brings the squadron home. Born in Monterey in 1847 and examined into the Polytechnic at fourteen, he earns the authority later used to lead it through service rather than descent. His name and birth are settled by the later constitutional record; the unprovided details of his early life and exact service remain qualified. Caleb remains a living alternative to professional calculation.

In 1884–1885 France's war with China opens the Keelung window. **[PROPOSED]** A Pacifican squadron protects the coal trade after Ferry's fall, preferring demonstration to battle. China grants a coal concession to a Chinese–Pacifican company, retains sovereignty and receives railway construction and fleet-rebuilding credit. <!-- CR-081: the fleet credit --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** The fleet credit is lent in money, its purpose clause excludes capital ships, and Tokyo, as Pacifica's ally, is shown the contracts. China buys its cruisers in Britain and Germany; no Pacifican yard builds a Chinese warship and no Pacifican officer serves in one. Most of the mission's naval graduates go to the merchant line, and a minority serve in the Beiyang fleet.

Soberanes commands the Keelung squadron in 1884–1885 without a battle, with Kiyoshi captaining its Japanese-crewed ships.

The Customs Inspectorate passes to Pacifican leadership in 1885 by China's appointment. Edmund Fong Kai-ming, born in San Francisco in 1859 to a Taishan merchant family and a customs officer since 1878, becomes the named official through whom trade, revenue and divided loyalty acquire a personal face. **[PROPOSED]** Hart takes the British ministership. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Li backs Fong, multinational staff remain, Chinese staff rise and accounts are published. The small agency fee and China’s retained appointment power distinguish administration from territorial seizure.

Private Pacifican merchants can still carry opium; no general state ban is adopted. **[PROPOSED]** Strict collection of duty and inland tax raises Chinese revenue and makes British supply dearer, without claiming moral purity. Pacifican credit secured on customs makes friendship increasingly consequential to China's independence.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** Papal arbitration in 1885 confirms Pacifica's Carolines on condition of freedom for the Catholic missions, while granting Germany trade and coaling rights; Germany keeps the Marshalls, and the settlement records Pacifica's claim to Wake, taken five years earlier for a future cable. Leo XIII's price and Pacifica's are one bargain: the Philippine parishes pass to Filipino secular priests, Filipino bishops are named with the right to succeed, and the friar estates are paid for partly into an endowment that stays in the islands. The cause for which Gómez, Burgos and Zamora were garrotted in 1872 is won by treaty. Port Hamilton in 1885–1887 becomes a near-war Britain loses diplomatically. British withdrawal rests on a proposed China–Russia–Pacifica guarantee of Korean territory. The decision gives Pacifica a duty to protect Korea without owning it. Foreign commitments pause around the 1887 succession; the network must survive its founder before it can expand again. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The pause has a price in Samoa. Hawaii had signed the 1878 treaty for Pago Pago on the Confederation's behalf, and in 1886–87 Kalākaua carries a Polynesian compact to Malietoa Laupepa. In August 1887 Germany's consul deports Malietoa and installs Tamasese, and Pacifica, burying its founder, does not answer.

---

# PART IV — Hawaii, the ruined marriage and the founder's death (1870–1887)

## 17. The island alliance unravels after Lot's death (1872–1876)

During their early voyages and negotiations, Lot Kapuāiwa and Elias recognized much of themselves in one another. As king from November 1863, Lot retains the Kuhina Nui and bargains with its incumbent, Victoria, over Hawaiian policy. The 1865 enabling settlement preserves crown, co-executive and local institutions, yet gives the confederal cabinet increasing scope to determine the conditions under which those institutions operate. Victoria's office still carries authority within Hawaii; her ability to restrain the common executive depends on government support, representatives, finance and enforceable commitments.

Lot and Elias continue to disagree over the nature of their alliance. Elias's commanders increasingly regard Hawaii as the center of an integrated naval system whose facilities cannot be placed beyond confederal control. Lot sees a kingdom whose cooperation was freely promised and whose crown cannot survive if every customs and military decision becomes a Californian administrative order. They remain bound by friendship and necessity, making the disagreements harder to reconcile.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** Lot dies in December 1872 without a direct heir. Under Article 22 of his constitution the crown passes to his sister, and Victoria becomes Queen of Hawaii without an election. She appoints her half-sister Ruth Keʻelikōlani Kuhina Nui: a great landholder and governor of Hawaiʻi island who speaks no English and keeps the old ways, a reassurance to the chiefs. Lunalilo, the man her brothers forbade her, becomes the face of everyone who fears a queen married to the Providentor. Queen Emma, Alexander's Anglican widow, leads those who look to London.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** In September 1873 the Queen's Guard mutinies. Its drillmaster, Captain József Jajczay, is a Hungarian veteran of the hussars who escorted Maximilian to Mexico, hired by Lot to train a guard loyal to the crown alone and owing nothing to Monterey's academy. The adjutant general is Colonel Charles Judd, a missionary's son and Victoria's schoolmate at the Royal School. When Jajczay jails four men for deserting the treasury post, the soldiers beat him and Judd, seize the barracks and load two cannon. The admirals at Honolulu offer marines; Victoria refuses them, because Hawaiians will settle this themselves. On the first day she crosses Palace Walk to the barracks with her women and no guard and speaks to the men in Hawaiian. She dismisses Jajczay and Judd, as the men demand, and the men lay down their arms before her. She does not disband the Guard: she gives its command to Major William Moehonua and takes its oath again herself. Jajczay leaves the islands and dies in Costa Rica the next year. Judd, whom she has sacrificed, attaches himself to Kalākaua. She suspects Kalākaua's hand in the mutiny, says nothing, and makes him answerable for the Guard's loyalty.

**[APPROVED — CR-027; amended CR-029]** For the admirals of the Manila squadron, who brought their damaged ships into Honolulu that September because neutral Japan would sell them coal but not a dock, a kingdom with a guard loyal only to its Queen is the decisive argument: Pearl Harbor lies beside a force Hawaii can use. They insist that the harbour can no longer depend on Hawaiian consent. Lunalilo dies on 3 February 1874. Days later his followers rise in Honolulu in the Queen's name against the confederal demand. Confederal marines land to restore order; there are deaths in the rioting, and each side blames the other. Victoria keeps the Guard in its barracks. She will not let a rising in her name become a war against her husband.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** In March 1874 she signs the Puʻuloa Convention: a fifty-year lease of Pearl Harbor and its shores to the Confederation and a treaty of common defence. It is a lease, not a cession; no Hawaiian land is alienated. Ruth countersigns as Kuhina Nui, and the legislature ratifies narrowly, carried by the nobles Kalākaua brings to the Queen's side. The reversal of her 1855 refusal is her own act, made to keep the crown rather than lose it to London through Emma or to the planters. Elias wants the harbour for cold reasons: the Manila station, the transpacific fleet and a kingdom whose legislature an outside power could sway.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** The illegality comes after. Between 1874 and 1876 the confederal cabinet stretches the Convention by ordinance under the Enabling Law: harbour police, then the Honolulu port administration, then the defence districts, and in 1876 an appointed Territorial Council of planters and confederal officials that takes over the legislature's business. The ordinances breach the Enabling Law's protected provisions and Article XIV's bar on annexation. Ruth refuses to recognize the Council and resigns as Kuhina Nui; the Queen leaves the office vacant. Hawaii's Supreme Court declares the ordinances void. No one can enforce the judgment against confederal marines, and Victoria will not ask her people to bleed for it. She lets the ruling stand on paper and the Council stand in fact, as she once let the constitution's form go to see her brother proclaimed. No missing signature or later claim of necessity supplies consent. The judgment is never reversed, leaving sovereigntists a legal claim even after the Act of Settlement of 1907.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** Emma becomes the loyalists' counter-queen and remains so until her death in 1885. In 1883 she builds a country house on the Puʻuloa shore outside the leased ground and plants banyans and two thousand coconuts at the gate of the fleet.

Afterward Victoria and Elias remain married and politically associated, but their trust has been damaged beyond easy repair. Elias insists that divided strategic authority threatened the security of the Pacific; she answers that no security can be called common if one partner has lost the ability to withhold consent. Both have contributed to the institutions whose consequences they now confront. The marriage survives through affection that neither will admit has vanished, mutual dependency, and Victoria's ambitions for what the empire might still become.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** The Territorial Council's English Schools Ordinance of 1876 removes Hawaiian from government schools. Many Hawaiian parents have long wanted English for their children; the planters on the Council make it the only language. Elias, who needs the Council for the harbour works and the cable, does not overrule it: he will not spend his capital on what he thinks minor, and the 1887 constitution's entrenchment of Hawaiian comes eleven years too late for a generation of children. Victoria fights the ordinance and loses, then pays for Hawaiian-language schools through the Kaʻahumanu Society for the rest of her life. This cultural wrong becomes part of the price of the order.

In 1875 Elias chooses Kāneʻohe Bay for a new capital. Honolulu carries the memory of February 1874 and San Francisco the bankers' and vigilantes' power; the Koʻolau separates the proposed Crown precinct from Kiyoshi's future fleet base at Pearl Harbor. Victoria leases the ground from the Hawaiian Crown lands and insists that it remain the throne's land, with Mōkapu's burial dunes unbuilt and Heʻeia fishpond kept working. Pearl Harbor is dredged in 1875–1885; the Pali railway tunnel and bay channel are built in 1876–1882. The San Francisco–Oʻahu cable opens in 1879 before the government moves. The Seat of Government Act transfers the offices from Monterey in 1885, into temporary halls. The capital's beauty rests on Crown land leased by a Queen governing under the disputed regime, local labor and expensive infrastructure.

## 18. Kalanimoku, the accident and a court divided (1870s)

Kalanimoku, Elias and Victoria’s legitimate son, born after the 1858 wedding and heir to his mother's Hawaiian crown, grows up amid Hawaiian traditions, frontier stories, technical education, sea voyages and the expectations of several political constituencies. He has fears, friendships and ambitions of his own. His name is settled; his birth year, age, personality and particular childhood experiences remain **[OPEN]**.

An accident in the late 1870s kills Kalanimoku and injures Victoria, leaving her unable to bear more children. Her grief and physical suffering create a division between what the state demands of her and what she can privately endure. Elias throws himself into travel, technical work and the organization of campaigns. Their attempts to comfort each other increasingly misfire. The accident’s circumstances, exact date and medical consequences remain **[OPEN]**.

**[PROPOSED — CR-027]** After the accident Victoria stops singing. Elias keeps a phonograph cylinder of her voice, made shortly before; on his deathbed in 1887 he asks for it.

Elias’s other relationships precede Kalanimoku’s death. Silas, his eldest son, born in 1851 before the marriage to Abigail Merritt, and Kiyoshi, his second son by a Satsuma mother, grow into the leaders of distinct constituencies. **[PROPOSED]** Kiyoshi is born in Kagoshima in 1859 to Uehara Hisa, a glass master's daughter and interpreter. **[APPROVED — CR-027]** He is conceived in the spring of 1858, during the Edo negotiations and months before Elias's wedding. When Victoria learns of him remains **[OPEN]**. Elias withholds his name from both. Their mothers have households, loyalties and political stakes of their own. Victoria retains political ambition and helps devise a viable inheritance for the empire.

## 19. The Golden Afternoon and the examination state (late 1870s–1880s)

The empire appears to reach a stable maturity. Cities, universities, arts and public works expand; a sophisticated culture of industry and imperial syncretism takes shape across the Pacific. Victoria encourages artistic and educational institutions and helps shape the state's memory of its founder. Elias's attention moves restlessly from trade to engineering and military construction even as infirmity makes activity more difficult.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** Kalākaua, the suitor she refused, becomes the Queen's minister of the image and her envoy. He revives the hula, prints the genealogical chants and retells Hawaii's legends for a foreign audience as the equals of the Iliad and the romances of chivalry. His tale of the Iron Knife, Japanese castaways on Maui whose descendants were plentiful, gives the Japanese partnership a Hawaiian prehistory. In 1881 he travels round the world in her name with Judd as his chamberlain, meets the Meiji Emperor and proposes an Oceanian union sealed by marrying his niece, the young Kaʻiulani, to Prince Komatsu; the Emperor politely declines. His motto, *Hoʻoulu Lāhui*, increase the nation, stands over the Queen's mortality figures. In his book he mourns Kekuaokalani, who died defending the old gods against the first Kaʻahumanu's canoes, and he publishes it under the patronage of the fourth. She never lets him near the purse. <!-- CR-079: Muybridge and Le Prince --> **[APPROVED — CR-079]** In 1878 he asks her to pay Eadweard Muybridge, an English photographer acquitted three years before of killing his wife's lover, to photograph a galloping horse with a battery of cameras on a Palo Alto stud farm. The Queen's government pays, and goes on paying through the 1880s while Muybridge projects his sequences with his zoopraxiscope. In 1887 Kalākaua's office brings Louis Le Prince, a French painter who has spent six years in New York building motion cameras, to San Francisco. **[APPROVED — CR-027]** He dies in San Francisco in 1891, and his project of the image passes to the press seat of the Five.

<!-- CR-043: Kaʻiulani lives --> **[APPROVED — CR-043]** Kalākaua's niece does not die young. Kaʻiulani is schooled in England from 1889. In 1896 her physicians find an exophthalmic goitre, and in 1897 she goes to Theodor Kocher at Bern, who ties off its arteries and then removes part of the gland, leaving her enough of it. In January 1899, at home on Hawaiʻi, she rides into the mountain rain and comes down with rheumatic fever. Her heart, no longer driven by the goitre, holds. Months in bed and the salicylate of the day bring her through, with a damaged valve she carries for the rest of her life. <!-- CR-081: her Chinese --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** On her return to Honolulu in 1897 she begins court Chinese with a northern teacher found through the Qing consulate, and keeps at it through her months in bed; from 1902 she is Victoria's envoy at the Qing court. **[PROPOSED — CR-043]** From 1909 she travels with Julian's missions to the European capitals, where her school friends have married into the houses he needs. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** In 1908 she is needed more at Peking. She spends the autumn there, is at the Lake Palace for the deaths of November, and joins Julian in Europe early in 1909 for the Bosnian settlement. She returns to the court in November 1909 and in January–February 1912. When she dies is **[OPEN]**.

The decorated Pacific-war commander who leads the Polytechnic, Rafael Soberanes, and other academy-trained officials substitute measurements, numbered personnel, examinations and statutory procedures for the personal loyalties of the old armies. The method promises fairness across races and provinces but also reduces human beings to entries in large systems. Financial syndicates, officers and hereditary court factions compete to direct the machinery in anticipation of Elias's death.

<!-- CR-037: anonymous scripts and avoidance -->
**[APPROVED — CR-037]** Soberanes guards the machinery against patrons as well as factions. From the late 1870s candidates are known to their examiners only by number, and clerks recopy every script so that no hand is recognised and send it to examiners from another state. No examiner judges a candidate from their own state or college, officials take their first postings away from home, and every board that fills a chair includes outsiders. A patron, a family or a guild can still recommend a candidate; it cannot pass one.

<!-- CR-071: the memorandum, the assumption and the Collar --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** In the winter of 1878–79, back from Vladivostok, Soberanes writes Elias a memorandum on what Japan has shown: money first, then the men, then the armed men, then the map, then the vote. Victoria reads it, and its first step is money. The slack years after 1873 and falling prices have put the members' railway and road bonds into arrears, while the Reserve Bank carried the coast's largest private bank through August 1875. Now the Confederation offers to take the members' debts.

**[APPROVED — CR-071]** The Assumption Act of 1879 goes to each member legislature with *An Act for the Common Execution of the Laws*, to be taken together or refused together. A member that takes them has its debt assumed and its paper guaranteed, and its treasury is audited by the Polytechnic. Its treasurer and auditor are examined and posted from outside. Its governor, still elected, accepts a confederal commission as Commissioner of the Confederation and answers to the Providentor for the common law; each receives his commission from Elias in person at Monterey. The Committee party's papers call it the Collar, and the *Deseret News* takes up the name. Oregon, Columbia, Idaho, western Montana, Washoe and New Mexico take it in 1879–80. California, which does not need rescuing, refuses, and so does Deseret, whose legislature will not open Deseret's treasury, and the Church's accounts with it, to Carquinez's auditors. Hawaii, under its Territorial Council, is not asked. Through the 1870s and 1880s the members adopt California's codes one after another on Party majorities.

<!-- CR-072: the examined take the Party --> **[APPROVED — CR-072]** About 1880 Broderick gives up the direction of the Prosperity Party, and the examined take it over. The estates and the Party are not rivals. The posts are examined and the Party cannot pass a candidate, but the boards that pass the budgets are elected, and the Party gives the estates their majorities. From the early 1880s its county slates are drawn from the Polytechnic's graduates, teachers, engineers, assayers, surgeons and surveyors, who are its cadre. **[PROPOSED — CR-072]** Soberanes sees what the Party is and what it is not yet: the founder's machine for winning votes, with no idea of what to do with the counties it wins. He gives it the use. Where the Polytechnic's surveys find a county behind its neighbours, the Party's slate runs on catching up, and the confederal grant arrives with a board ready to spend it.

<!-- CR-071: the trades and the soldiers come into the Party --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The Party takes in the trades as well. In the slack year of 1877 Denis Kearney draws crowds to the sandlot and loses them when work comes back. From about 1880 Frank Roney, an Irish moulder who despises Kearney's methods, builds the Party's trades side. Hiring goes through the trades, not the brokers, and the ticket runs on *Eight hours and the same wage*, which the Confederation's own works keep. To the settler the same wage means no one can undercut him; to the Chinese workman it means equal treatment, and the trades admit everyone who earns it. The Party enrols the soldiers too: in the interior its lodges are largely veterans of 1861–65 and men home from the companies abroad.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** The examinations reach the officer corps in mid-career. Many of the old army's fighting officers fail them. Silas fails the mathematics and is capped below general rank; Elias refuses to intervene. The American officers' grievance against the examination state has his face.

<!-- CR-071: the Militia Act, the Commissions Board and Sherman's refusal --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The Militia Act of 1882 makes every member militia a reserve of the common army, its officers commissioned by the Confederation and its arms kept in confederal arsenals. Sherman chairs the Commissions Board, which examines every commission, the militias' with the regulars' and the Auxiliary's, and keeps the commissions out of the Party's hands. Elias accepts it as Sherman's price: rank is no longer a reward the Party can give, no Party committee stands in the chain of command, and no serving officer stands on the Party's ticket in a county he commands. At Sherman's request, with Elias's consent, the officers who fail the board's examination are kept together as a command of their own, the inland command. From 1882 to 1885 the Auxiliary's veteran battalions pass to the interior garrisons and come under the inland command, and Silas commands on the Sierra line. In June 1884 men of the old army, and Prosperity men who do not trust the Queen's influence or the Japanese naval party, offer Sherman the state after the founder. He answers that he will not take it. <!-- CR-081: Sherman's death --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Sherman dies on 14 February 1891, aged seventy-one. The Commissions Board outlives him as his legacy and keeps the commissions out of the Party's hands.

<!-- CR-071: the survey --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** In 1880 the Superintendency commissions a survey of water districts, stations and land values, headed by Theodore Judah, on whose tables the Patrimony's Development Fund lends. Its second purpose is the map of counties that will replace the members, and it is known to Elias, Soberanes and Judah. Ralston, who sees every sheet the Fund lends on, works it out and says nothing. The survey reprints the census of 1880 by its own districts, on a provisional map of about 130 districts in 1884, and every confederal table from then on is printed by district. The second map, the 124 in 1886, is argued over seat by seat inside the Party's county committees. Where two towns want the seat, the railway station decides, and the loser receives the court, the school or the hall.

<!-- CR-071: the Pacific Companies' first generation --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** By 1887 the Pacific Companies have served abroad for a generation under the venture licence. <!-- CR-074: the first company abroad (T6) --> The first company abroad is raised at Shanghai in 1860 and serves until 1863. Veterans of 1861–65 serve in Japan between 1866 and 1875. In the war of 1878 the Republic issues letters of marque, a company guards the Keelung coal in 1884–85, and from 1885 Pacificans serve in the Customs' preventive service. <!-- CR-074: Ward, the will, Suzhou, Hakodate, 1878 and Keelung --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** The Shanghai company is Frederick Townsend Ward's. On his way to China in 1859 he swears allegiance to the Republic at San Francisco. **[PROPOSED — CR-074]** The oath alone makes him a Pacifican, so that no American consul can touch his trade. **[APPROVED — CR-074]** The company serves under the Pacifican consul's licence, paid by the taotai Wu Xu and the banker Yang Fang. Its first assault on Songjiang in July 1860 fails. Ward finds Vincente Macanaya and some eighty Manilamen on the waterfront, and the town falls with two of every three men killed or wounded. Sacramento steamboat men run the force's river steamers on the Huangpu. Pacifican officers and sergeants command Western, Filipino and then Chinese ranks, and the force holds Shanghai. In March 1862 Ward sends his brother Harry, still an American, to New York with Chinese money to buy steamers. On 21 September he is shot at Cixi, and on the gunboat taking him to Ningpo he dictates his will: what Wu and Yang owe him, fifty thousand taels for his wife Changmei, Yang Fang's daughter, ten thousand taels' worth in gold to the Republic's Treasury, the rest to his brother and sister. He dies the next day. In 1863 Harry saves the steamers from seizure as an enemy's property by swearing they are his own, and sells them to the Union Army; the Pacifican consul, Ward's executor, sets the Chinese money the steamers cost against Harry's share of the estate. The company serves on under its own captain and Gordon's orders. In December 1863 Li Hongzhang has the Taiping leaders who surrendered Suzhou put to death, and the company leaves the service, because its articles forbid it to serve a commander who kills prisoners who have surrendered to him. In 1866 a survey company, many of its men home from the technical mission on the Gulf, works the Hakodate coal for the shogunate and then for the new government. When Enomoto's fleet arrives in 1868 it will not serve him, and in 1869 Kuroda finds its maps intact and hires its men. Its sergeants train his settler soldiers from 1874, on land the Ainu were never asked for, and in 1875 Japanese sergeants take their place; sergeants who join the army mission are discharged from the companies first. In the spring and summer of 1878 armed sealers and whalers sail under letters of marque and bring a handful of Russian supply ships to the prize courts at San Francisco and Honolulu, and a few men make fortunes; Russia answers with its Volunteer Fleet. **[MODEL]** About a dozen ships hold letters in 1878. **[APPROVED — CR-074]** At Keelung in the spring of 1885, after Ferry's fall, a company lands to guard the miners at the Keelung pits. **[APPROVED — CR-071]** Some thousands have served, and hundreds have bought farms in the interior with what they brought home. They served beside Chinese and Japanese comrades, and they are the settlers least ready to hate them.

The Polytechnic becomes the world's leading technical university system from the 1870s, with state-funded chairs, laboratories and technical schools drawing talent from Europe and Asia. Appointment to a chair has brought citizenship without a religious or national test since the 1850s. Tesla arrives in San Francisco in 1884; Steinmetz and Baekeland join in 1889. A fast patent office and the licensing of Polytechnic inventions turn the examination state's learning into firms, equipment and careers. <!-- CR-057: the open rule --> **[APPROVED — CR-057]** Every Polytechnic patent is licensed to any firm, Pacifican or foreign, at the same published royalty (Concord partners from 1928 under the Hayama annex), and Tesla puts his own polyphase patents on the open list.

<!-- CR-034: the first chairs stay; Zeuner at Freiberg from 1871 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** The Polytechnic's first chairs stay for what they have built. Zeuner keeps his students, his testing floor and his programme until 1871, when he goes to direct the Mining Academy at Freiberg, and from Freiberg he sends Pacifica engineers. By 1875 Carquinez alone has about ninety teachers and 1,400 students, as many as Vienna's polytechnic **[MODEL]**. **[PROPOSED]** Saxony's amnesty of 1862 would have let Zeuner teach at home; he stayed.

<!-- CR-036: californium, the college of 1866, Michelson and Morley, Ehrlich, Kitasato; CR-050-R1: the college named Ralston College -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** Its learning is not only applied. In 1873 Carquinez finds the metal Mendeleev had predicted beneath aluminium, in zinc blende the Sutro School sends down from the deep Comstock, and calls it californium, and the periodic table is proved right on a Pacifican specimen. Ralston College, founded in 1866 by San Francisco's merchant and banking houses of every faith, draws Sylvester from Carquinez and Cohn from the Methodists' college. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** It bears the name of William Ralston, its chief founder, whose fortune is the Comstock's silver, so that Carquinez, built on the gold royalty, and Ralston College become Pacifica's colleges of gold and silver. <!-- CR-069 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-069]** The houses chose his name because he kept his doors open in the run of 1862 and published the offer he refused in 1864, and the silver his Gold Hill agency brought to San Francisco endows the college. **[APPROVED — CR-036]** In 1868 it brings the chemist Edward Morley from New Jersey. In 1878 the Murphys boy Michelson begins there to measure the speed of light. Paul Ehrlich, who read Koch's anthrax paper as a student in Silesia, comes to Koch's laboratory that year and stains the cells no one could see. In 1887 Michelson and Morley's interferometer finds no ether wind. **[PROPOSED]** From 1885 Kitasato Shibasaburō works in Koch's laboratory, and Japanese medicine begins to learn from Pacifica.

<!-- CR-049: rival schools, a method that fails, the programmed machine -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Ralston College's founding in 1866 is the first great contest for talent. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** Both colleges claim the Calculation Office, and it becomes a shared service of the League with its machines at Carquinez, where Peirce stays. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Kate Layne marries Hugh Curran in 1866 and keeps her post. In 1867 Kate Curran finds that a checking rule Peirce devised has been discarding recurring observations as instrument faults, some of them hers. The register has kept everything, so she reconstructs the discarded readings, and they are real. Peirce has to acknowledge in public that the checking rule he was proudest of was wrong, and Genth puts the next computing money into independent checking and holds back the machines. She is credited only as an assistant, and she does not let it go. The colleges agree terms for recording omitted observations and contrary results, and the case is the first that the journals' corrections columns teach: the original claim, the correction and the name of whoever found the difficulty. The custom it founds will one day give credit back; this time it does not. Relay arithmetic enters service in 1868–1870, and limited preset sequences follow by 1872; the staff learn not to mistake a calculation that runs for an account that is true. Zeuner's leave-taking in 1871 becomes the model for every master's: it names his successors and his unfinished questions, and his instruments and notebooks pass on.

<!-- CR-049: the customs and the civic usage of mana -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Public provings of works, county showings, contests with declared terms and the welcomes and leave-takings of masters make skill visible, and fellows coming home from Japan, Hawaiʻi, the mountains and the islands shape these customs rather than merely undergo them. In Victoria's educational circle and Kalākaua's cultural programme, Hawaiian participants give the ethos a sacred register: achievement and remembered obligation acquire *mana* for some people, in the master's tools, the returning fellow, the works that stand. The historian Samuel Kamakau, whose series on the old ways runs in the Honolulu press from 1866, argues in print until his death in 1876 over whether the masters of the crafts can carry *mana* and how that sits with genealogy and the gods; the argument goes on after him, in the newspapers and from the pulpit. The founder who wrote "not I" ignores the kingdom's own judges in 1874–1876, and the ethos he founded has no answer for him.

<!-- CR-076: the earned cup --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** The cup belongs to a thing done. At a proving, a master's leave-taking, a company's return, a race won, a dam opened or an examination passed, the others fill the cup of the one who did it. At Carquinez the cox pours for the stroke. No one pours their own. Japanese and Korean partners bring the same habit of filling one another's cups, and in Hawaiian ceremony ʻawa is served by another hand, so the custom feels old to every part of the Realm. Someone who drinks alone or by day is understood to be in trouble. Someone drunk on the scaffold, on the road behind a team or in the street puts the people around them at risk, and loses standing in lodge or Company; an officer or examined official loses the post. Boys do not become men by drinking, men do not court women with it, and no one shows rank by what they can hold. In the Californio and Hispano counties wine at the family's midday table on a feast day survives, never spirits and never in a public house, and each generation keeps it less. The railways dismiss a man found on duty with drink in him, and the rule spreads to the hoists, the switchboards and the engine rooms.

<!-- CR-049: the atomic branch, 1864–1877 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Carquinez buys the Joachimsthal works' uranium residues, which nobody else wants, and separates them fraction by fraction, assaying each with the electroscope instead of the balance. In 1868 an active substance follows bismuth through every separation, a new element. In 1869 a far more active one follows barium and shows new lines in Genth's spectroscope. **[OPEN]** Their names; californium is the precedent for a Pacifican name that sticks. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The readings Peirce's rule threw away in 1867 were electroscopes near thorium preparations, drifting with the draughts because thorium gives off an active gas. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The gas is inert; its activity dies away at a fixed rate and leaves activity on whatever it touches; and a thorium product separated by chemistry loses its activity while the thorium regains it, both at rates Peirce's curves describe exactly. In 1870 Genth and Peirce conclude that one element is turning into another. The paper names them and not Kate Curran, whose readings found the gas, and her grievance becomes a quarrel the whole League hears. Morley confirms the result independently at Ralston College in 1872, and that year the journal's corrections column adds her name to the discovery, the first time the custom gives credit back. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** From 1872 the separation outgrows the laboratory, and the Superintendency's mill and the school set up a works at Carquinez that treats tonnes of residue a year. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** By 1876 Carquinez holds weighable salts of the barium-borne element and short-lived preparations of the bismuth-borne one, the first intense sources anywhere. In 1877 Morley's calorimeter shows the salt keeping itself warmer than its surroundings with no sign of slowing: gram for gram, more heat in a year than any chemical reaction gives once. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** From 1876 the works lends and sells sources to foreign laboratories under licence, each licence entered in a register, and for two decades Pacifica decides which laboratories abroad can do this physics at all.

<!-- CR-049: the atomic branch, 1876–1887, and the Light and Matter Works -->
