**[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1876 Eugen Goldstein, a Jewish physicist from Gleiwitz who has named cathode rays in Berlin, comes at twenty-six to an assistant's post under Genth, with citizenship and the laboratory's discharge tubes. With the Sprengel pump the vacuum is good enough for an electric field to bend the rays as well as a magnet, and their ratio of charge to mass is the same for every gas and cathode. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The part of the barium-borne element's radiation that a magnet bends gives the same ratio for its slowest particles and a smaller one the faster they move, an anomaly no one in Pacifica can explain until Europe's relativity of 1905. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1879 Goldstein and Michelson announce a common negative constituent of matter. William Francis Hillebrand, born in Honolulu in 1853, has come home in 1878 from Oahu College, Carquinez and Bunsen's laboratory at Heidelberg. In 1879 he reports a gas driven out of pitchblende as nitrogen; in 1881, with a better spectroscope, he finds in it and in the gas collecting over the barium-borne salt the yellow line of the solar element, helium, and prints the correction to his own claim. That year Hillebrand and Goldstein bend the heavy, positive radiation slightly with strong magnets and electric plates, and it proves to be helium that has lost both its charges. Goldstein takes his doctorate in 1881 and becomes master of the laboratory. In 1881 Tom Talbert finds yellow uranium-vanadium ore on Roc Creek near the Dolores River, on the Western Slope that the 1865 line made Pacifican. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** It lies in Uncompahgre Ute country, which cannot be sold; the Republic leases the mining ground through its own office and pays the lease fee to the Ute, so the claims open and the Ute stay. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Hillebrand assays the ore in 1881–1882, and small mines work it for the barium-borne element's salt, with the uranium stockpiled.

<!-- CR-060 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-060]** The Front Range comes over the way Idaho and Deseret came, by its own vote. Its gold fails in the refractory ores of 1864–67, its statehood bills are vetoed in Washington, and from 1868 a Pacifican smelter at the head of the Arkansas buys Gilpin's and Clear Creek's ores in coin. Pacifica's narrow gauge comes up from Santa Fe through the San Luis Valley and over Poncha Pass, and when Leadville's silver is struck in 1877 it is on Pacifica's side of the crest. Clear Creek and Gilpin vote themselves over in 1878–79 under the article, Boulder's mountain districts in 1880, and the survey of 1880–82 fixes the line along the foot of the mountains: Denver, Cañon City, Pueblo and Trinidad on the Union's side at the foot of another power's range. The Union's newspapers call it the theft of Colorado. <!-- CR-076: Denver, Kansas --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** In the 1880s the Union attaches its Colorado plains, Denver among them, to Kansas, which has been dry by its constitution since 1881. Without its mines Denver stays a small cattle and railway town. **[PROPOSED — CR-076]** Its joints live off the Pacifican miners who come down from Idaho Springs and Central City on payday, earning twice the Union's wages, for whiskey without an evening hour, card rooms and boarding houses, and Kansas's police look away. Kansans ride the other way, up the passes, to drink lawfully in the evening and at full price in the Pacifican mountain towns.

**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Screens of Sidot's blende show each alpha particle as a single flash. In the counting room of 1882–1883 Kate Curran's observers count the flashes by eye in the dark as particles from the bismuth-borne source strike beaten gold leaf, and a few bounce back. Peirce computes what spread-out and concentrated charge would each do, and only a concentrated charge explains the rebounds. In 1883 Goldstein proposes that almost all of an atom's mass sits in a tiny positive core. Counting also gives the number of atoms that decay each second, so the heat of 1877 can now be stated per atom. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** In 1883, the year of the nucleus, Elias charters the works, the Western Slope mines and the source trade as one state champion with the state holding the shares, and makes Hillebrand, twenty-nine, its director. The laboratories stay in the Polytechnic, and the estate pays for them. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** It is the Light and Matter Works, named after the laboratory of 1859. By 1886 an element can have atoms of different weights: Hillebrand finds pairs of active substances no chemistry separates and lead from uranium ores lighter than common lead, and Goldstein's positive rays sort atoms by mass. In 1884–1885 Morley settles the ratio of oxygen to hydrogen to a few parts in ten thousand, and in 1886 his atomic weights show helium lighter than four hydrogens by about seven parts in a thousand. Peirce and Morley conjecture that the missing mass and the barium-borne salt's heat are the same thing, and say plainly that they cannot turn one into the other; the reason will come from Europe in 1905. In 1885–1887 Michelson's laboratory builds a counter in which a single particle sets off a small discharge in gas, read on a photographed electrometer, with the steady high voltage from Tesla's works. In 1887 the Japanese partnership sends Nagaoka Hantarō, newly graduated in physics at Tokyo, to Carquinez.

<!-- CR-049: the programmed machine -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** A programmed electromechanical calculator enters service in 1876–1879, its sequences written in a common procedural notation worked out in 1878–1882 from Babbage's notation and the operation tables of Lovelace's notes. It runs because Peirce's circuits were designed on paper first, and dependable operation becomes an achievement the Office is honoured for.

<!-- CR-057: the Patrimony, the estates and the Previsión --> **[APPROVED — CR-057]** In 1880 Elias writes the charter of the Patrimony, which takes the state's rents, not its taxes: the royalties on gold, oil and minerals after the school of mines' share, the profits of the state's own estates after what they keep for their own works, the concessions' fees and, once the canal is open, its income. From 1890 it takes half the levy employers pay for guest workers, the price of a place in a rationed labour market. From 1900 the Realm stops selling the public domain beyond the farms it settles. It leases town lots, industrial sites, forests, grazing and water-power sites, and leases the land beside each new railway, station and dam at the value the works give it, and the rents go to the Patrimony. Until the peace of 1919 the Patrimony holds railway, mortgage and champions' bonds at home. Afterwards it invests across the world, never in a government in default or in treaty negotiation with Pacifica. Its board publishes the return it aims to beat and, every year, what it earned. The charter requires every loss to be written off in public, as *On the Public Arts* requires of every account. It spends nothing for thirty years. From 1910 it pays three percent of its value each year into the budget for infrastructure, the Polytechnic and the research institutes. At home its lending arm, the Development Fund, lends only beside private capital that takes the first loss, except to the state's own estates. Ordinary surpluses go to a crisis reserve and then to lower taxes. A state savings bank and, from 1919, the Previsión, a voluntary account deductible up to a yearly limit, gather the households' savings and lend them at market rates. The state keeps a standing stock of public debt as the Reserve Bank's collateral and the savers' safe asset; the Bank lends against it and holds no more of it than its own capital. The model's five-percent-of-GDP floor and three-percent reserve buffer are planning estimates.

<!-- CR-073: the Patrimony's first chairman --> **[APPROVED — CR-073]** Elias makes Ralston the first chairman of the Patrimony in 1880, over Collis Huntington, the one man in the field with an organisation as large as Ralston's. Ralston keeps his shares in the Bank of California and gives up its direction to William Alvord. Every private syndicate that takes the first loss beside the Development Fund now comes to him, and every loss he writes off is printed. **[PROPOSED — CR-073]** He sits out every syndicate his own bank leads. The Bonanza men and the railway men say the Fund follows the Bank of California's syndicates, and the printed accounts are his answer.

The champions grow on their own profits and the savings bank's loans, and on Polytechnic patents any of them may license. The state holds the shares of its own estates and the rights of way, and from 1889 the canal.<!-- CR-039: the state's shares, not the Crown's --> Abroad the champions build with partners: Japanese and Chinese merchant houses and Gulf and Mexican investors take about a quarter of each venture. Before 1914 the champions sell bonds in London, Paris and Amsterdam, and in the 1920s on Wall Street and in Tokyo. Like the Union before 1914, Pacifica becomes a creditor through its Treasury and partly a debtor through its companies, and foreigners draw a growing income from its success. **[MODEL — CR-025]** By 1929 foreign holders own about $3.8bn of claims on the champions and draw about $280m a year from them.

The Panama Canal draws imperial labor and capital into dangerous tropical works. Engineers encounter disease, unstable ground, tropical rainfall, strikes and political resistance from neighboring states. Pacifica secures Colombian transit and neutrality rights, first refusal on the concession and control of the Panama Railroad in 1880. France begins the main excavation and bears its early disease and design failures. The regency later purchases the failed enterprise in 1889 and completes a lock-and-lake canal in 1900. Early Pacifican commitments concern the railway, treaty and technical participation, with the main construction burden falling after the purchase.

## 20. Kaʻanoe Ward and Julian's birth (1881–1887)

Kaʻanoe Ward, known as Noe, is born about 1860 in Lahaina to a settled New England ship's surgeon and a woman of a minor chiefly family tracing its Maui descent to Kahekili. During Honolulu's 1881 smallpox epidemic she nurses at the Queen's Hospital. **[APPROVED — CR-027]** The hospital is Queen Emma's foundation, and Noe's first patrons are loyalists. She passes the Polytechnic's medical examination in San Francisco in 1884, the first Hawaiian and one of the first women to qualify, and becomes physician to the failing founder in 1885.

Clinical, unsentimental and angry at waste, she tells Elias the truth about the body behind his legend. Her gallows humor pleases him, and her figures about the dying Hawaiian population command the attention that ministers struggle to obtain. Their relationship is real and clear-eyed. In 1886 his decree establishes the Hawaiian Board of Public Health, with money, quarantine powers and Noe as medical director. She builds district birth-and-death registers and begins vaccination in Honolulu and Maui; the unfinished work is her achievement, not a miraculous reversal of mortality in three years.

Julian is born in 1886. Noe agrees to his hānai adoption by Victoria, with Elias's written consent. Noe bargains for the health board, a place in the boy's life and Hawaiian upbringing; Victoria requires that he be raised as her son and successor. The two women respect and fear each other. One controls access to Elias's body, the other to the state. Noe hopes the Hawaiian people will stop dying and her son will sit on a restored Hawaiian throne; she lives to see neither.

## 21. The Korean settlement, the 124 counties and the death of the founder (1887)

The Port Hamilton settlement and the Chinese Customs appointment display an empire increasingly powerful through guarantees, finance and standards. The founder's final years also carry approved state programmes for fuel oil from 1876 and steam turbines from about 1885. Early oil finds, generator trials and later naval delivery dates are engineering targets to test. Tesla's 1884 arrival and the electrical institute established in 1886 connect Sierra hydropower, one AC standard, mining, irrigation, cities and future Asian equipment sales. Soberanes develops the professional administration and communications while foreign adventures pause around the succession. **[PROPOSED]** During Elias’s decline in 1883–1887 Victoria increasingly governs in practice; Keelung, the Customs appointment and Port Hamilton establish her record before she becomes regent.

<!-- CR-071: the Feather River --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The irrigation counties have seen what a confederal title is worth. In February 1886 a night mob on the Feather burns the packing sheds of Chinese families who bought their district farms from the Land Fund, and puts the families on a river barge. The district holds its water by confederal grant, so the ringleaders are tried before the Confederal Court, not by a county jury, and convicted. The district restores the families to their land, damages are levied on the rioters' property, and the followers who pay their share are amnestied. **[PROPOSED — CR-071]** No one is killed. <!-- CR-071: the triggers --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** Two events of 1885–86 make the constitution urgent while the founder lives. In 1885 California's legislature resolves against a war with Britain over Port Hamilton; the resolution has no force and is printed in London. In the water decision of October 1886 the Confederal Court, on Henry Miller's appeal in his riparian suit against a confederal district on the Kern, holds that a confederal grant does not extinguish a private water right held under a member's law, and every district in California now holds its water only until the next suit. The court that convicted the Feather rioters cannot protect the settlers' own rivers. The Party runs on the decision in every irrigation county and in November narrowly takes California's legislature from the Committee party. Romualdo Pacheco, home from Manila, is chosen President of California.

The Constitution of the Realm of 1887 abolishes the states and draws 124 counties across their old boundaries, dividing Deseret among several of them. California and Hawaii remain historic lands with entrenched language and tenure guarantees, not sovereign members with vetoes. Thalassia is the capital, the Court of the Realm sits at Monterey and the Polytechnic is chartered at Carquinez. A regency article names Victoria, Queen of Hawaii, Regent of the Realm until an Act of Settlement establishes the Crown, and Julian Heir of the Realm. <!-- CR-071: the crown declined --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** In December 1886 Elias's draft makes Victoria Queen of the Realm. She strikes the article and has Soberanes draft the regency in its place. A crown now, she tells him, would choose between his sons while he is dying, and the members' men took a life Providentor, not a king. She does not say that the Confederation still carries the ordinances that stretched her lease, or that power held for a crown not yet settled is the older Hawaiian form. Elias's life office has no successor; he lays it down when the regency begins.

The constitution follows amendment procedure in form, with two-thirds of both chambers and founding-state ratification. Grants buy interior legislators; Hawaii's assent comes from the appointed Territorial Council created by the illegal ordinances of 1874–1876. Legal form does not restore the consent that made the original partnership. Hawaiian loyalists reject it.

<!-- CR-072: the Party carries 1887; Judah's map; Deseret's Aufhebung --> **[APPROVED — CR-072]** The Prosperity Party carries the two-thirds of both chambers, California's ratification and the interior legislatures, and keeps the bought votes bought until ratification. The lines are Theodore Judah's: his survey draws the 124 counties. Deseret is the one member the Party has never won, and the Church fears for more than its state. Deseret's marriage and church-property law was protected as the law of a member state, and the member states are ending. The Constitution carries the protection over to persons. A marriage made in the form of a religious community registered with the Realm is valid in every county, and church property is held as corporate property. Plural marriage is among those forms. The guarantee covers every registered church and congregation, and the Realm's floors bind every marriage of every form: no bar of race or origin, the free consent of each party before a registrar, the ages of marriage and a limit on the gap between the spouses' ages, registration, and the right of any spouse to leave with a share of property and support for the children. John Taylor, who leads the Church, does not trust a guarantee from a Realm that is abolishing his state. He resists until his death on 25 July 1887, and George Q. Cannon, negotiating for the Twelve, then brings the Church in. Its People's Party becomes the Prosperity Party's Deseret caucus: the wards are the Party's precincts in the Deseret counties, and their senators sit as a bloc.

<!-- CR-071: the ratifications --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** Pacheco settles California's terms with the Regent-designate at Thalassia in the winter of 1886–87, on the island where he went to school. He takes home the historic-land clause, Spanish entrenched, community property kept and the Mexican grants as organic law, and he knowingly signs away the power through which California could have excluded the Chinese. The bill goes to the confederal chambers in January 1887 and passes by two-thirds in April. In May Reginaldo del Valle, born in the Republic, carries it through California's senate, where a riparian amendment fails by one vote and the ratification passes by three. Ralston brings the San Francisco houses to give their word for it; the Patrimony spends nothing. Every charter of 1866 carries Elias's article of supersession, so the Constitution enters into force on 1 August 1887 whatever Salt Lake does. Deseret's acceptance, after Taylor's death, is its legislature's last act, and Cannon's telegram reaches Thalassia two days before the handover. Elias reads it himself.

<!-- CR-071: the settlers' price --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The settlers of the interior are paid as well, in rules they can see kept. The Constitution's labour article holds four rules: no labour bound by debt, penal contract or a broker's ticket; the same wage for the same work, whoever does it; a day of rest in seven, on the day of the worker's faith; and the eight-hour day on the Realm's works and in its chartered undertakings. A convention of the regency answers the settlers' fear of being policed by strangers: each people is kept in order by its own, the interior by the inland command and the counties' reserves and Hawaii by the Queen's Guard, never by the naval party. The promise of a law for the companies goes with the article: a school, land for service and treaties for service abroad. Silas says nothing through the campaign.

Residual powers belong to the Realm; counties administer enumerated local work. The center controls credit, borrowing, immigration, armed force, networks and the general criminal and commercial code. County boards retain land taxes, roads, sanitation, primary schools and ordinary policing, with appointed judges and central standards. A Commissioner from the examined service executes the Realm's law and commands the reserve. He never serves in his home county, rotates at least every five years and may suspend a county act for thirty days for the Court's review. No county may borrow abroad; domestic borrowing needs Treasury consent.

<!-- CR-038: senators held to the age of sixty-eight -->
County boards choose one senator each. Appointments held to the age of sixty-eight form up to a quarter of the Senate, with twelve Polytechnic seats and eight aliʻi seats. The elected lower house becomes the Chamber of Deputies. The Court's constitutional chamber hears disputes; the Polytechnic's examinations, audit, General Staff and telegraph form a chartered estate with its own continuity. Its chief is chosen by the ruler from three graduate-council nominees and removed only with Senate consent. The Office of Public Measures, established in 1888, supplies the other independent estate, with twelve-year heads reporting to the Senate. The ruler appoints and dismisses ministers; they answer to the ruler, not the Chamber.

<!-- CR-071: the handover --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** Twice a constitution of members has stood in Elias's way: in 1860 it stopped him, when Hawaii withheld its assent to Washoe, and in 1864 the vote of twelve to six kept him by a single Californian. It could stop anyone after him, and the examined men he has trained since 1879 still serve under governors the members elect. On 1 August 1887 the regency begins at Thalassia, and the states end with it. Elias, too weak to stand long, watches Victoria take the oath; no one records what he says. He keeps the title of Providentor as a style, with no office and no seat. Her first act as Regent is to sign the commissions of the 124 county Commissioners from the list he approved; they take up their counties in 1888, when the first boards are elected. Silas, Kiyoshi and Soberanes take their commissions from her hand and sit for the first time in the Regency Council. A week later Silas takes the oath again at Sacramento, before the inland command's officers.

<!-- CR-071: the members' men, the Senate's other seats, Alaska and the homelands --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** With the regency the member offices end. The governors who hold the confederal commission that day, and Pacheco, receive appointed Senate seats held to sixty-eight. The member legislatures are dissolved, and their men may stand for the new county boards. California's capitol at Sacramento becomes the inland command's headquarters, Salem's a county hall and Olympia's the courthouse of the Court of the Realm's Columbia circuit. Thalassia, governed by the Crown without a board, chooses no senator. Each homeland of a million acres or more chooses one through its own council, as does each league of smaller homelands that reaches that size, and Alaska's nations choose one. Alaska stays a territory under the Northern Commission, outside the 124, and Baja's two counties replace its commission. The homelands are not counties. A homeland of a million acres or more stands outside the 124, and a smaller one lies inside a county's lines beyond its board's reach. Their compacts run to the Realm and change only with the nation's consent. A member of a nation becomes a citizen by entering himself on the register, and votes where he lives; the miners and townsmen on ground a nation leases, which lies in no county, are on the roll of the county beside it.

In August 1887 Elias dies. The news passes through the telegraph system he built. A son from his marriage to Victoria would have presented a widely recognized dynastic possibility, but that son is dead. Other claimants possess blood ties, military supporters, or control over parts of the state, yet no uncontested legitimacy. Victoria is both the widow of the founder and the reigning Queen of Hawaii. Julian is already Victoria’s adopted son and the intended successor, while his biological mother is still living. The negotiated pact holds; the empire consolidates without a succession war.

---

# PART V — Regency, the Japanese partnership and Julian’s succession (1887–1907)

## 22. The managed succession and the Donner Pass crisis (1886–1887)

Elias’s decline brings three adult power centres into negotiation. Silas, Elias's eldest son, leads the American military aristocrats, drawing on Confederate association, Auxiliary careers and Southern works. Kiyoshi, his second son by a Satsuma mother, leads the Japanese naval party. A decorated commander unrelated to the Vances leads the Polytechnic officers and communications establishment; Rafael Soberanes leads this estate from Carquinez. Each faction can block the others, but none can obtain power without an unaffordable civil war. Victoria’s adopted infant son is their acceptable successor, and she becomes regent without a war of inheritance.

**[APPROVED — CR-039]** Negotiations take place in Hawaii during 1886–1887 and the Truce of Thalassia is signed in spring 1887 while Elias lives. **[PROPOSED]** Victoria’s standing combines her marriage to the founder, and from August his widowhood, her reigning Hawaiian crown, Hawaiian aliʻi authority, control of harbours and her recent diplomatic record. The 1887 regency article supplies her authority despite the continuing legal wrong of 1874. Soberanes chooses institutions over a strongman; Sherman supports constitutional military allegiance rather than personal rule. **[PROPOSED]** Sherman dies in February 1891.

The Truce is extra-legal before its terms become organic law. Silas's inland command sits at Sacramento, Kiyoshi's naval party at Pearl Harbor, Soberanes's Polytechnic at Carquinez; Victoria holds Thalassia and Honolulu. The named holders keep their offices for life, after which the Regent appoints with Senate consent. San Francisco holds mint, reserve, syndicates and champion headquarters but no government; Monterey holds the Court and Californio legitimacy. No single city can capture all the state’s functions, and the factions meet through the Crown. Los Angeles becomes a Crown city in 1910 for oil and film; Portland, Seattle and Salt Lake remain counties, not Crown cities, with the Church steering a coherent Deseret Senate bloc. **[APPROVED — CR-072]** The bloc is the Prosperity Party's Deseret caucus, which the Church steers from inside the Party.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** One armed standoff at Donner Pass in October 1887 tests the pact. The Polytechnic corps comes up to take over the Sierra telegraph under its charter; Silas's veterans hold the summit as the inland command's line. Caleb brokers a parley in the telegraph hut inside the snowshed. Before it, Abigail has told her son that Soberanes must not come down from that pass. When Soberanes says that Elias's last instruction to him was that the wire must not be divided, Silas draws and fires at him. Caleb steps across and takes the bullet. Dying in Silas's arms, he says: "Don't give it." Silas's officers wait for the order to advance, and he does not give it. Soberanes dictates the first dispatch: shot from outside the shed. The inquest finds a person unknown. Every man in the room is bound by the lie, and Silas is praised for the restraint of a man who fired. There is no campaign, general mutiny or civil war. Caleb's sacrifice remains the working implementation of the managed-succession direction.

## 23. Noe's death, the registers and the heir (1887–1889)

After Elias dies in August 1887, Noe retains a household, a mother's claim and her health work. As natural guardian she could contest the regency; Hawaiian loyalists who reject the ordinances begin to court her. **[APPROVED — CR-027]** With Emma dead since 1885 they also look to her cousin and heir Albert Kūnuiākea, by common knowledge a natural son of Kamehameha III, never acknowledged and the squanderer of his inheritance. Her rivalry with Victoria concerns the boy and Hawaii's future, not a simple contest for the dying man's affection.

She dies in the autumn of 1888 during a typhoid outbreak she is fighting. That is the official account, certified by Victoria's physician. Her servants swear she was poisoned. The registers disappear for a month and return in the new Office of Public Measures. Loyalists call it murder, and Silas's people spread the story as part of "the Regent's year": Caleb, the Donner telegraphist burned in his hut that January, and now Noe. Silas suspects his own mother; his mother suspects him. Neither asks. Victoria, the factional rivals and those who need a martyr have motives; the Chronicler can prove none guilty.

<!-- CR-071: the Honolulu rising of 1889 --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** On 30 July 1889, the eve of Lā Hoʻihoʻi Ea, Robert Wilcox, home from the military academy at Turin, occupies the grounds of Aliʻiōlani Hale with a hundred-odd men. Rising in the Queen's name, he demands that the Territorial Council be dissolved and the kingdom's legislature restored. Kiyoshi's naval party offers marines and the Commissioner for Honolulu offers the reserve; the Regent refuses both, because each people is kept in order by its own. She sends the Queen's Guard over the Pali under Samuel Nowlein, with Kalākaua answerable for its loyalty, and her terms ahead of it. Wilcox refuses them. The Guard takes the grounds that afternoon and seven of the rising are killed. The Regent rides into Honolulu that night. The kingdom's Supreme Court tries Wilcox for treason, and a Hawaiian jury acquits him in 1890; the Regent accepts the verdict and says that the Act that establishes the Crown will end the Council. The loyalists sing of the seven; the Realm's press notes that no marine landed. Wilcox dies in 1903, before the union is put to the Hawaiian people.

Victoria builds public legitimacy on Noe's honest figures. She completes vaccination across the islands in 1888–1892, brings Hawaiian-speaking nurses and clean water to plantation districts in the 1890s and reforms Kalaupapa. By about 1895 the population curve turns upward by Noe's method. Infant mortality becomes the state's sacred number, later carried into county league tables and the Gulf admission test. Care, rivalry with a dead woman and possible guilt remain inseparable to the observer.

Julian grows up speaking Hawaiian with Victoria, English with tutors, Spanish among Soberanes's circle and Japanese with the naval party. He later finds Noe's registers in the Office's archive. His two mothers have given the same state its measurements and its political uses; his later falsification in 1921 corrupts an inheritance more intimate than an administrative rule.

## 24. The Truce, the Japanese compact and the regency council (1887–1889)

The Truce supplies a governing balance through the life offices and Regency Council. The restored Chancellor is appointed by the Regent with Senate consent. Elias's enabling power lapses; Article XIV remains the emergency route. Victoria governs as Regent of a Realm whose crown has yet to be established, rather than as guardian obliged to surrender office on the boy's majority.

<!-- CR-071: the interim --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** Until the first Senate and Chamber sit in 1888, the Confederal Council and Assembly sit on as the Realm's chambers under the Regent, and the old county boards carry on in their old counties. The Confederal Council consents to the Chancellor the Regent appoints, and the Senate confirms him at its first sitting. The interim chambers' first act, early in 1888, is the Franchise Law; the first county boards are elected on it in the spring and choose the first Senate. **[OPEN]** The first Chancellor.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** The regency's working government sits in the chartered estates. The Constitution gives the Polytechnic its examinations, audit, General Staff and telegraph and the Office of Public Measures the Realm's numbers; the Crown holds the state's shares in its own estates, the trunk rights of way and, from 1889, the canal, and the Patrimony receives the rents. Five people at the head of the Polytechnic and the chartered estates run the technocratic state. In 1887 they are Soberanes for the Polytechnic and General Staff, Logan Turner for naval construction and heavy industry, Tesla for power, **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** William Francis Hillebrand for the Light and Matter Works, the atomic estate, <!-- CR-079: the press seat --> **[APPROVED — CR-079]** and William Randolph Hearst, San Francisco-born, who takes over the *Examiner* that year and makes the six-language weekly the Realm's press; from 1889–90 Louis Le Prince's camera gives it projected film, and from 1895 W. K. L. Dickson, recruited from Edison, runs its film unit. **[PROPOSED — CR-027]** Seats pass to successors such as Baekeland, Doheny, Giannini and Montgomery, and after 1915 to Marcus Vance III; there are always five. San Francisco calls them the Five, Honolulu nā Kahuna, the priests and masters of their crafts. <!-- CR-052: Ryan in the power seat -->**[APPROVED — CR-052]** In 1924, when Tesla reaches sixty-eight, the power seat passes to Harris J. Ryan, raised in Pacifica and examined at the Polytechnic, the line-builder of the Realm's long transmission. <!-- CR-058-R1: Doheny in Turner's seat -->**[APPROVED — CR-058-R1]** About 1907, when Logan Turner reaches sixty-eight, the champions' seat passes to Edward L. Doheny, the oilman who has spent a decade in the Senate attacking the trust; he holds it until he reaches sixty-eight in 1924. **[APPROVED — CR-079]** When Hearst reaches sixty-eight in April 1931, Julian gives the press seat to Walter Lippmann with the Senate's consent (§35).

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** Their arrangement with the Crown is never written down. The Crown has no army beyond the Queen's Guard and no purse of its own; force, money, communications and the numbers sit in the estates. The Five have power but no legitimacy; Japan, the creditors, the Senate and the Hawaiian people accept them because the Crown sanctions them. The Crown does not touch their charters, appointments or the Patrimony; the estates never take sides against the Crown and never let a faction capture them. Victoria is the one person all five trust, because she shields their charters from the Senate and gives them prestige. Hearst wants spectacle and she wants honest numbers with the weak spots shown; he may dramatize the Union's failures, but he prints the Office's tables unaltered.

<!-- CR-049: the atomic estate among the Five; Hollerith at the Office -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** The atomic estate sits among the Five because the atom's great results come decades before anyone can use them, and with Hillebrand Honolulu's *nā Kahuna* has a native son among them. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** Herman Hollerith's punched cards tabulate Noe's registers from 1888 at the Office of Public Measures, whose heads report to the Senate and which has no seat among the Five. Peirce, nearly fifty, wants the registers' work for his relays and loses it to the younger man's cards, and the Calculation Office ends up borrowing the method that beat it.

<!-- CR-077: the algebra, the card and the exchange --> **[APPROVED — CR-077]** Peirce has already published the Office's circuit algebra in 1886: relays in series and in parallel, and the one operation, neither–nor, from which all the others can be built. It is the first course in the Office's school, and in 1887 he asks in print whether a logical machine reasons. His answer to losing the registers is the card. The Office and the Office of Public Measures agree one card, the Realm card, in 1890, and by 1893 the Office's relay calculators read their numbers, and then their orders, from cards. The Realm's telephone exchanges are designed with the same algebra. San Francisco dials in 1893, and by about 1910 most of the Realm's telephones dial. <!-- CR-078: who owns the wire --> **[APPROVED — CR-078]** In 1876 Bell files in the Confederation as well as at home, and in 1877 the Confederation's state telegraph buys the Confederation's rights outright, on Elias's instruction that the Republic should own any wire that may become a network, and licenses them at one published royalty; the transmitter is Hughes's unpatented microphone. In February 1878 the city and county of San Francisco lets the first exchange concession under California's law, with connection to every other line at a published rate. From 1887 the Polytechnic's telegraph estate owns the long lines, the cables and the Realm's switching standard as monopolies the state owns, and the exchanges stay county concessions, relet every twenty years after 1888 with the Office of Public Measures' service figures, the Realm's standard and a rule that a losing incumbent's plant passes to the winner at a published valuation. A homeland that wants a line lets its own concession. San Francisco reaches Los Angeles on heavy copper wire in 1894, lines are loaded from about 1900, valve repeaters are tried on chosen circuits in 1903–05, and from 1909–10 San Francisco and Monterey talk to Salt Lake, Puget Sound and Santa Fe; by 1929 there are about 25 telephones for every 100 people **[MODEL]**. From the 1880s the telephone takes the town messages from the Morse telegraphers and the messenger boys. **[APPROVED — CR-077]** The dial puts thousands of operators, most of them women, out of work. In 1898 the trades congress carries their complaint to the Senate's committee, and the answer comes from the Office: its school examines former operators for the computing rooms, and the county concessionaires pay a year's wage to those who leave. In 1896 Hollerith leaves the Office of Public Measures and founds a tabulating company in San Francisco for the railways, the insurers, the Reserve Bank and the county halls. The Genealogical Society of Utah, founded in 1894, puts its records on the Office's cards from the start and becomes the company's customer in 1896, and the Saints become the Realm's second-largest keepers of records.

<!-- CR-035: the Polytechnic examines its rivals -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-035]** The arrangement is tested in 1888, when Soberanes's examiners fail half of Santa Clara's engineers and a third of Oakland's. The rectors appeal to Victoria against the pass lists. She will not touch the estate's examinations, and they go back to Carquinez to bargain with the power that taught the Republic to examine the churches' colleges and is now their rival.

Kiyoshi has entered Kuroda Kiyotaka’s house in 1886 as an adopted son-in-law, marrying into it and taking the name Kuroda Kiyoshi. Japan gives him the name Elias withheld. His family position binds the naval party to a recognized Japanese house. Lady Chiyo belongs to another branch of that house; **[APPROVED — CR-026]** Kiyotaka's own son Kiyonaka remains heir of the main house. Kiyoshi's bride is Kiyotaka's adopted daughter Hyaku. After the victory of 1905 Kiyoshi founds his own branch house and is made Count Kuroda in 1907; his son will inherit it.

**[PROPOSED]** A compact with Tokyo is signed alongside the Truce. Japan backs the regency and holds its officers to the agreement; Pacifica guarantees Japan against Russia and supports its treaty revision with Europe. The western squadron’s command is reserved to officers of the Japanese naval party. Both states ratify renewals every ten years, in 1897, 1907 and 1917. Japan’s crown, domestic government and army remain Japanese. Examination, service and imperial careers gradually give Japanese-origin officers and financiers a stake in institutions seated in Pacifica. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** The compact runs until its fourth renewal falls due in 1927, when the Pacific Concord takes its place.

Victoria governs through bargaining. Elias’s enabling-law delegation lapses at the beginning of the regency, reassuring the factions while limiting her instruments. **[APPROVED — CR-022]** The 1888 restriction is presented as Silas's price, but it is what Victoria wanted, and she holds the Donner report. He takes the blame. In the Bread March of 1894 his border command detains the marchers, and he insists that they be fed and tried, not shot. Pacifica consolidates through 1887–1889, with foreign military adventures paused around the succession. The compact begins a relationship whose later Concord will depend on Japanese consent and on different understandings of who has gained command.

## 25. The regency's measures, continental settlement and Asian guarantees (1887–1902)

Victoria and Soberanes consolidate through examinations, finance and published comparisons. The 1888 Franchise Law gives each adult citizen one ordinary vote, a second for the civil examination and a third for a degree, commission or Polytechnic diploma. Each certified language beyond English on the Crown’s list adds a tongue vote. Tongue votes are uncapped. Referendums remain restricted to examined citizens. The Office of Public Measures builds on Noe's registers, and its county league tables reach the public through the Five's press: the six-language weekly, images, film and study tours. Victoria insists on honest numbers and Pacifica's weak spots appearing alongside its gains.

<!-- CR-071: the first Commissioners --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The first Commissioners arrive in 1888 from the examined men of 1879–87, each outside his home county and posted by the record of his works, not his marks. On the Rio Grande a Californio Commissioner hears complaints in the plaza as the alcaldes did and publishes the accounts in both languages, and in his third year the county's school passes are the best in the old New Mexico. At Hilo a Commissioner who has passed the Hawaiian examination speaks English to the Queen's people and takes their petitions through an interpreter, and Victoria recalls him early in 1889. Ordinary people notice less: the sheriff stays, the school examiner is a stranger, the courthouse changes its seal, and the tax bill loses the member's line. In Oregon the exclusion law has lapsed with the member, and the first Black family to buy a Willamette farm in 1888 does so without anyone saying why it can. **[OPEN]** The Commissioners' names.

<!-- CR-071: the labour law, the settlers' arenas and the Companies Act --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The labour law of 1888 fills in the article: county wage boards on the Realm's benchmarks, and the working day outside the Realm's works. Every child is in school until fourteen, on a family farm as anywhere else. The Land Fund sells no household more than a family farm, a rule that binds the great buyers as much as anyone. The Development Fund lends to the districts' co-operatives, so that a settler can meet a harder-working neighbour with machines rather than hours, and the Polytechnic's farm stations teach the high-value crops to all who come. The settler's sons win their honour elsewhere too: the militia's commission, which carries the third vote; the counties' contest for the excellence of their works; and the counties' festivals of marksmen and riders. <!-- CR-074: the Companies Act --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** The Companies Act of 1888 makes the companies a system. No company serves abroad except under a treaty of service with a recognised government, laid before the chambers, and never in the Americas, against a partner or for a pretender. Its school at Sacramento, under the inland command's officers, admits by trial of body and nerve and teaches every cadet the tongue of his station. The host pays at no less than the Realm's rate, and half the pay waits at the Land Fund with interest until discharge. Five years served earn a warrant for forty irrigated acres or eighty dry in the interior's districts, and ten years a whole farm, the family farm of eighty irrigated acres or a hundred and sixty dry that the labour law allows a household. A company's commission, once the Commissions Board has confirmed it, carries the third vote. The article from Suzhou stands in the Act: no company takes part in killing prisoners or serves a commander who does, and a captain may take his company out. The men call themselves privateers, a name the Companies Office deplores.

<!-- CR-074: raised companies, the Philippine companies and the roll --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** Beside the open companies stand raised companies, chartered to any body of citizens that can fill one: the Chiricahua and the Ute, the Saints of Deseret, the settlers of the irrigation districts. Each chooses its own people, language, colours and honour code, and none may hold its arms at home, commission its own officers or preach under arms. The Office's appointment tables do not count whom a raised company takes, since it rules no one at home. A nation's council consents to each posting, and its men's warrants are paid in land joined to the homeland. In the Philippines the Philippine Council charters companies of the islands' men, whose warrants are paid in the friar land. The Act's roll of deeds begins with the dead of Olompali, and its preamble claims two companies as the first: the Republic's men who fought there in June 1846 and the Coast Miwok who held their own ground beside them.

<!-- CR-074: Korea's guard and the Customs --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** In 1888 Korea becomes the first partner to sign a treaty of service under the new Companies Act: a Pacific Company drills the king's guard at Seoul in Korean words of command, paid by the Korean treasury through the Companies Office. That autumn China turns the Customs' preventive service into the second.

County autonomy is earned. Five consecutive years at the Office's four standards—infant mortality, examination passes, revenue per head and order—bring chartered status; three years below or declared disorder permit receivership. A board can be suspended for up to five years, renewable by the Senate. Measures grants follow results. Every Realm programme lapses after ten years unless the Office certifies it. County powers, boundaries and grades are organic law, amendable by both chambers without local consent. Victoria usually obtains obedience through prestige and comparison; Julian later uses commissions and ordinances more freely, giving county oligarchies reason to fight through their senators.

<!-- CR-072: the regency's pillar; one party and its friends; Broderick's death --> **[APPROVED — CR-072]** The Prosperity Party outlives the founder and becomes a pillar of the regency. It goes on printing its own ticket, and from 1888 it publishes its accounts with the Office's other numbers: who paid, what was spent, in which county. **[PROPOSED — CR-072]** Its slates run on the league tables in every board election, which is why the Regent seldom needs receivership. The tables are true, weak spots included; the Party chooses which true numbers to make loud. **[APPROVED — CR-072]** It absorbs or neutralises its competitors, and by about 1888 there is no second party that could win the boards or the Senate. The member parties lose their arena with the member legislatures. The Committee party's money is bought before its men: the San Francisco houses have subscribed to the Party since 1875, and in 1888 the Party wins San Francisco's board with the houses' men on its ticket. What is left of the Committee party is a reform club of merchants and editors who publish the Office's tables against the Party's men and lose every election they contest. Opponents may stand, print their own tickets and lose; real politics is fought inside the Party. The Hawaiian loyalists who reject the ordinances of 1874 are the exception: they contest the state itself, not its boards, and stay outside it. In 1888 Broderick reads the Party's first published accounts, a machine paid for from above set out county by county, and he dies in San Francisco in January 1889, at sixty-eight. The old machine turns out for him one last time, the examined cadre runs the funeral like an operation, and Ralston brings the city's banks.

<!-- CR-037: appointment tables and the 1890 audit -->
**[APPROVED — CR-037]** The Office also counts who gets the posts. From 1888 it publishes, for every chartered body, college and licensed profession, how appointments and promotions compare with the examination lists, and a gap that lasts brings in the Office's auditors. The first great audit, in 1890, falls on the board of the state savings bank in San Francisco. Its seats have passed for a generation among a few intermarried families, Yankee, Californio and Irish, and the examined clerks below them never rise. The auditors void three appointments and the families appeal to the Regent. Victoria will not touch the audit, and she refuses the newspapers' demand for quotas: the measure is the examination, not the mix. From then on officials declare their relatives and former students.

<!-- CR-037: the county rule and its limits -->
**[APPROVED — CR-037]** From 1890 each county's appointments are measured against its own pass lists, so a New Mexican county may be run mostly by Spanish speakers and a Hawaiian one by Hawaiians. A county loses marks for order when any group of examined candidates is appointed at under two-thirds the rate of the best-placed group, or when a single family, marriage network or congregation holds more than a third of its senior posts. Every county's treasurer, auditor, examiner and chief of police is posted from outside by the Office, in rotation, as the Commissioner is, even in chartered counties. The rules catch blood and faith better than school ties, and whoever calls an audit holds a weapon.

<!-- CR-071: the first receivership and the first charter --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The first receivership is of an owners' board. In July 1892 the Regent declares a disorder in Coeur d'Alene, where the owners' lockout and their guards' rifles have met the union's dynamite. The board is suspended and a commission enforces the labour code against both sides: it tries the men who blew up the Frisco mill and the guards who fired into the crowd, and hands the county back in 1895. Salt Lake is the first county to qualify for a charter, in 1893; the people most suspicious of the centre top its tables. The charter waits a year on the inspectors' test that no congregation may capture a county, and the Church's men on the board resign their Church offices to keep it. Salt Lake is chartered in 1894.

<!-- CR-038: retirement at 68, Vallejo College and its two sides -->
**[APPROVED — CR-038]** Victoria and Soberanes make the machinery guard itself. The regency extends the Constitution's age of sixty-eight for appointed senators to every senator, judge, Commissioner, estate head and senior officer, except the Truce's named holders. From 1889 the government offices left empty at Monterey when the seat moved to Thalassia house Vallejo College, beside the Court. Rising officers of the Polytechnic, the Office, the navy, the champions and the counties, women and men, spend a year there. Their rooms are assigned by lot across origins; their evenings are not. The engineers and managers take over Monterey's old Presidio barracks with their rowing and climbing clubs; <!-- CR-076: CT1 --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** the barracks drinks on the night a race is won or a route climbed, when the losers pour. **[APPROVED — CR-038]** Many of the women of the hospitals, schools and Office gather in the house they name after Victoria's Kaʻahumanu Society. A woman surveyor rows with the barracks crew; Polytechnic men sit at the House's lectures on sanitation. The barracks crowd of the 1890s will later be called the Carquinez men. **[APPROVED — CR-072]** They are the Prosperity Party's cadre. Many of the women of the House are the estates' inspectors and statisticians: the Party runs on the numbers they count, and the half of the machine that wins the boards is audited by the half that counts.

<!-- CR-073: the Patrimony's chairman retires --> **[APPROVED — CR-073]** The charters of 1887–88 recharter the Patrimony with the estates and bring its chairman under the age of sixty-eight. Ralston turns sixty-eight in 1894 and retires from the Patrimony. He asks the Regent to set the rule aside for him, and she answers that the charters are not the Crown's to touch.

<!-- CR-038: inspectors, hard numbers, scholarships, official pay, county halls, results-based pride -->
**[APPROVED — CR-038]** The Office's inspectors arrive in a county without warning and check its returns against what is hard to fake: freight tonnage, coal and power, savings deposits and burials. They publish what they find. From 1888 a Realm scholarship binds its holder to five years' work in Pacifica, and a posted grant to five years in the county surgery, survey camp or island service it was made for. Each year the Senate prints senior officials' pay beside what the champions pay their managers. In the 1890s every county opens a hall for all its residents, with sick and burial funds, interpreters, night classes, notices of work and the county's tables on the wall beside the Realm's. An Office inspector holds it to the Office's minimum of services; above that it keeps its own language, festivals and character. In 1897 Fresno's hall rings its bell for a week when the county first meets all four standards. Pacificans learn to be proud of what the tables show.

<!-- CR-076: the Spirits Act of 1888 and the halls --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** In 1888, the year of the labour law, the Realm's chambers pass the Spirits Act. The Spirit Stores become universal for spirits and smoking opium sold to take away, and beer and wine stay in private hands under licence; nothing is sold to anyone under eighteen. In Hawaii the Territorial Council has no hand in them: the Queen gives Hawaii's stores to the Board of Public Health, and its duty to the board's work. A licensed smoking room keeps a public house's hours and liability, county police and inspectors enforce the licence, and the Chinese district associations are consulted. Laudanum, morphine and later cocaine are dispensed only on a physician's order. No one is jailed for being drunk: he sleeps it off in the sobering room and pays a fine scaled to a day's wage, and a court may put a man who comes back too often on the county's roll, which forbids any store to sell to him for a term. Drink is never sold on credit, wages are never paid in it, and a crime done in drink is punished as if sober. The duty and the stores' surplus go by law to the county surgeries and the halls. Every hall is dry on ordinary nights and open late, with a coffee counter, and a county may vote the cup in on its own festival nights. From 1888 the label carries the Office's figures on the liver, the nerves and the memory, and from 1900 a warning against drink in pregnancy. The saloon is not abolished. In most counties it loses its customers to the hall, and the Office's tables show the counties where it does not. The duty is set at about five parts in six of the Stores' price for standard whiskey, near the top of what it can raise, and the Office resets it every five years from its tables of the rate against what is taxed; nothing over sixty degrees is sold for drinking. Drink carried home across any line pays the full duty, a person on the roll who comes back drunk across a line is held in the sobering room at the port of entry, and no company man under contract takes drinking leave abroad.

<!-- CR-076: across the lines; Guaymas --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** Those who want drink without the rules cross a line: to Mexico's border towns at Nogales, Agua Prieta and Palomas, to Vancouver until British Columbia goes dry in 1917, and to the ports abroad. In the 1920s a casino resort on the Sonoran shore at Guaymas, under Mexico's law, is where Los Angeles's film money and the Realm's rich go to drink without the evening hour, and what they do there stays out of the county tables.

<!-- CR-076: coffee --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** Coffee is the working day's drink. San Francisco's roasting houses, Folger's Pioneer Mills of 1850 among them, supply the coffee rooms, the halls' counters, the ships' watches and the Companies' kits, and Hills Bros. packs it in vacuum tins from 1900. Japanese students who meet in the coffee rooms of San Francisco and Honolulu take the habit home. Kona's smallholders, most of them Japanese tenant families by 1930, grow the best beans under the Pacific crowns, with a co-operative mill on Development Fund credit. When the leaf rust reaches Batangas in 1889, the Philippine government's quarantine, copper spraying and replanting with Liberica and highland Arabica keep the industry alive, though Batangas loses most of its old trees. In 1901 Satori Kato, a Japanese chemist on a Polytechnic fellowship, shows soluble coffee at San Francisco, and his powder goes to war in the Companies' kits in 1914. By the late 1920s Pacificans drink more coffee a head than any Americans, and a fifth or so of it is grown under the Pacific crowns.

<!-- CR-049: things work in this country -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Families honour schools, physicians, reliable works and successful fellows without knowing any of the science behind them. Things work in this country, and most people's honour rests on making them work. By 1900 the founders' circle has become several generations; Peirce is past sixty and Kate, Brandegee now by her second marriage, past fifty-five, the living memory of the careful account, the worthy opponent, the difficult journey and the person who makes the work dependable.

<!-- CR-077: the analyser and the latch --> **[APPROVED — CR-077]** In 1897–1898 Michelson builds a harmonic analyser at Ralston College, first of twenty elements and then of eighty, for spectra and tides, and Davidson's tide tables run on it. The analogue line stays small, because the Office trusts what it can check digit by digit. In 1899–1901 Michelson lends his valve-amplified counter to the Laboratory's counting room at Carquinez, and there it sticks: a pulse sets a soft valve conducting, and it stays on until the plate current is cut. In Kate Brandegee's counting room Peirce sees in it a relay with no moving parts. Two such valves, cross-coupled, hold one of two states, and within two years the room counts in pairs.

English is the Realm's language of command, the civil examination and business with the center. Spanish and Hawaiian are entrenched in their historic lands; further county languages are grants of organic law. Spanish acquires new commercial reach through the canal, Philippines and Pacific Latin America. Hawaiian remains protected on paper while schools operate in English, until 1907 restores Hawaiian-medium education and the Crown's Hawaiian oath and proclamation. No county has Chinese language status by 1932, though San Francisco courts use Cantonese interpreters. Japanese gains Realm standing in 1927; Hawaiian counties with large Japanese populations become trilingual in 1928.

Skilled entrants face no language test at entry: technical examinations can be taken in several languages, followed by a compulsory English bridging year. Naturalization and the civil examination require English. Guest contracts and wage disputes use interpreters, without requiring English; lack of English blocks the examination route to political power. Community language schools remain and gain subsidies from 1927. Additional interpreter certificates reward reach in Spanish and Hawaiian from 1888, Japanese and Chinese in the 1890s, and Russian from 1921.

<!-- CR-071: the 1888 act and the labour article -->In 1888 selective skilled and sponsored-family immigration replaces the open door, and as the labour article requires, no one enters on a broker's debt. The Asian family programme continues, while unskilled Union arrivals are refused. The policy narrows the earlier Chinese labor promise and makes the bureau's favorable figures partly a result of selection. Confederate abolition in 1888–1889 ends the fugitive agreement; the Hapa ideal is proclaimed in 1889. Douglass and other critics see a door closed just as enslaved people become free. Whether Gulf planters quietly secured that result remains **[OPEN]**. Later mixed families inherit both the ideal and its exclusions.

The champions' demand for workers later broadens the routes into Pacifica. Skilled entry brings families and relatives; higher wages divert some European migrants who would have gone to the Union. When Washington begins to retain its own talent and compete for Europe's, Pacifica recruits through Japanese and Chinese universities and among skilled Koreans, Filipinos and Indians. Returning graduates also carry knowledge into the partners' own economies.

<!-- CR-036: talent from every faith and background -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** The examined come from every exile and every ambition. Forty-Eighters and Polish engineers of 1863 came early. German Catholic scholars and Jesuits come out of Bismarck's struggle with the Church, and Jews shut out of Russia's universities by the quota of 1887. Armenians come after the massacres of 1894–96 and Chinese reformers after 1898. Scandinavian, Scottish, Japanese and American graduates come simply for better laboratories and pay. They sit the same examinations, and the Realm takes them one by one, never as communities. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Not every offer is taken. In the 1890s Carquinez offers a chair to Fritz Haber, a Jewish convert at Karlsruhe still waiting for a professorship, and he stays in Germany, a German first. His synthesis of ammonia will give Germany the nitrates for its war.

<!-- CR-036: Victoria's decisive test of diaspora loyalties, 1891 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** Victoria's decisive test of the diasporas' loyalties comes in 1891. Famine spreads along the Volga in the year Moscow expels its Jews. San Francisco's bankers ask her to send the Tsar nothing, and the nativist papers dare her to prove that the bankers do not govern. She sends grain on Pacifica's terms. Pacifican agents distribute it to the hungry of every faith, and the weekly photographs every station. The bankers get no say and the nativists no gesture. Both complain, and neither leaves.

From 1890 a guest-worker programme fills jobs left by resident growth and skilled migration. Sector quotas and an employer levy protect resident wages; three-year contracts provide inspected housing, wage protection and paid passage home. Workers may change employer after a contract and seek residence after six years and a trade test. The statistics bureau handles permits as routine administration. These routes sustain the industrial economy while leaving a class of workers outside citizenship.

<!-- CR-038: the guest contract's origins -->
**[APPROVED — CR-038]** The programme's contract is the one Hawaii's plantations have used since 1856 and the railway and interior works since the 1860s, now made the Realm's law.

Texas abolishes slavery in 1884; Upper-South states follow, and an 1885 law frees those over sixty. Final Confederate abolition in 1888–1889 is financed by long compensation bonds secured on customs co-administered by Pacifica. Emancipation leaves sharecropping, coercion and contested citizenship. When Richmond misses coupons in 1893–1895, Pacifica's guarantee pays them, deepening dependence. No Confederate state is a Pacifican member in 1895.

The bureau's first great campaign follows Wounded Knee on Union soil in 1890. Photographs and reservation measures set it beside the Navajo treaty and Apache peace. Union critics answer with the Los Angeles massacre of 1871, the Feather River, the Calamba evictions and the fugitive agreement. Prosecution and better treaties can support a comparison; they cannot make those wounds disappear. The depression, Coxey's Army and Pullman in 1893–1894 give the weekly further material. <!-- CR-060 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-060]** Among the critics' cases is the Paiute camp killed in the Owyhee in 1865–66, which Pacifica's courts could only avenge.

<!-- CR-079: projected film and the press estate --> **[APPROVED — CR-079]** The press seat's estate is chartered on 1 August 1887 with the others, and the state holds shares in it. It owns the *Examiner* and the six-language weekly, and on the day of its charter it takes over Le Prince's contract. His single-lens camera films on a paper roll in 1888. With celluloid roll film, perforated strips and an arc lamp on the Realm's cheap current, the first projected film is shown at San Francisco in 1889–90: short subjects shot in the open air, with Muybridge in the audience. Through the 1890s the estate's newsreels play in music halls from Chicago to Berlin; in the Union, Edison's peephole Kinetoscope of 1894 is a parlour novelty beside Pacifican projectors. In 1895 Dickson leaves Edison for the estate and runs its production, its newsreel and its work on sound, and Le Prince keeps the camera and projector works until he retires in 1909. Muybridge dies in 1904.

<!-- CR-033: Native Californian recovery and the leakage of land -->
**[APPROVED — CR-033]** Protected in their homelands and paid for their work, Native Californians, reduced by disease to about two-thirds of their number at the gold discoveries, begin to grow again in the later 1890s. The Office's county tables show them poorer and sicker than their neighbours, beside its comparisons with Wounded Knee. The treaties hold, but long leases at low rents, annuity claims signed over to merchants for debts, and court rulings on boundaries and water keep moving land out of Native hands, and the Realm earns a fee on every lease. By 1932 they number about 130,000 **[MODEL]**.

<!-- CR-071: the nations' ladder --> **[APPROVED — CR-071]** The nations have a ladder of their own. A nation may buy back its ceded land still in the Land Fund at the price the Fund paid for it. Its purchases join the homeland by a supplementary compact, and the county is paid its lost land tax for ten years; a homeland that grows past a million acres leaves its county. A nation whose accounts and services meet the Office's standard for five years becomes chartered. It is then its own trustee and keeps the lease fee the Realm has always taken. It holds its royalties in a patrimony of its own, may charter companies and borrow at home with the Treasury's consent, and keeps a court of first instance and its own police. It may have its language certified, so that its citizens who pass in it cast a tongue vote. The leak of land through long leases still runs, and the Office prints both columns for every nation. **[OPEN]** The year of the ladder's law; which nations climb first.

<!-- CR-076: drink and the homelands --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** Whether drink enters a homeland is the nation's decision. An outsider who sells spirits there against its ordinance answers in the Realm's court on the nation's complaint, and once a nation is chartered its own court and police try him. A nation that licenses may open its own store at the Realm's floor and keep the whole duty, chartered or not, half for its clinics and nurses and half for its roads and water, printed in the Office's two columns. No county may license a public house within ten miles of a homeland line without the nation's consent, the floor is the same on both sides of every line, and on leased ground the lease sets the terms of drink. Royalties and fees go into the nation's account, held in trust until it is chartered and then in its own patrimony, never as cash shares. The Office prints each homeland's drink deaths beside its neighbours'. Where a nation's store is the only one inside the ten miles, county drinkers ride in to it, and the councils argue over whether they want it.

In the Bread March of 1894 armed industrial marchers gather on the Union's Kansas plains below the passes and try to force them. A border post is attacked and men die. Pacifica detains hundreds and tries the armed men fairly, on film. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** Governor Waite's acquiescence helps the march gather. <!-- CR-076: Waite in Kansas (CT4) --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** Davis Waite came to Larned in 1876 and sat in Kansas's legislature in 1879; with Leadville's silver on Pacifica's side he stayed, and he is Kansas's Populist governor in 1893–95. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** In 1897 the prisoners return under Whitney's commerce and arbitration treaty. **[OPEN]** The sentences and any detention settlement remain to be specified. **[PROPOSED]** The affair damages silver populism and revanchism while exposing the exclusions of Victoria's settlement.

<!-- CR-038: the 1895 trades congress and the Guest Workers' Aid -->
**[APPROVED — CR-038]** Victoria means Pacifica's own workers never to need a march. In 1895 a trades congress is chartered by law. It keeps sick and burial funds and the register of tradesmen who have passed the Polytechnic's trade tests, and with the employers it publishes a reference table of wages that contracts may cite. The contractors still hire, train and pay. Guest workers do not join the congress. Its Guest Workers' Aid, paid partly from the employer levy, finds them interpreters and lawyers in a dispute, and shelter and the passage home when an employer fails.

Pacifica rescues the Union's gold reserve in 1895. <!-- CR-073: the 1895 syndicate; Ralston's death --> **[APPROVED — CR-073]** The Treasury's gold goes east through a syndicate led by the Bank of California. **[PROPOSED]** The price includes canal recognition, Asian-war neutrality and tariff concessions. Whitney wins the 1896 presidency with a supportive House; the Senate follows the 1898 state elections and recovery. He seeks competition through investment rather than reconquest, and keeps the Union neutral during the Primorye war. Revanchists call him bought and go underground. The Northern republic remains the continent's largest industrial power, with a navy capable of revival. **[APPROVED — CR-073]** Ralston, who kept the coast's gold from the Union through the war, retired the year before. In 1897, at seventy-one, he dies of a stroke while swimming off North Beach.

In October–November 1894 Victoria supports Japan's case for Korean independence with coal, equipment, credit and diplomatic cover, while keeping Pacifica out of combat. After the victories at Pyongyang and the Yalu she brokers an armistice: Korea independent and neutral under Chinese, Japanese and Pacifican guarantee, Chinese suzerainty ended, Japanese economic predominance, a moderate indemnity and commercial rights, with no mainland cession. There is no Liaodong cession, Port Arthur massacre or Triple Intervention. Queen Min survives and Gojong does not flee to the Russian legation. Japan's army resents the shortened victory; the Qing retain solvency and an ally in the regent. <!-- CR-081: the mission's dead --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Some of the mission's naval graduates are killed at the Yalu in the Beiyang fleet, and the men Pacifica trained are among China's dead.

<!-- CR-074: the companies in 1894 --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** Through the war the companies serving China and Korea stand down from armed duty under the Act. Fong's revenue cruisers keep to the customs, and the guard at Seoul keeps to the palace.

The Formosan Revenue Convention of 1895 leaves the island Chinese. A Pacifican Director-General nominated by the Regent holds a Qing commission; the Republic later continues it. The administration controls the camphor monopoly, Keelung mines, Formosan customs and Keelung harbour trust, including pilots, lights, works and coaling rights. China keeps the governor, courts, police, land and taxes outside the specified revenues. Receipts pay the indemnity, Japan, Pacifica's fee and then the provincial treasury. Fong's Inspectorate collects revenue while his government backs Japan, deepening his divided duty. <!-- CR-074: the Formosan guard --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** A company guards the Keelung mines and the camphor stores for the Director-General, and never serves on the camphor frontier against the mountain peoples. **[PROPOSED]** A smaller indemnity facility and Pacifican gold loan support Japan's gold transition in 1897.

**[PROPOSED]** Credit offered before Li Hongzhang's 1896 tour supports a Chinese-owned Manchurian railway and the Hankow–Canton trunk. <!-- CR-081: the conservancy and the railway loans --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** In 1891, when the Qing puts the Yellow River dikes on the localities, a Chinese conservancy board engages Pacifican engineers for the Yellow River and the Grand Canal under a contract secured on the customs, so the conservancy charge ranks among the customs' first obligations. The railway bonds of 1896 are placed by a syndicate the Bank of California leads under William Alvord. They are secured on the customs, not on the lines' receipts, which go to the Chinese railway administration; only the later line to Huludao will pledge its own receipts to its bondholders. Pacifica lends only to Chinese-owned lines with no foreign management, in silver for work paid for in China and in gold only for imported equipment, carrying the silver risk itself as a silver producer. The Hankow–Canton trunk is built from 1896.

When Russia demands Port Arthur or a Korean harbor after Germany’s Kiaochow seizure, the guarantees are tested. Japan fights on land in 1898–1899; Pacifica holds the sea and lends, while China remains benevolently neutral. Japan gains the Ussuri region, Vladivostok, the Razdolnaya valley and all Sakhalin; China recovers the Amur’s north bank. <!-- CR-039: Japanese place names after 1899 -->Japan names its new province Enkaishū, the port Urajio and the island Karafuto. France remains neutral while its lending sustains Russia. Kamchatka, the Okhotsk coast and the Amur mouth at Nikolayevsk remain Russian until 1921. <!-- CR-042: Dalian stays Chinese; the Liaodong Gulf pledge and the Huludao option --> **[APPROVED — CR-042]** The Liaodong peninsula stays Chinese. Russia does not get Port Arthur, and Japan, which has fought to keep Russia out of it, does not take Port Arthur or Dalian for itself. Victoria negotiates the terms with the Empress Dowager and holds Tokyo to the armistice of 1894, which gave Japan no mainland territory. **[PROPOSED — CR-042]** Cixi's return to her friend is small and written down. China pledges that no part of the coast of the Liaodong Gulf will be leased or ceded to any power, and gives Pacifica first refusal on a harbour at Huludao, a sheltered bay on the gulf's western shore below Jinzhou, if a harbour is ever built there with foreign capital. In the year Germany takes Kiaochow, Pacifica takes nothing, and Victoria keeps the paper.

Russia loses Vladivostok before joining its trunk railway to the Pacific. Japan completes the eastern link and holds the terminal; the Manchurian section belongs to China, with Japanese running rights and a Pacifican railway board in wartime. Russia retains its western line into Transbaikalia and the capacity to threaten Manchuria. **[PROPOSED]** Japan retains the inherited Russian gauge, enabling through traffic before 1914 and the coalition’s later westward advance. Kiyoshi commands at sea and enters Vladivostok on the proposed service chronology.

The Confederacy fractures after emancipation and the debt crisis. **[PROPOSED dates]** A 1891 Deep South presidency and deadlocked 1897 election lead Virginia and North Carolina back to the Union in 1898–1899. Tennessee has been Union since 1865. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** In 1900, the year the canal opens, Texas, Louisiana and Arkansas form the Federal Republic of the Gulf, with Indian Territory as Sequoyah. At its founding the Republic signs the Gulf compact with Pacifica, which sets the tests any admission must pass. **[PROPOSED dates]** Austin is capital, New Orleans the economic center. A mutual defense pact and trade treaty bind it to Pacifica; citizenship stays separate. South Carolina, Georgia, Alabama, Mississippi and Florida remain the rump Confederacy.

<!-- CR-048: Spindletop, 1897 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-048]** Oil is worth more here than in the Union's history: Pacifica's oil programme since 1876 and its oil-fired navy since the 1890s keep prospectors drilling where they would otherwise have given up. **[PROPOSED — CR-048]** In January 1897 Pattillo Higgins's Gladys City company, drilling the salt dome at Spindletop near Beaumont with a Pacifican crew and a rotary rig, brings in the gusher four years before it came in our history. Texas has a fortune of its own in the year the Confederacy's election deadlocks.

The Gulf Republic adopts a strong executive, merit civil service, independent courts, equal civil rights and an examination franchise for specified referendums. Its official language is English, with French in the Louisiana parishes and Spanish in south Texas. The Defense Law test may be sat in English or Spanish, measuring competence rather than command of English alone. Pacifica considers admission only if the Republic adds usable power and freedom of action without adding risk or a veto. Five measures—infant mortality, literacy, real wages, homicide and typhoid—must meet the bar set by Pacifica's weakest former-territory state. The sixth test has two parts: Black and freedmen twenty-year-olds must pass the Defense Law's joint medical and literacy examination at no less than ninety-five percent of the white rate, and their infant mortality must be no more than 1.05 times the white rate. The inegalitarian examination state accepts differences between individuals, but refuses a member that wastes a large part of its people or leaves an underclass a rival could arm.

The compact also requires registers covering at least ninety percent of births, deaths, land titles and taxpayers; revenue sufficient for the common navy and debt quota; and a limit on debt held by Union creditors. Gold, standard-gauge rail, the AC standard, Crown-share concession law and ports open to the common navy must accompany examinations for office and franchise, recognition of the continental settlement and a common foreign policy. Every measure must hold for twenty-five consecutive years before Pacifica will consider admission, and Pacifica may demand a second generation. The Republic's leaders doubt they can meet the challenge; Pacifica is in no hurry to inherit problems they cannot solve.

<!-- CR-058: the oil law and its ceiling -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** The compact also writes Texas's antitrust law into the Republic's founding. Under its law of 1889 Texas had expelled Standard's hidden affiliate Waters-Pierce in 1900 for secret combination and price agreements, not for its size, and it expels the company again in 1907. The Republic bans secret affiliates, rebates and price agreements and separates the stages of the business: no refiner may own more than a minority of producing wells, trunk pipelines are common carriers at published tariffs, and no refiner may refine more than fifty-five percent of the Republic's crude. The ceiling is the compact's, so the Pacifican trust's share is a treaty limit, not an accident. The trust owns refineries, tankers and canal terminals and minority stakes in fields, and buys crude at posted prices; it earns its share as the best buyer, because it has the canal and the Asian markets. Standard's method, fields and pipelines owned through hidden affiliates, is what the law forbids, so Standard buys Gulf crude at the border like anyone else. Gulf populists, Huey Long among them, call the ceiling a foreign quota.

Oil royalties from Spindletop and later Sequoyah finance the Gulf's own schools, health services and statistics. Registration initially makes its reported conditions look worse. Compulsory schooling, clean water, mosquito and hookworm control, licensed midwives, Black teacher training and county clinics attack the gaps. Laws against debt peonage, the end of convict leasing, written sharecropping contracts, farm credit and a professional constabulary challenge planter power. The 1916 Sequoyah settlement secures equal citizenship and land for freedmen. Pacifica supplies engineers, credit and statistical standards while the Gulf chooses its means.

The five general measures first pass together in 1925.
