They hold narrowly through the Mississippi flood of 1927, when Hoover runs relief and the Gulf prosecutes planters holding Black refugees in levee camps. Its published numbers remain honest. Planters oppose both the cost of Black health and schooling and the examination franchise; Huey Long fights that franchise in the name of the common white voter and loses. Black Gulf leaders divide over a bargain that brings protection and opportunity while treating their people as a resource. The 1931 oil glut breaks the general-measures streak, and both parity tests remain far from passing in 1932. Admission is outside the novel: even a late-1930s first success would put the earliest conceivable entry in the mid-1960s. The Gulf ends as an ally, oil partner and protégé with a course for the next century.

**[PROPOSED]** Sequoyah surveys further define the oil partnership; Mobile's lease and Birmingham works remain treaty rights inside the rump, with Galveston the fallback. Galveston's seawall is built in the 1890s; the 1900 storm strikes a protected city, with the residual damage unchosen.

<!-- CR-048: the rump Confederacy and the long emergency -->
**[APPROVED — CR-048]** The rump that remains governs from Montgomery. **[MODEL]** In 1900 just under half its seven million people are Black, far more in the Delta, the Black Belt and the Lowcountry, and the share falls every decade as people take the road north: by 1932 the rump has some nine million, fewer than three in ten of them Black.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** The Protocol of Richmond of 1898 divides the Confederate debt by rules Montgomery's own delegates cannot fault. The war debt follows the customs that secured it, and the compensation bonds and the coupons Pacifica's guarantee paid follow the owners who were compensated, who lived in the Deep South because Texas and the Upper South had abolished by their own laws. Virginia and North Carolina pay their small share in cash, borrowed in New York, and Pacifica's customs officers leave Norfolk and Wilmington. The Gulf turns its share into a loan of its own on Spindletop's royalties at its founding, and Pacifica's customs officers leave Galveston and New Orleans. The rump keeps more than two-thirds of the whole. Pacifican customs officers still sit in Charleston, Savannah, Jacksonville and Mobile, and the coupons are paid before the schools. Pacifica offers Montgomery the Gulf compact in 1900, unchanged. Montgomery never signs, because the sixth test would oblige it to register, school, examine and keep alive the people it rules, and the offer is reprinted every year. Cotton carries the state until the weevil crosses the river in 1907; after it, the modern places in the rump are foreign: Pacifica's Birmingham works and Mobile station, Union money in Florida. **[PROPOSED — CR-048]** Convicts are still leased to the mines, but not to Pacifica's, which pay the Realm's wage-board rates and hire more Black miners than any employer in the South.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** Pacifica reads its 1871 guarantee narrowly: it protects the Confederate States from foreign invasion, not from their own people. It refuses the planters' calls for help, sells arms to no one and offers a mediation that neither side accepts. The guarantee, which passes to the rump in 1898, still keeps Union regulars out, so the trouble can never be ended by war. Pacifica's part is a thin layer of money and a loud honesty. From 1901 the statistics bureau funds Du Bois's Atlanta Studies on the bureau's counting rules; the Office of Public Measures buys pictures from Black photographers at its standard rates; Carquinez offers fellowships to Black scholars the Union will not appoint. Every payment is in the Realm's published accounts. Each year the Office prints the Montgomery table, the compact's measures for the rump beside the Gulf's and, on the same yardstick, the Black districts of Union cities. The charter forbids a false figure. Where the camera points is a choice. **[PROPOSED — CR-048]** Du Bois, who takes Pacifica's grant, says in print that the same Pacifican customs officers collect the coupons that come before the rump's schools.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** The revolution never comes. In 1906 Black families in Atlanta's Brownsville district arm against a mob and a pogrom nearly becomes a rising. On 16 October 1910, the anniversary of Harpers Ferry, Josiah Haynes, a Delta-born veteran of the Union's Black cavalry, crosses from Memphis with forty men, opens the camps at the Parchman prison farm and calls the Delta to rise. It does not rise. The militia and the posses take or kill his men within four days, the reprisals fall on families who never heard of him, and Mississippi hangs him in December while half the North calls him a martyr. The emergency becomes chronic, and the movement it feeds does not stop at the border.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** Virginia and North Carolina came back into the Union on fundamental conditions: every resident a citizen, no citizen's vote taken away except for crime, no Confederate debt and no compensation for the Union to pay, and a general amnesty that keeps the Confederacy's cabinet, congressmen and generals out of federal office for ten years. Wilmington's white supremacists overthrow its elected government in November 1898 while North Carolina's act is before Congress, and Congress adds federal election supervisors there for ten years. The white machines accept the conditions on paper and then write poll taxes and tests equal on their face, Virginia in 1902 and North Carolina in 1910 once the supervisors go. The Union's Supreme Court will not strike them down; Congress can neither enforce the conditions nor drop them. George Henry White holds eastern North Carolina's seat until 1910, and the new Southern Democrats give Wilson his majority in 1912. Washington must answer Springfield in 1908, East St. Louis in 1917 and Chicago in 1919, segregate its service under Wilson and half undo it, alternately appeasing and striking at an assertive Black minority it can no longer ignore. Each concession raises Black expectations, and each backlash radicalises the next demand.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** The cost falls on Roosevelt's programme. Garrisons and refugee relief draw on the money meant for laboratories, and when letters on Haynes's men name the Northern professors who paid for his raid, Congress passes the Parchman Act of 1911, attaching loyalty and neutrality oaths to the programme's grants, so the instrument built to keep talent at home helps send it abroad. Ernest Everett Just, born in Charleston and refused a research post in the Union, signs the Parchman oath at Howard, and after Chicago's riot takes a Carquinez fellowship in 1920 to survey Kāneʻohe Bay; he is on the reef when the Crown Prince dives it. Conspiracy-minded Union writers, most of them revanchists and loudest in McCormick's *Chicago Tribune*, say Pacifica made the whole crisis. They cite the Atlanta grant, the raid backer who once held a Carquinez fellowship, the Office's purchases, Carnegie's Tuskegee gift and *The Crisis* carried as freight on Pacifican lines. All of it is public and lawful; none of it proves design. The Office learns the raid backer's name from the Union's newspapers. Pacifica's attention is on London. **[OPEN — CR-048]** Who suffers the Act's celebrated dismissal, the case every European scholar reads, is undecided.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** After 1916 the movement's mass belongs to Marcus Garvey. His divisions spread through the Delta and the Black Belt, and the generation that watched Parchman fail joins him instead of raiding: pride, uniforms and the road out take the place of a rising. Montgomery tolerates a man who preaches separation, and in Atlanta in 1922 he meets the Klan's Edward Young Clarke. Emigration becomes the one programme the rump's populists and the Black nationalists share, and the rump cannot pay for it; its revenue is pledged to the coupons. Garvey makes the Pacific order his proof of what non-white peoples can build, a Hawaiian queen at its origin and a brown crown at its head, though in Colón in 1911 he had seen West Indians paid well on Pacifica's canal and offered no road to its citizenship. The *Tribune* calls his praise Pacifica's payroll. Pacifica answers by refusing him in public. Its yards sell the Black Star Line no ships, its finances having failed the published credit test, and Honolulu declines his lecture. Garvey is a British subject whom London hates, and Julian will not let London think Pacifica turns Garvey's race against its empire in the years before the Concord. In 1927 the Union deports Garvey to Jamaica in concert with the British, and Thalassia, which watches the two services work together for the first time, refuses him entry.

**[PROPOSED — CR-048]** The rump's civil religion is the Lost Cause. The second Klan is refounded on Stone Mountain in 1915 and travels north; the carving on the mountain begins in 1923. Mill villages live with pellagra and hookworm, and populists who once courted Black farmers win office by hating them. Black life gathers around Tuskegee, Atlanta's colleges and the churches, with the road north always in view. Across the river the Gulf is the mirror. In 1921 its Republic Constabulary stops the mob at Tulsa. In 1927, while the Gulf prosecutes planters holding refugees in its levee camps, Greenville on the rump's bank holds them at gunpoint, and Montgomery takes Hoover's relief only through its own committees and refuses Pacifican inspectors. The Office films both banks.

Cuba becomes independent after the Maine crisis of 1898; Puerto Rico goes to the Union. Pacifica stays neutral while its forces support the Primorye war. Tomás Estrada Palma, based with the exile junta in New Orleans, becomes Cuba's first president. Pacifica, the Gulf Republic and the Union jointly guarantee Cuban independence, allowing intervention only by agreement of all three and no foreign bases. **[PROPOSED]** Pacifica buys discounted colonial debt secured on Cuban customs, funds Finlay's yellow-fever work and acquires exclusive Nicaraguan canal rights. Victoria suppresses her engineers' suspicion that the Maine suffered a bunker fire; that suspicion remains evidence, not a proven cause.

The French Panama enterprise is bought in 1889. A lock-and-lake design and mosquito control guide the regency's work. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** The canal opens in 1900. **[PROPOSED]** A Galápagos station protects the approach; Colombia's war, from 1899, threatens the transit. The canal makes earlier Gulf access less indispensable without dissolving the partnerships built around it.

<!-- CR-073: the canal purchase money; CR-072: Judah's death --> **[APPROVED — CR-073]** The Treasury pays for it with canal bonds, and Ralston, as the Patrimony's chairman, arranges the syndicate that places them. His own rule bars the Bank of California from any syndicate he arranges, so the Nevada Bank and the London houses lead the largest issue of the decade. **[APPROVED — CR-072]** Theodore Judah, who drew the counties, heads the first survey of the works, and in 1890 Judah dies of yellow fever at Colón, at sixty-four. His death hardens the regency's resolve on mosquito control.

**[PROPOSED]** Cheap network moves fill the intervals: Baring-crisis gold loans, Chilean nitrate and diplomacy, Tutuila through the Samoan settlements of 1889 and 1899, Pearl Harbor works, a cable through Midway and Guam to Manila, electrical equipment for Asia and wireless.

<!-- CR-078: the southern wire and the warnings compact --> **[APPROVED — CR-078]** Port Hamilton showed that the alliance's telegrams to Tokyo crossed British wires, by Europe and by the Hong Kong–Manila cable that the Eastern Extension company landed at Bolinao in 1880 on a Pacifican landing grant. In 1888 Victoria asks for a wire to Tokyo that touches no foreign soil. The inland command's senators call it a wire for the coast's merchants; Soberanes reads the crisis's delays to the Senate telegram by telegram, and the vote passes. The trunk runs from Oʻahu by Midway, on a cable-station lease from the Hawaiian Crown, to Guam in 1889–90, from Guam to Manila in 1891, and from Guam by Chichijima to Tokyo Bay in 1892, on Japanese landing grants under the compact of 1887; it carries traffic from the autumn of 1892 and lands only on Pacifican and Japanese soil. Its core is gutta-percha, bought by the Borneo company from the interior's peoples under its compacts and worked at the Carquinez cable works, which has made cable since 1879; the tappers fell the trees and go deeper into the hills, into the country of Mat Salleh's people. On 15 June 1896 the news of the Sanriku earthquake reaches Honolulu over the wire before the waves do, and no one there knows what it means; that night the waves strike Hawaii's coasts. In 1897 the Realm and Japan agree to exchange warnings of earthquakes at sea and of typhoons, and from then on typhoon warnings reach Guam, Manila and the Visayas a day ahead.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** In Samoa the hurricane of March 1889 wrecks the German warships at Apia while a Pacifican cruiser steams out to sea; the Berlin Act of June makes Germany, Britain and Pacifica the protecting powers, and the partition of 1899 gives Pacifica Tutuila. In the Philippines the land board's evictions at Calamba in 1890–91 turn José Rizal from a physician into a critic. In 1892, when he founds the Liga Filipina, the Regent answers with elected provincial juntas. The Philippine Office of Public Measures grades each province: Pampanga, Bulacan, Batangas, Ilocos Norte, Cebu, Iloilo and chartered Manila first, the Cordillera and Moroland administered. The evicted tenants are resettled at New Calamba on the Pacifican Borneo company's land at Marudu Bay as a self-governing colony. <!-- CR-074: Borneo --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** The company buys that land by compact from the people of the bay before it grants it, as it must for any land beyond its stations. Tenants evicted at Calamba who serve in the Philippine companies have first claim on the friar-land warrants. In August 1895 its resident orders the village of Mat Salleh, a Sugut chief, burned at Jambongan; the constabulary's captain refuses under the Act, and the Companies Office upholds him. Mat Salleh burns the company's station on Gaya in July 1897 and is hunted under warrant, and in April 1898 the company's director, William Cowie, makes peace with him at Palatan. When the board moves on Tambunan the next year, the Office holds it to the peace on pain of its licence, and Tambunan becomes a compact land under its own people. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** When cholera returns in 1888, the quarantine at Mariveles and the ordinance towns hold it to a few hundred deaths, where Spain's islands had buried twenty thousand. The Office's tables say so in every province. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** In 1886 the peso goes onto a gold-exchange basis, ending the silver chaos Spain had left. Its reserve is built from the profit on coining a token peso and held in San Francisco, and the Banco Español-Filipino remains the bank of issue. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Manila hemp makes the islands indispensable: every navy's rope and every wheat farmer's binder twine is spun from it, and a Pacifican cordage champion spins most of it.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** In August 1896 the excluded rise: artisans and clerks of Tondo and Cavite, perhaps eight thousand, in a secret society with no ilustrados in it, over rice prices, estate installments and debt. The chartered towns refuse to join, Rizal declines to endorse them, and gunboats seal Manila Bay and the Cavite shore. The rising lasts weeks. A German cruiser division arrives to protect German nationals; it finds Pacifican ships in strength and leaves for China. A young advocate, Felipe Calderón, defends the accused, and the Regent commutes the sentences. The Union's illustrated weeklies still call her Pacifica's Weyler. In the spring of 1897 a steamer bought in Japan by Filipino exiles, a pan-Asian patron and a Chinese revolutionary, with a vice-minister looking the other way, is followed from Nagasaki and stopped in the Balintang Channel. Pacifica protests in private, because Japan's war with Russia is near and Tokyo needs the fleet. The vice-minister goes, the ship goes back, and its rifles find their way to the Chinese revolutionaries. The exiles learn that Japan will not take their side against Pacifica, and the southward school in Tokyo learns what acting without the cabinet costs.

The approved Klondike policy holds the Alaska boundary firmly while offering Canada fair transit; a Skagway–Whitehorse railway, assay and banking capture route income from Canadian gold. <!-- CR-070: the copper and the road to the Yukon -->**[APPROVED — CR-070]** In 1885 the Ahtna leader Nicolai shows the Commission's party the copper of the Chitina and makes a finder's compact for it, so that he never has to trade its whereabouts for food, and the mine opened under it ships its ore from about 1900. The road to the Yukon is ready before the gold. In 1880 the Chilkoot of Dyea make a compact with the Northern Commission: their packers carry, their trade goes on, and prospectors cross their pass for a toll. The Chilkat of Klukwan refuse, and their pass stays theirs and closed: their leader Kohklux drew Davidson a map of that road when he was a guest, and will not open it to him as Commissioner. From 1884, for the Yukon bar miners, the Chilkoot and the Commission build a wagon road to Sheep Camp and an aerial tramway over the summit, and the Fortymile strike of 1886 pays for it. When Bonanza Creek comes in in August 1896, one of its finders a Tagish packer, Skookum Jim, on Canadian soil where no finder's compact runs, the Tagish and Chilkoot packers bring the news over the pass that winter, the wire carries it south from Dyea, Ottawa's own reports with it, and the railway over the White Pass from Skagway is building before the stampede of 1897, on a compact with the Chilkoot, whose ground the pass is, that gives them a share of its freight for the packing it will end. Pacifica's own wire to Sitka has run under the sea from Puget Sound since 1888, ordered by Elias's cabinet in 1886 on Soberanes's case. Pacifica builds on the news and does not use it against Ottawa: in the spring of 1897, when the strike is public, it sells Canada the old line through British Columbia as the first half of Canada's own wire to the Yukon. Between 1900 and 1903 Pacifica carries the Sitka cable on, to Kodiak and Unalaska in 1900 and by Attu and the Kurils to Hakodate in 1903: the second route to Japan is open, landing only on Pacifican and Japanese soil.

**[PROPOSED]** Honolulu and San Francisco shelter Chinese reformers and revolutionaries after 1898; a limited Boxer relief contingent promptly withdraws and turns its indemnity into scholarships. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** After 1901 part of Pacifica's share also goes to the conservancy, and the land reclaimed from the canal lakes is sold or taxed to repay part of the works. <!-- CR-081: the exiles, Cixi's price and Kaiping --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Kang Youwei, Liang Qichao and Sun's men have asylum and the right to organise among the Chinese Pacificans, but no arms, credit or ships for risings, and Victoria asks Cixi to leave the exiles unharmed without promising anything about their plans. The Baohuanghui and Sun's society compete for the same remittance money in San Francisco and Honolulu. Reform money from the Americas has helped pay for the Hankou rising of August 1900, and Cixi asks her price for the asylum: in 1901 Victoria has the Companies Act's ban on filibustering enforced against fundraising for armed risings, and Liang's collections in Honolulu are its first case. The society keeps its newspapers and schools and loses its arms fund, and Kang's men accuse Pacifica of serving the Empress Dowager. In 1900–01 Herbert Hoover, a young Pacifican mining engineer, refinances the Kaiping mines on Pacifican credit, and they stay with their Chinese owners. Fushun stays Chinese too, and both sell coal to Japan on long contracts. **[PROPOSED]** A settlement with Britain during the Boer War fixes canal, Alaska, Samoa and Pacific recognition before the next stage of rivalry. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Pacifica recognizes British Fanning as the station of the Pacific Cable; Britain recognizes Pacifican sovereignty over the equatorial islands from Palmyra to Baker.

<!-- CR-058: seals, salmon and the western waters -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Canada learns to conduct its own diplomacy with Pacifica, on the business London knows least: seals, salmon and rivers. <!-- CR-070: the seal convention of 1886 -->Pacifica owns the Pribilofs and makes no claim that the Bering Sea is closed; **[APPROVED — CR-070]** in 1884, when the sealing schooners first reach the Bering Sea, **[APPROVED — CR-058]** it proposes a conservation regime and seats Canadian delegates at the table. **[APPROVED — CR-070]** In 1886 Pacifica, Britain for Canada, Russia and Japan sign **[APPROVED — CR-058]** the four-power convention, which bans pelagic sealing and gives Canada fifteen percent of the Pribilof catch, fifteen percent of the Commander Islands' and ten percent of Robben Island's. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** About 1906, before the Union and Canada make their own treaty, **[APPROVED — CR-058]** Pacifica and Britain, for Canada, with Canadians doing the negotiating, sign a treaty on the western waters: the Columbia, the Kootenay, the Okanagan, the Similkameen, the Skagit, the Pend Oreille, the Fraser's salmon and the Alaskan rivers of 1871. Its joint commission is a commission of equals with powers to regulate as well as to investigate, the strong commission Canada wanted, and Pacifica, downstream on the Columbia, rejects the doctrine that an upstream state owes its neighbour nothing. When Canada and the Union make their treaty in 1909, Canada asks the Union for what Pacifica has already granted and gets less; its quarrels with the Union over the Chicago diversion, the St Mary and the Milk and Niagara go on in the east. Canadians learn that a treaty with Pacifica is a treaty and a treaty with the Union a draft for its Senate. <!-- CR-058: the 1911 election -->In 1911 nobody in Canada fears annexation by a Union that has lost two wars, but Laurier still loses narrowly to Borden on 21 September: the Canadian Pacific's Van Horne sets out to bust reciprocity, the Toronto Eighteen and the Montreal banks defend the east–west economy in which Pacifican capital and the Vancouver–Asia trade now sit, Bourassa splits Liberal Québec over the naval law of 1910, and the West's wheat can leave through Vancouver and the canal. Canadians also remember that the Union cancelled reciprocity in 1866 and the fisheries articles in 1885. Pacifica does nothing visible.

Gold also comes from Alaska's Juneau, Nome and Fairbanks, Nevada's Tonopah and Goldfield, and the Unsan concession granted by Korea in 1897. Foreign concession gold reaches San Francisco's reserve through sale to the Reserve Bank against credit. It is neither Pacifican mineral ownership nor domestic GDP. These sources widen the financial base without making every discovery an automatic public windfall.

### Industry beneath the measures

Coal from Washington, Utah, Rock Springs and New Mexico and partner contracts in Japan, Keelung and Kaiping supply the earlier fleet's colliers and stockpiles. The oil programme of 1876 and turbine programme of about 1885 lead to oil-fired torpedo boats, protected cruisers, turbine destroyers in 1893–1895 and turbine cruisers by 1898. Submarines follow from 1900 and all-big-gun turbine capital ships from 1903. Britain's Dreadnought of 1906 answers Pacifica's ships; its arrival causes no Pacifican technical or fiscal shock. Aircraft carriers follow from 1922. The design dates are approved narrative targets whose engineering and procurement capacity still require evidence.

National champions grow outward from open Polytechnic patents, infrastructure and Asian markets. **[APPROVED — CR-057]** Oil, mineral, rail, port and power concessions are auctioned, and the state takes royalties and fees rather than equity. It holds shares only in the canal and the trunk rights of way, the monopolies it owns, and in its own estates. <!-- CR-078: the cables among the monopolies --> **[APPROVED — CR-078]** The telegraph estate's long lines and cables are among those monopolies. **[APPROVED — CR-057]** Competitive steel, motor, chemical, electrical, aluminium and aircraft firms carry no state stake, <!-- CR-079: the press estate's film --> **[APPROVED — CR-079]** nor do film firms other than the press estate's; **[APPROVED — CR-057]** a golden share survives only in a short published list of defence firms, against foreign takeover. Commercial banking has no state stake and remains under Reserve Bank regulation. Private firms retain management and competition.

The oil trust refines Gulf crude from Spindletop onward, sells lamp oil across Asia and supplies bunkers to Pacific shipping. Steel draws on western coal, hydropower, Philippine and Chilean ore to serve Pacific rails, ships and ports. In 1901 a San Francisco syndicate buys Carnegie Steel and combines it with the Pacifican mills as Pacifica Steel, the world's first billion-dollar company. The syndicate pays the $480m price in paper, Pacifica Steel gold bonds and preference shares, and Carnegie becomes the largest creditor of a Pacifican company. The Union's great apostle of peace lives on Pacifican coupons, paid twice a year in gold. The sale succeeds and produces lasting Union anger at Pacifica and at Wall Street's foreign ties. <!-- CR-073: the 1901 syndicate --> **[APPROVED — CR-073]** The syndicate is the Bank of California's, and the Pacifican mills it joins to Carnegie's began as Ralston's Pacific Rolling Mills.

A Pacifican engineer and firm open the moving motor assembly line in 1911. Chemicals develop from licensed dynamite and naval powder to electrochemistry, plastics and drugs. The AC standard spreads with Pacifican equipment and credit through the Pacific, East Asia, Latin America and the Persian Gulf oil coast. British India and most of Africa remain beyond its general reach. Cables, radio, rail concessions, shipping and underwriting connect these industries; Los Angeles film and sound, Hawaiian aluminium and aircraft add new products. Standards, credit and corporate control make the network profitable without making every trading country Pacifican territory.

<!-- CR-052: the oilfields found early -->
**[APPROVED — CR-052]** The state survey of the 1876 programme maps California's anticlines from the start, and the fleet's and the railways' demand for fuel oil drives the drilling, so the limit on the oil is the depth a well can reach, not knowledge. **[MODEL — CR-052]** The shallow fields of the Los Angeles basin, Los Angeles City and the Puente and Brea hills, come in during the early 1880s; Kern River, Coalinga, McKittrick and Midway–Sunset follow in the San Joaquin by the 1890s, and Huntington Beach about 1895, at the deepest reach of cable tools. Rotary drilling from Spindletop carries the wells to Signal Hill, Santa Fe Springs and Elk Hills in 1902–1910, and the Gulf's Hughes bit of 1909 takes them to Wilmington and the deeper sands; Kettleman Hills, which needs a seven-thousand-foot well, waits until 1923. **[APPROVED — CR-052]** From the 1910s the state survey's reservoir engineers keep the fields' pressure up with gas and water, fifteen years before the Union learns how. **[MODEL — CR-052]** Home output is about 74 million barrels in 1900 and 228 million in 1913, peaks at about 304 million in 1924 and is 255 million in 1929. **[APPROVED — CR-052]** Haste brings the oil forward; it does not make more of it. In the later 1920s the old fields decline, and the trust ships home through the canal all the Gulf crude it controls beyond its Asian contracts. Coal from Utah's Carbon County, Rock Springs, New Mexico and Washington, raised to about four times its historical output, carries the railways and mills that cannot get oil, and the Realm imports no coal.

<!-- CR-058: Doheny, the railway fuel and Mexican oil -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Edward L. Doheny, born at Fond du Lac in 1856, leaves school at sixteen for the West, drives mules for a boundary survey and then works for the state survey, which from 1876 maps California's anticlines, and its geologists teach him to read rock. In the 1880s he mines at Kingston, New Mexico, where he marries Carrie Louella Wilkins in 1883 and makes a friendship with Albert Fall that never becomes a transaction. He reaches Los Angeles about 1890, after the survey has found the city field, and wildcats on its fringes; his gift is selling. He wins the railways' fuel contracts, the Santa Fe's among them, and becomes the largest independent beside the trust. In 1900 the president of the Mexican Central, A. A. Robinson, invites him south because his locomotives need fuel oil; the Mexican Petroleum Company's wells at El Ébano come in in 1901, the year after the canal opens, so Tampico crude can also go west. Its Huasteca company brings in Cerro Azul No. 4 in February 1916, the greatest gusher the world has seen, and his holding company, Pan American, builds a tanker fleet for the canal. In Mexico his companies contend with Pearson's El Águila and with the Union's Standard. He pays the royalty on every barrel from the fields he wins at auction, resents it, and from about 1896 sits in the Senate for Los Angeles as the voice of the independents against the trust. <!-- CR-058-R1: the critic brought inside -->**[APPROVED — CR-058-R1]** About 1907, when Turner reaches sixty-eight, the Five bring the critic inside: Doheny leaves the Senate for Turner's seat, the seat of naval construction, heavy industry and the champions, where the Realm's fuel policy now lives, and holds it until he reaches sixty-eight in 1924. A rival who keeps winning is safer at the table than outside it. Under the rules against capture he keeps the ownership of his companies and gives up their management, to Canfield until Ned is old enough. When Mexico's constitution of 1917 gives the subsoil to the nation, Doheny the oilman wants intervention, and Doheny of the Five must enforce the Realm's refusal on his own company. He does it, furious, and his companies take concession terms, because Pacifica's own model is state-owned subsoil, auctioned concessions and royalties. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** Pan American's concessions cannot pass to foreign control, so it is never sold abroad, and there is no scandal to force a sale. In 1919–1921 his money for the Irish republic is a private embarrassment to Thalassia, all the sharper because it comes from one of the Five while Britain is inside the coalition in Russia; Thalassia leaves it private, it costs him with Victoria, and London remembers. His son Ned takes over the business in the 1930s.

<!-- CR-055: the Columbia built early, and its electrochemistry -->
**[APPROVED — CR-055]** The Columbia is built early, because Pacifica has uses for its power that no other country has. **[MODEL — CR-055]** The power estate's hydro companies decide Bonneville, on the lower river at the Cascades, in 1908, and it gives first power in 1913; Rocky Reach above Wenatchee follows, decided in 1914 and running in 1919, then Wells on the Methow reach (1921, power 1926), McNary above the Umatilla (1922, power 1927) and Chief Joseph (1926, power 1931). **[APPROVED — CR-055]** Chief Joseph is built lower than the site allows so that salmon can climb it. Every dam is laddered, and each is leased from the peoples of its reach under a compact that pays a royalty on every kilowatt-hour, sells their villages electricity at cost, gives replacement fishing sites and publishes the fish counts before their observers. None is built where salmon cannot pass: there is no dam at The Dalles over Celilo Falls, none on the lower Snake, and none at the Grand Coulee over Kettle Falls. The Bonneville compact of 1908, with the Cascade and Wasco bands and the Yakama, Warm Springs and Umatilla fishing peoples, sets its own terms a year before the Me-Wuk lease. **[OPEN]** The peoples' leaders and the councils' names. **[APPROVED — CR-055]** The river's electrochemistry comes a decade before the Union's: electrolytic copper refining at Great Salt Lake from 1905, electrolytic zinc at Spokane on the Coeur d'Alene ores from 1908, magnesium from Great Salt Lake brine from 1909 and from seawater at Tacoma from 1924, ammonia on the Columbia's electrolytic hydrogen from 1915 under a BASF licence of 1913, and furnace phosphorus on the upper Snake from 1915. **[PROPOSED — CR-055]** The cells' residue is rich in heavy hydrogen. The Works recognises it in 1916–1917, beside the proof of the light uranium isotope; the isotope itself appears in Dempster's published survey, but the Works keeps to itself that the Columbia's cells concentrate it by the ton.

<!-- CR-049: the Works' leverage, the valve and the neutron -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-049]** From 1888 to 1907 the counters, the neutron and the hunt for new activities run on the Light and Matter Works' sources. Ralston College, a private college, depends on them like every laboratory in Europe. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1891 the Ames plant near Telluride sends alternating current to the Western Slope mills. Joachimsthal remembers the cheap years: from about 1890 Vienna raises its prices for residues and begins extracting the barium-borne element itself. The current that crosses the vacuum from a hot lamp filament is explained at once by the electron of 1879, and Tesla's wireless work for the navy needs faint signals strengthened; by about 1895 his estate and the Works build valve amplifiers, and with them the counter records single particles reliably. <!-- CR-050: the subscription trade and Langmuir -->**[APPROVED — CR-050]** Because the bismuth-borne preparations lose half their strength in a few months, the Works sells them by subscription, renewed several times a year. The valves of the 1890s are soft and erratic. In 1903 Irving Langmuir, a Brooklyn-born graduate of Columbia's School of Mines, comes to Tesla's electrical institute instead of Göttingen and becomes Tesla's student; about 1908 he makes the first hard-vacuum valves. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The bismuth-borne source, put against beryllium, then gives off something that no magnet bends, that goes through lead, and that knocks hydrogen nuclei out of paraffin with more energy than any ray could give them. Goldstein reads it as an extremely hard ray and refuses Nagaoka's request to measure the recoils. Ralston College gives Nagaoka Michelson's valve-amplified counter, and Peirce works the recoils of hydrogen and nitrogen through the conservation of energy and momentum to a neutral particle of about hydrogen's mass. Ralston College announces in 1899. Carquinez confirms it in 1901, and Goldstein accepts it then; he keeps his chair and the credit for the nucleus, but the next generation's best students go to Ralston College. The neutron is public. From 1902 to 1907 laboratories on both sides of the world slow neutrons in paraffin and water, fire them into every element, uranium among them, and report heavier elements that are not there.

<!-- CR-077: the pair, the adder and Peirce's last evening --> **[APPROVED — CR-077]** From 1901 the counting room's trigger counters count the neutron's recoils and the Works' activities at thousands a second. They are unreliable, and the room logs every false count. Langmuir's hard valves make the two-state pair stable, and his laboratory at Tesla's institute publishes it in 1910 as the pair. San Jose broadcasts from 1909 to listeners on crystal sets; from about 1914 the valve receiver is a household good, and by 1916 the Realm's valve works make oxide-cathode valves by the hundred thousand. In 1912 the Office demonstrates a valve adder and a decade ring of ten pairs, which add in thousandths of a second where the relay machines take tenths. In the autumn of 1913 Peirce, seventy-four, is the respondent at his last challenge evening and asks how anyone will know the fast machine is right. The room accepts Kate Brandegee's answer: every calculation is run twice by different routes, and the discrepancies are kept. Peirce dies at Carquinez in April 1914. The adder is in the room's inventory under his name.

<!-- CR-078: the air --> **[APPROVED — CR-078]** About 1900 soft valves give receivers and low-power continuous waves, while high power comes from arcs; voice goes by radio about 1905, and from 1909 hard-valve transmitters begin to replace spark. About 1902 Tesla proposes to send power through the earth from a tower. At the challenge evening Ryan, thirty-six, asks what fraction would reach a receiver ninety miles away and how anyone would measure it; Tesla accepts the room's test, the receiver at Sacramento takes in less than a thousandth of what is sent, and Tesla withdraws the tower himself. The result is printed under both their names. Photographs go by wire from 1908–10, and the Concord's newspapers, orders and contracts in kanji, Chinese and Hawaiian travel as pictures; teleprinters carry a six-language news wire from about 1910. In 1910–12 the power estate builds long-wave arc stations at Oʻahu, Guam, Manila, Sitka and Tutuila, which talk to every maker's sets, and from 1913 Fuller gives the chain its valve transmitters; about 1910 a second span from Midway by Wake to Guam doubles the trunk's longest stretch. The Wireless Act of 1911 makes wavelengths concessions of the telegraph estate, auctioned, each carrying a public-service article: the Office of Public Measures' daily tables are read unaltered, and air time goes to the examination lessons. San Jose's station has to bid for its own wavelength, and keeps it. Regular broadcasting begins at San Jose and San Francisco in 1912, with stations at Honolulu, Los Angeles, Salt Lake and Manila in 1913–14. In August 1914 Julian speaks to the Realm on the air at the outbreak of war. From 1915 the air school broadcasts the examination lessons at fixed hours, in English and Spanish, then Hawaiian and Japanese (1916), Tagalog from Manila (1917) and Cantonese (1918), and the county halls lend receivers to families who have none. Hearst's editors find the press seat's own radio news read out before their papers are printed.

<!-- CR-079: the loudspeaker, the record, the anniversary broadcast and the air's rules --> **[APPROVED — CR-079]** Peter L. Jensen, a Dane of twenty-three, comes in 1909 to set up the power estate's arc stations. At Napa, with Edwin Pridham, he builds a moving-coil loudspeaker that works in 1912, and a public-address set follows in 1913. Valve amplifiers make Poulsen's magnetic wire usable about 1910 for dictation, the courts and the Senate, the Office's hearings and the cipher room's intercepts. From about 1912 the press estate records electrically in its San Francisco studio and presses its records in phenolic resin under Baekeland's licence; from about 1915 the radio set's loudspeaker plays the records too, and families listen together. Victoria never sings again and never speaks into a microphone. Her voice belonged to Elias and to the chant, and she will not let the press estate cut and arrange it. The cylinder Elias kept of her voice stays with her and is never copied. She also holds Elias's own cylinders. In 1913 Julian asks for electrical copies and she agrees, and from 1 August 1913, the anniversary of the Constitution, the press estate broadcasts the founder's voice each year. The first time she hears it on the air she switches the set off. In the same year she settles the rule for the Office's films, which her own work began: the Office makes them, and the press estate distributes them unaltered, as it prints the tables. In August 1914 the crowds in Union Square and at Honolulu hear Julian's broadcast through Jensen's loudspeakers, and older listeners hear Elias's cadence in him. Eugène Lauste, Dickson's colleague at Edison's laboratory, follows him to San Francisco in 1896 and in 1906 records sound photographically beside the picture on one film; in 1911 the estate shows a film whose sound is read by photocell and played through a valve amplifier. Le Prince's and Dickson's cameras, the Latham loop and Lauste's track are licensed at the one royalty, so no patents trust forms, and from about 1908 the studios move from San Francisco to Los Angeles for light and land. Joseph Kekuku, who found the steel guitar's sound as a schoolboy at the Kamehameha School about 1889, plays and teaches at San Francisco from 1904, and from 1913 the Honolulu station broadcasts chant, hula songs and the steel guitar. An amending act of 1913 to the Wireless Act gives no wavelength to a party and sells election air time to every ticket at one published rate; the Office prints each ticket's minutes beside its votes, and the Prosperity Party buys the most. Outside the public-service hours programmes may be sponsored, and each sponsor is named on the air. From 1916 the air school also teaches the languages on the Crown's list at fixed hours for the language examinations, and the Office prints the new certifications each year by language and county. Hawaiian, the language of the Kaʻahumanu Society's schools, becomes a second tongue and a tongue vote for thousands of haole and Japanese islanders, and county boards and the Party argue over whether the school is teaching or campaigning. The *Examiner* answers the radio news with the picture wire and becomes a picture paper.

<!-- CR-050: Europe answers Pacifica's data -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** The nucleus of 1883 proves an atom that classical physics says should collapse, and Balmer's formula of 1885 for hydrogen's lines makes the puzzle sharper. Kelvin, Lorentz, Larmor, FitzGerald and Poincaré build models that fail. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** At Carquinez in the 1890s Nagaoka proposes a ring of electrons around the core, the brave Pacifican model that fails. In the late 1880s Peirce's fits to the scattering from foil after foil give each core a charge near half its atomic weight and in the order of the periodic table, so charge, not weight, orders the elements while Mendeleev is alive to see his table's anomalies explained. Morley's heat of 1877 has already ended Kelvin's objection to Darwin about the age of the Earth. Vienna founds an institute for the barium-borne element in the 1890s on the salt it learned to keep after the cheap years. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Planck reaches the quantum in Berlin in December 1900 by his own road, and Einstein's light quanta and relativity follow in 1905. Physics has needed something like the quantum since 1883, and Pacifica has measured the effect of light on metals precisely since the 1890s, so the quantum is accepted almost at once. In 1907 Arthur Erich Haas in Vienna joins Planck's quantum to the nucleus of 1883 and Balmer's formula and derives hydrogen's spectral constant: Europe answers Pacifica's question, as Einstein answered Morley's deficit. Between 1906 and 1910 a European astronomer, reading Morley's helium deficit with Einstein's relation, suggests that the sun shines by turning hydrogen into helium. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** Bohr extends the quantum atom to heavier atoms in 1911–1913, and quantum mechanics comes in 1922–1924. The Kaiser Wilhelm Society (1911) is set up explicitly to compete with Pacifica.

<!-- CR-050: the dangers published -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** Pacifica publishes the dangers, and no craze for the barium-borne salt ever takes hold. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** In the 1880s the Works' physicians record burns and blood changes among the people who handle its sources. From 1888 the exposure registers of miners, mill hands and laboratory staff are tabulated on Hollerith's cards at the Office of Public Measures, and Kate Curran's counting room is the first group studied. In the 1890s the link between the mines' active gas and the miners' cancers is proposed, and ventilation rules follow on the Western Slope and in the Ute lease's mines; treatment of tumours with the sources begins at Carquinez and Honolulu, and the Works' own protection rules become a public standard about 1900. About 1910 Pacifica convenes an international conference on protection from the barium-borne salt and from the rays of the discharge tubes, honest science and diplomacy at once, which also gives the Works' slow licensing of strong neutron sources abroad a public reason. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The public learns to think of activity as medicine and danger, not power. The popular idea of atomic energy is thirty years older than Europe's, a theme of romances and of Hearst's press, but so is the belief that the rate of release is fixed by nature and can never be tapped.

The pre-1900 strategy seeks local superiority in the western Pacific; later combined-fleet parity emerges through the alliance and negotiated limits. Peacetime military and overseas spending has a planning ceiling of four percent of North American GDP, interest fifteen percent of public revenue and public debt thirty percent of that GDP. The audit keeps period, perimeter and crisis exceptions explicit.

## 26. Japan’s victory, the hard renewal and the Act of Settlement (1900–1907)

Julian grows up amid the public memory of Elias and the private history of Victoria’s marriage. Officers, servants, teachers and his adoptive mother give him incompatible accounts of the founder. The factions court the intended heir while the conditions attached to his future power become visible. Victoria offers tenderness and a frightening example of political authority. His education, friendships and early public life remain **[OPEN]** and must form an independent trajectory.

**[PROPOSED]** During the Boxer rising in 1900 Russia enters Manchuria from Transbaikalia and occupies China’s railway toward Harbin. It seeks to regain a southern outlet lost in 1899. In 1904–1905 the completion of Russia’s Circum-Baikal section strengthens that advance without giving Moscow control of the Japanese terminal or Chinese eastern route. Japan’s army answers from Enkaishū and north through Korea, supplied by sea. The decisive land battle centers on Harbin and the Sungari; Russia is driven toward the Transbaikal border. <!-- CR-040: the Petropavlovsk squadron replaces the Baltic Fleet --> **[APPROVED — CR-040]** At sea Russia has no Pacific port left to fight from. Between 1900 and 1903 it builds a coaling and repair depot at Petropavlovsk in Avacha Bay, and in 1903, under ordinary peacetime orders, two armoured cruisers and a protected cruiser come out to it. They cannot blockade Japan, but through 1904 they raid the transports carrying its army to Enkaishū and Korea, and escorts, diversions and the hunt tie down half of Japan's cruisers. <!-- CR-070: Kiyoshi and the northern cable -->**[APPROVED — CR-070]** In 1904 one of the cruisers comes for the cable that joins Japan to Pacifica by the Kurils and the Aleutians, and Kuroda Kiyoshi keeps it from being cut; the northern wire between the allies stays whole through the war. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** In May 1905, as the squadron returns from its spring cruise, he gathers merchant sightings, port reports and wireless signals and concentrates a stronger cruiser division on it east of Hokkaido. He disables and takes one cruiser with most of its crew. The other two escape damaged to a harbour that cannot repair them, and he does not chase them into the fog. The squadron was the only threat to the army's sea road, and he ends it; at the New Year of 1907 Japan makes him a count for it. Russia's battle fleets never came east and survive, so Russia ends the war with its navy and its trained men intact, and it turns its money and railways west. **[PROPOSED]** Takahashi Korekiyo raises the war loans in San Francisco.

Japan wins the war it fought. Victoria advises it to refuse Theodore Roosevelt’s mediation, and Russia negotiates directly. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Russia's defeat is regional, not a catastrophe, and its revolution has causes of its own. The zemstvo congress demands an elected assembly in November 1904; strikes follow, and Bloody Sunday in January 1905; in October a railway strike becomes a general strike while peasants seize land, and the Tsar concedes a Duma. **[PROPOSED]** A government that needs a peace to govern at home, and Pacifican pressure on French lending, bring Witte to terms: withdrawal from Manchuria, an indemnity, recognition of Japan’s southern gains and renunciation of claims in Korea. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** Witte comes to Urajio, the port Russia called Vladivostok until 1899, to sign away Russia's Pacific there. <!-- CR-081: Japan leaves Manchuria --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Japan's army leaves Manchuria with Russia's.

<!-- CR-039: Korea's post-1905 status is canon -->
Russia retains its northern coast until the later Siberian settlement. Korea remains an independent guaranteed state with Japanese economic predominance and agreed military and transit rights; coordinated foreign policy does not make it a protectorate. It is not annexed in 1910. China retains the Manchurian line with Japanese running rights. **[PROPOSED]** Victoria restrains Japan at the Transbaikal border, leaving the army’s remaining territorial ambition pointed north.

Russian indemnity payments help keep Japan's postwar debt manageable. The working model assigns about $300 million paid in 1906–1910; that amount remains a MODEL estimate rather than an independently fixed treaty clause.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** Silas's nativists raise a school-segregation crisis in 1906. His former adjutant Harlan Tate now runs Sacramento; his board segregates Japanese pupils and revokes the licence of the mission school Silas's mother endowed. Silas, declining into drink, lends the crisis his name and is privately ashamed of it. Roosevelt courts Tokyo and sends the Great White Fleet around Cape Horn to Yokohama, while his immigration restrictions make the courtship unreliable. A more confident Japan bargains hard in the 1907 compact renewal, securing a joint war council, family immigration and civic equality for Japanese Pacificans.

<!-- CR-033: Japanese predominance among Asian immigrants -->
**[APPROVED — CR-033]** Japanese emigrants have been choosing Pacifica in growing numbers, and in the 1900s their arrivals overtake the Chinese. **[PROPOSED]** With Japanese families admitted under the compact, the lead widens, and by 1932 Japanese-origin Pacificans outnumber Chinese-origin ones, about 5.8 against 4.8 million of some 37 million **[MODEL — CR-057, CR-059]**. Filipino migration stays small until the Philippine crown joins Julian's, and grows after it.

The Carnegie purchase gives Theodore Roosevelt a cause for a broader response from 1903. That the richest man in the Union now draws his income from a Pacifican company makes the grievance personal. The Union funds laboratories, technical chairs and universities, develops a central bank after the 1895 gold humiliation, and keeps industrial trusts as national champions. Scholarship bonds and taxation of citizens abroad discourage skilled departure; tariffs, anti-dumping duties and subsidies to the Union's own laboratories constrain Pacifican firms. The response is in full force by 1910 and continues under Taft and Wilson. Pacifica answers by recruiting more skilled Asians. The Union emulates at home and contains abroad through 1932, retaining the capacity and ambition to challenge the order. From 1919 Union investors buy champion bonds and stakes in Pacifican oil terminals and power companies, and containment begins to collide with their portfolios: on Wall Street the "Pacifican interest" argues for peace. **[APPROVED — CR-048]** From 1911 the Parchman Act's oaths turn the programme's own grants against it. <!-- CR-076: the Union and drink --> **[APPROVED — CR-076]** The emulation reaches drink. The Union's drys never carry national prohibition: the Union stays out of the European war, its brewers are never made the enemy's men, and its treasury needs the excise for the laboratories. From 1903 a federal duty graded by strength and, in many states, public liquor stores follow Pacifica's pattern, with the patronage Pacifica's rules keep out. Dry counties and dry states such as Kansas and Maine keep their own prohibition, the drys call the system Thalassia's compromise, and the Office prints the Union states' tables beside its own. <!-- CR-057: Baekeland's fight --> **[APPROVED — CR-057]** At home the open rule is contested. Baekeland's Bakelite of 1907, made in a Polytechnic chair, is licensed to every rival at the same two percent. From his chair he leads the chemical and electrical champions' campaign for exclusive licences, and Tesla answers him at the Five's table. The Senate keeps the rule.

<!-- CR-036: the Nobel decade -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-036]** Between 1905 and 1909 the Nobel committees confirm what Roosevelt's programme fears. Koch wins in 1905, Michelson in 1907 and Ehrlich in 1908, all three as Pacificans. In 1909 Ehrlich and Hata Sahachirō find the arsenical that cures syphilis. The Union's new laboratories are measured against Pacifica's colleges.

Pacifica's capital ships already embody the new naval technology when Dreadnought appears. Oil firing reduces fuel handling and crew demands rather than forcing an emergency rebuilding programme. The MODEL fleet plan reaches fourteen Pacifican capital ships by 1918 and ten Japanese ships, the combined twenty-four that set the 1919 British parity.

Japan’s demographic and industrial weight makes the partnership increasingly unequal. Pacifica supplies oil, wheat, finance, the canal and ocean communications; Japan supplies manpower, officers, yards and a land army. Japanese-origin families rise through the examination state, shipping and imperial contracts. Japanese merchant houses take about a quarter of the champions' ventures abroad, in oil terminals, power companies and mines, so Japanese money owns part of the order long before a treaty says so. They need Japanese credentials and domestic allies as well as common careers. The Hapa generation carries these connections through schools, services and households while racial hierarchy and selective entry remain visible.

In 1905–1906 Julian spends a year in Tokyo attached to the legation and navy, meeting Saionji as he becomes prime minister. Victoria's language education gives the nineteen-year-old access beyond interpreters and formal audiences. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** He also meets Chiyo, of another branch of the Kuroda house.

<!-- CR-040: Silas's renunciation of 1906 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-040]** He comes home to the school crisis, and in 1907 he comes of age. Victoria has delayed the Act of Settlement while the founder's eldest son lives, and Tokyo's terms for renewing the compact in 1907 now include a guarantee that the man whose name stands on Tate's campaign will never stand in the line. In the autumn Lieutenant Tanabe Makoto of Kiyoshi's staff asks Silas for his younger daughter, and she wants to be married in her grandmother's chapel, which Tate's board has closed. Victoria offers Silas a bargain: renounce, and the renewed compact reopens the mission school; refuse, and Tokyo refuses the renewal and the Regent says publicly why. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** In December 1906 Silas renounces, for himself and his descendants, any claim to the crown the Act will create. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Victoria says nothing of Donner: it would expose her own silence and the bargain on which the regency was founded, and she does not need it. She weighs what the renunciation opens, that the founder's second son becomes his eldest claimant, and judges that Tokyo will never back him. Silas does not know she knows.

<!-- CR-081: Victoria and Cixi --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** When the court comes home to Peking in January 1902, Victoria writes to Cixi as one regent to another. Both are widows who have governed for adopted sons, and they negotiated together in 1898–99. In the spring of 1902 Princess Kaʻiulani brings the letter, and Cixi receives her as a royal guest of the Hawaiian house, not as one of the legation wives. Kaʻiulani needs no interpreter, so what Cixi says to her is heard neither by the court's interpreters nor by the Board of Foreign Affairs. Because of her heart she comes to Peking only for the autumn and winter. Guangxu's empress, Cixi's niece, is her sponsor among the court ladies. The Grand Council under Prince Qing tolerates a channel to Victoria that brings Pacifican credit and asks for no appointments; the Manchu princes who want the army and the revenue in Manchu hands, Tieliang among them, distrust her. About 1903 the court photographer Xunling photographs Cixi holding her hand at the Summer Palace, the court sends prints to the legations, and the *Examiner* prints the picture. In 1905 she brings Victoria's assurance that Japan's army will leave Manchuria, and once the peace keeps it Cixi uses her for what she does not want in the Board's files. In the spring of 1906 Victoria visits the Summer Palace herself and meets Guangxu's empress, the future Empress Dowager Longyu. The friendship lends money and advice, never troops or warships, and never asks Cixi to change her court; Japan trains the New Army's officers. In 1905 the revolutionaries' league, the Tongmenghui, is founded in Tokyo, where China's students are.

**[APPROVED — CR-081]** Pacifican money and the Realm's model reach the New Policies. Cixi keeps the gradual plan of 1903–04 for the examinations, and from 1906 the old degrees are awarded by new-subject state examinations under a board of the new Ministry of Education. Its examiners are Chinese degree-holders and new-school graduates appointed by the throne, the papers are marked blind by two readers on the Polytechnic's method, the halls are the old ones and the degrees keep their names, so the gentry's sons keep their road into office. In May 1906 a Revenue Council takes over the customs service, with Tang Shaoyi as associate minister and Fong reporting to him, and China's first national budget comes sooner. Dai Hongci and Duanfang's constitutional mission stops at Thalassia after Washington, and the Outline of 1908 keeps Japan's model for the throne and sets a parliament for 1912. A Pacifican loan of 1908 pays for the edicts of 1907 that capitalise the provincial bannermen's stipends and grant them land, on the pattern of the Realm's Land Fund. Its service comes from the stipend funds the provinces no longer pay, with the customs balance as second security, so the garrisons convert in four or five years and by 1912 most provincial bannermen hold land or bonds. **[MODEL — CR-081]** The stipends had cost the provinces 3–5 million taels a year; the loan's service is 2–3.5 million once fully drawn. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** From 1906 Cixi brings Guangxu into the Grand Council's business and means him to reign under the constitution, and when Victoria lays down her regency in November 1907 Cixi has seen a regency end without a coup.

**[APPROVED — CR-081]** From 1904 Pacifican credit pays for Pingxiang's coke and its haulage, so the Hanyeping works can service their Japanese loans while the Daye ore still goes to Yawata under its contract, its price reviewed. From 1908 Hunan's Huachang company refines antimony at Changsha, and from the 1910s Jiangxi's small miners dig tungsten; both trades are Chinese-owned and sell to every buyer, with K. C. Li as their trader. In 1906 Chin Gee Hee, a Chinese Pacifican of Seattle, begins the Sun Ning Railway in Taishan with emigrants' money. **[MODEL — CR-081]** By 1911 China's state lines run 11,000–12,000 kilometres. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** A few thousand Pacifican-trained engineers and officials are in China's service.

The Polytechnic of Formosa opens at Taipei in 1906, the year the old degrees are first awarded by new-subject examinations, with engineering at Keelung and Boxer-indemnity scholarships. Chinese and English teaching and examinations in mathematics, engineering, law and administration train officials for Chinese ministries, railways and Pacifican firms. Its graduates carry both useful standards and the nationalism that may turn against foreign administration. <!-- CR-081: the examination's door --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Its graduates, like any Chinese scholar, may sit the Realm's examination on the same terms as anyone. A scholarship China funds, the remitted indemnity's among them, binds its holder to China and not to the Realm; the Realm binds only those whose places it pays for. Its posts of trust go to entrants who live in the Realm and take the Crown's oath, which admits no office, pay or rank under a state other than the crowns and Japan, and an entrant brings a spouse and children, never a lineage.

<!-- CR-040: Kiyoshi's claim and the Act of Settlement, 1907 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-040]** With Silas out of the line, Kuroda Kiyoshi claims the crown the Act is to create. Early in 1907, Count Kuroda and commander of the western squadron, he petitions the Regent as the founder's eldest son who has not renounced, and asks that the crown follow the founder's blood. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** He offers to reign only for his lifetime, with Julian after him; Victoria sees that an eldest-son rule would carry the crown to his Kuroda son. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** He needs to deny Julian two-thirds; outlasting a Regent of sixty-eight is no plan. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** He lets it be known that if the Act passes over him, no officer of the naval party will accept the western squadron's command. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** The squadron anchors off Kāneʻohe Bay on a courtesy visit, in sight of Thalassia, and he gives no order.

His coalition comes apart piece by piece. The ʻAha Aliʻi will proclaim only Julian, and Thalassia stands on the Hawaiian throne's land. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Saionji's government will not let a count of Japan take a crown equal to the Emperor's. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** The crown would cost Kiyoshi the house he founded and the name Japan gave him. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** Victoria publishes the memorial; Tate will not be seen with the Japanese pretender and votes against the Act alone. The examined young officers of the naval party will not strike; **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Lieutenant Tanabe, who is to marry Silas's daughter, tells the count so to his face. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** Julian goes to Pearl Harbor without an escort and asks his brother, in Japanese, for a wife from his house. Kiyoshi accepts and renounces the crown for himself and his descendants. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** He renounces in March 1907. Chiyo and her branch give their own assent, and the compact renewed later that year makes Kiyoshi Japanese Fleet-General: the price of the crown he gave up is the command of the common fleet.

**[APPROVED — CR-040]** The 1907 Act of Settlement establishes the Crown of Pacifica in the House of Thorne-Kamehameha and joins to it the Hawaiian crown Victoria has worn since 1872. The Act dissolves the Territorial Council and restores Hawaii's legislature. **[APPROVED — CR-029, redated by CR-040]** Victoria will not join her crown without asking her people, as she did not ask them in 1874. The restored legislature and the ʻAha Aliʻi put the union to a plebiscite of Hawaiian subjects, and the loyalists campaign against it. <!-- CR-072: the loyalists come in --> **[APPROVED — CR-072]** When the union carries, the loyalists come in with the restored crown. **[APPROVED — CR-029, redated by CR-040]** The ʻAha Aliʻi proclaims Julian heir to the Hawaiian crown, to reign as Kamehameha VI; Senate and Chamber confer the Realm's sovereignty by two-thirds. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** In November 1907 Victoria lays down both the regency and her crown, and Julian, about twenty-one, accedes to both. Her long government has prepared the ruler who will make its institutions his own. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Hawaiian is restored as the Crown's language and in its schools. The ʻAha Aliʻi keeps custody of Hawaiian crown and kuleana lands; Thalassia's ground is confirmed as land of the Hawaiian throne. The Polytechnic retains its charter. <!-- CR-074: the Hawaiian companies --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** The restored legislature passes an act of its own for Hawaiian companies.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** In the same months the Philippine Cortes offers the Philippine crown, and a plebiscite confirms the offer. Its terms are signed before the vote, because Filipinos remember what was promised at Biak-na-Bato and never written down. Calderón's organic law gives the kingdom a single-chamber Cortes with a permanent commission, its own budget and army, and provinces under Gobernadores who are never posted to their home province. Neither crown is subordinate. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** The Realm takes over what remains of the Redemption Loan at its 1907 balance **[MODEL]**. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The Crown of Pacifica becomes a crown made of three sources: Hawaiian rank, a Filipino people's offer and the Senate's law. Each crown keeps its own legislature, language of the Crown and coronation, and the alternat names each first in its own texts. A Kuhina Nui in Honolulu and a Lugarteniente General in Manila govern day to day in the Sovereign's name, on his instructions and without the old veto. Ka Lōkahi, the council of the crowns, gives the Realm, Hawaii and the Philippines one vote each over the succession, the agreed matters renewed every ten years and disputes between them. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Its first review falls in 1917. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Hawaii votes for the union narrowly; the Philippines, broadly. <!-- CR-074: the crowns' companies --> **[APPROVED — CR-074]** The Philippine crown takes over the islands' companies from the Philippine Council. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** Julian's style becomes Sovereign of Pacifica, Mōʻī of Hawaiʻi, Rey de Filipinas, and each land promulgates it with its own crown first. **[APPROVED — CR-029, redated by CR-040]** In December 1907 he swears before the Cortes in Manila, as Spanish kings swore, and is proclaimed under the raised standard. No bishop crowns him. At the Te Deum in the Cathedral, sung by the first Filipino archbishop, Victoria, Kīnaʻu's daughter, sits in a pew and sings the one hymn her church shares with Rome.

Succession passes to the eldest son, then eldest daughter, otherwise to an heir adopted with Senate consent. Noe's Maui chiefly descent strengthens Julian's claim alongside his adoption by a Kamehameha queen. Loyalists dispute whether a hānai son may continue the Kamehameha line. The Act heals much politically, but does not reverse the Supreme Court's ignored judgment of 1876. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** The Act recites Kiyoshi's renunciation and names him "Kuroda Kiyoshi, son of Elias Thorne", the only public document that ever does. He never uses the name.

In November 1907 Julian marries Lady Chiyo, a Japanese woman of the kazoku and another branch of the Kuroda house. Kiyoshi arranges the match, binding the naval party to his half-brother’s throne. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** The match is the price of his renunciation: his house supplies the queen, and the heir after Julian is of Kuroda blood. Chiyo’s Japanese standing protects the imperial class from appearing to have abandoned Japan. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Their first children are daughters, and for eight years the Kuroda bargain waits on a son. He is born about 1916. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** He is named Kahekili for Noe’s Maui line, with the Japanese name 雷, Ikazuchi, given by meaning through Chiyo’s family. The heir will reign in Hawaii as Kamehameha VII; he is distinct from Elias and Victoria’s lost Kalanimoku. Japan’s succession law excludes him, so the shared dynasty does not merge the crowns.

**[PROPOSED — CR-022]** At his accession the regency's sealed papers pass to Julian. Looking for the truth of his mother's death, he finds instead the truth of Donner, kept by the woman who raised him. He tells no one that he knows. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Silas keeps the inland command until 1912 and never learns that his sovereign has read the file.

The growing dynasty gives each public a different account of the partnership. Japanese supporters can see a half-Japanese heir to come and Japanese admirals at its summit; Pacificans can see Japan entering institutions their state created. The Austro-Hungarian constitutional template will become useful only when the later financial and political crises make a formal Concord acceptable.
