# PART VII — The Concord, the crisis and the Pacific order (1921–1932)

## 33. China’s reunification and the Pacific Concord (1921–1929)

<!-- CR-081: canon tension 1, case A --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Pacifican money supports Whampoa and Sun’s movement without a Soviet bargain, since no Soviet state survives 1920. After Sun dies in 1925, Chiang Kai-shek succeeds him. The Northern Expedition leads to reunification under the Nationalists.

<!-- CR-081: works, warlords and the customs, 1920–1923 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-081]** Pacifica lends to no faction in Peking. Its works credit goes to whoever holds a province, if the province publishes its accounts and accepts the labour article on the sites, and in practice that rewards Hunan under its constitution of 1922 and Chen Jiongming's Guangdong. From 1920 a regional works share of the customs surplus is divided by published percentages for the regions where the revenue is collected and paid to contractors, never to armies; Canton's part pays for the Pearl River works, the Swatow warnings and the Whampoa port. **[MODEL — CR-081]** The share is about 3–7 million taels a year in the 1920s, Canton's part well under one million. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** In March 1921 Peking pledges its surplus to its internal loans, and Fong declines to act as its agent for internal debt. The pledge works through a fund held by the Bank of China and the Bank of Communications, Peking's bonds trade low, and Pacifican banks, the Bank of California first, buy them for their yield. When Zhang Zuolin declares the Three Eastern Provinces autonomous in 1922 after his defeat by the Zhili generals, he leaves Newchwang and Antung in the Inspectorate, because the works share reaches Manchuria and none of the customs serves Peking's debt. Under his finance director Wang Yongjiang the provinces publish their accounts from 1922, and Fengtian keeps the labour article on the works sites, though not in its own mines and arsenal. When Wang resigns in 1926 as war spending runs out of control, no new works contract is signed with Mukden, and the existing ones run on. Early in 1922 Sun, preparing a northern expedition from Shaoguan, asks Pacifica's consul at Canton to have the Pearl River payments made to his headquarters. The consul answers that they go to the contractors under the contract with the provincial government, which is Chen's, and Sun says in public that Pacifica pays Chen. On 16 June Chen's troops shell Sun's residence. In Hunan Mao Zedong, a graduate of the First Normal School, takes part in the self-government movement of 1920 and turns against Governor Zhao Hengti after the governor's men kill the labour organisers Huang Ai and Pang Renquan in January 1922. He joins the Nationalists in Hunan. The labour article in the contracts of the Pacifican-financed Pingxiang works is read in the night schools at the Anyuan collieries, and the strike there in September 1922 demands its terms. Mao uses the article as a promise the employer and the governor have signed, and argues that a promise ending at the edge of the Pacifican-financed sites leaves the rest of Hunan's workers outside it.

<!-- CR-081: Canton, Chiang and Whampoa, 1923–1924 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-081]** After Chen's attack Sun sends Chiang Kai-shek to Pacifica by way of Tokyo for about three months in 1923, to learn how to train officers whose loyalty survives a quarrel with their commander. Chiang sees the Companies' school at Sacramento, the Commissions Board, the Polytechnic, a county board and the Party's machine, and brings back one recommendation: commissions granted on examination by the party's military council, never by the general commanding, and cadets and junior officers paid from an account the commanders do not hold. In Tokyo he looks up officers he served with at Takada and his classmates of the Shinbu Gakkō, as personal acquaintances. In December 1923 Sun threatens to seize the rest of the Canton customs surplus, which belongs to Peking, and accuses Fong by name of keeping China's money for foreign bondholders. The Customs' own Chinese guards of the preventive service hold the customs house for the recognised government, and the Pacific Company in the service waits at Whampoa's anchorage and is never seen at the customs house. Fong publishes Canton's share and its payments since 1920, and his defenders point to the Office's printed accounts of the grant Sun's party has taken for six years. Sun backs down, saying he will not have his soldiers fire on the Customs' guards over money Canton's works already receive, and takes the customs question to his party's congress; his province keeps its share. Other holders of customs ports, Zhang at Newchwang and Antung and the Zhili generals at Tianjin and Hankou, see in the share a reason to leave their ports in the Inspectorate. Britain's minister has the diplomatic body confirm the percentages of 1920 about the start of 1924, so that no Inspector-General can change them alone. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** The Kuomintang's congress of January 1924 takes from the Prosperity Party examined cadres appointed from the party schools' published lists, branches supervised by the organisation department rather than the provincial commander, and accounts published twice a year and audited by the Central Supervisory Committee, and it starts a news wire of its own. It declares the equality of the sexes and sets up a Women's Bureau, and it refuses contested elections and the secret ballot above the county during tutelage. Whampoa opens in June 1924 on Chiang's rule, with the grant's academy line paid into its own account. Its instructors are mostly Chinese officers from Baoding and Japan's academies; a few are Pacifican-trained Chinese who have left the Companies, and no Pacific Company serves Canton. The warlords' accounts look worse beside the party's, and its own officers resent audits they cannot vote against.

<!-- CR-081: Peking's coup, the strike and the Expedition, 1924–1926 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-081]** On 23 October 1924 Feng Yuxiang turns back from the Rehe front and takes Peking in a night. Thalassia has seen the strain in the circular telegrams and the consuls' reports, and the Chinese Pacifican bankers in Peking have reported his men's arrears, but the decision is taken in private, and Thalassia learns of it when the Peking telegraph office stops answering. When Feng's men take Puyi's title and allowance and drive him from the Forbidden City on 5 November, Pacifica invokes the third term of 1911 at Victoria's request for his safety only, obtains an undertaking for his person and his property, and offers no asylum; he goes to the Japanese legation and in 1925 to Tianjin. The coup brings Sun north, and he dies in Peking on 12 March 1925. After the May Thirtieth shooting the Canton–Hong Kong strike runs from June 1925 to October 1926 under a committee of the Seamen's Union and the Nationalists' labour bureau chaired by Su Zhaozheng, aimed at Britain and at the Japanese mills where the shooting began. About 250,000 people leave Hong Kong. Relief comes from Canton's treasury, the committee's permit fees and the Chinese Pacificans' subscriptions; **[MODEL — CR-081]** they come to about 6 million yuan a year. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Any carrier that keeps away from Hong Kong and Shamian, carries no British goods and pays its Chinese crews the same wage as its other crews gets a permit, so the China Merchants' Pacific line and the Pacifican lines qualify, and London calls it collusion. From that summer the cipher room reads British official traffic in China and the Pacific, the legation at Peking, the consulates, Hong Kong's government and the China Station, because Britain is not a treaty ally and Thalassia needs to know whether it will act at Canton. Pacifica has kept to the powers' arms embargo since 1919, and no Soviet arms come; the embargo lapses on 26 April 1929, after Nanjing's recognition. The Northern Expedition sets out on 9 July 1926 with about 100,000 men, armed from the Canton arsenal, by German and Bohemian private sellers and with arms taken in 1925. The army grows mostly by defections. Tang Shengzhi, who has driven Zhao Hengti out of Changsha, joins as commander of the Eighth Corps and leads the advance up the Xiang. His price is the governorship of Hunan, support against Wu Peifu, his army kept whole and Hunan's works share paid to contractors under his government. The Expedition's dependence on him puts him on Wuhan's side in 1927. Canton ends the strike on 10 October 1926, the day Wuchang falls, and pays the strikers from its own surtax.

<!-- CR-081: the split of 1927 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-081]** The Nationalists split in 1927 over land and labour, with no Communists in either camp. At Wuhan the left (Wang Jingwei, the peasant and labour bureaus and Deng Yanda, the Expedition's political chief) stands against Chiang at Nanjing, who has the Shanghai bankers and the officers whose families own land. Britain returns its concessions at Hankou and Jiujiang by agreement in February and March. Mao's report of March on the Hunan peasant movement, a Nationalist document, claims the associations as the countryside's authority over rents, disputes and militia. On 24 March Nationalist soldiers attack the consulates at Nanjing and British and American warships shell the city; Nanjing settles with the Union in March 1928, with Britain later that year and with Japan on 2 May 1929. In Shanghai the General Labour Union's armed pickets have held the Chinese city since the rising of 21 March; on 12–13 April Chiang has them disarmed, with Green Gang men in the work, closes the union's offices and arrests or exiles its leaders. The arrests are of the union's own leaders, and the dead number in the tens. In Hunan the officers who turn on the peasant associations after 21 May are men of Tang's corps under Xu Kexiang. Outside the Canton–Hankow line's works districts the repression is the commanders' and the landlords' militia's; inside them the commanders hold back, because the works contracts would stop, and the landlords hold land whose value the works raise, so the associations are dissolved and their leaders dismissed. At Xiangtan, Mao's home county, Xu's men kill the association leaders anyway. The contractors there are paid as before, because the rule binds the contracts and not the army, and Mao says so in print. After the coup he could arm the associations and take to the hills; with no other party, no army and the works districts before him, he stays with the party, gives up the associations' claim to govern, their militia and the seizure of land, and keeps the rent limit and the registered lease. Wuhan rejoins Nanjing in September, with its notes collapsing, Feng on Nanjing's side since June and Chiang resigned in August; it gets a joint special committee and the party's resolution of October 1926 on reducing rents, and gives up its armed peasant and labour units. Tang refuses to submit, Nanjing's western campaign of October and November breaks him, and he leaves for Japan. The paths of Chen Duxiu, Li Dazhao and Zhou Enlai remain **[OPEN]**.

<!-- CR-081: Jinan and the Concord's request, 1928 --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Tanaka's cabinet sends troops to Shandong in May 1927 and April 1928 for Japan's residents, outside the Concord's command. After the clash at Jinan on 3 May 1928 Chiang's armies go round the city, and a short boycott answers it. In May the Concord asks both sides jointly to keep the war out of Manchuria. Zhang Zuolin, Grand Marshal in Peking since June 1927, withdraws without a battle, the Nationalists stop at the Wall, and Yan Xishan's army takes Peking. Japanese officers assassinate Zhang Zuolin in June; Zhang Xueliang takes Manchuria and joins the Nationalist state. The officers act against their own government's signature, so the murder breaks the Concord's undertaking. At Jinan Julian spends influence to obtain a Japanese withdrawal. His settlement that summer has Nanjing guarantee the Japanese residents and their property, garrison Jinan and the railway zone with provincial troops and accept a joint claims commission with a Pacifican umpire, and Tokyo withdraw within two months, by about the end of August, with regrets on both sides instead of an apology. A joint commission of the war council's Pacifican and Japanese staff officers certifies each stage, and Chiang's Tokyo acquaintances carry the first messages. Zhang Xueliang invokes the Concord's undertaking when Manchuria joins Nanjing in December, Pacifica raises it at the Council at Hayama in 1929, and Tanaka's cabinet, which will not punish the officers, falls in July 1929. **[PROPOSED]** His father’s killers make him hostile to Japan’s demand for a territorial corridor, and both sides need Thalassia’s restraint.

The Manchurian railway stays Chinese while carrying Japan's lifeline between eastern holdings and Transbaikalia. **[PROPOSED]** Guaranteed transit and joint railway management answer the army's territorial demands. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** In 1928, after Jinan, the Nationalist government signs the Treaty of Nanjing with the Concord. The Concord guarantees China's frontiers, Manchuria included, and China guarantees the railway transit and its joint management. <!-- CR-081: no differential rate --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** The joint management may set no rate that favours a shipper or a destination. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** Under the treaty China recovers the Inspectorate and customs in 1928–1929, and with them the tariff the Treaty of Nanking took from it in 1842. <!-- CR-081: accession, Fong's successor and the company's return --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Nanjing accedes to the standing alliance of 1922 on the ground that the Chinese state continues; it keeps the Harbin gains and the Peking-era railway bonds and repudiates only the Anfu loans. **[PROPOSED — CR-081]** The accession is a clause of the treaty. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Fong, seventy, hands the Inspectorate to China, and in 1929 Nanjing appoints Ting Kwei Tang Inspector-General. He is a Customs College graduate who served at Antung and in the Peking Inspectorate, and has been Fong's named deputy since about 1926; by then Chinese commissioners hold most ports. The Pacific Company's commission in the preventive service ends at the handover and the company goes home, and China keeps individual Pacifican specialists on its own contracts. Nanjing settles with the holders of Peking's internal bonds at a discount, **[MODEL — CR-081]** and the Pacifican banks' loss on them is Pacifica's largest in China before 1932. **[PROPOSED]** The Formosan administration remains as credit security but becomes an affair of the Concord: China commissions both a Pacifican Director-General and a Japanese deputy. <!-- CR-081: Formosa's petitioners --> **[APPROVED — CR-081]** The gentry associations of the western counties and the Polytechnic of Formosa's graduates petition the Chinese governor for an elected assembly to vote the provincial budget and be consulted on the fee and on the administration's end. From about 1926 the governor consults an elected advisory council, and the Director-General publishes the receipts, the prior charges and the fee each month and answers the council once a year. The council claims the vote on the province's surplus, and the governor keeps the last word. **[PROPOSED]** The Japanese deputy holds a Chinese commission and declines to answer a provincial council, and the petitioners call him a second foreign officer. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** Formosa's sugar and rice go to Japan on long contracts. The administration is to end, once the indemnity and the loans are paid, after 1932. **[PROPOSED — CR-042]** The Huludao Harbour Trust is not made a Concord affair. It stays under its Chinese commission, with a Pacifican director, until its last bonds are paid in the 1930s, and then passes to China's own harbour board. **[PROPOSED]** Japan's old grievance becomes a shared asset while China still resents foreign control. The Polytechnic becomes a joint Sino-Pacifican foundation, retaining its examination, beside Japan's school opened in 1928 with a joint board. Fong has helped finance the generation that demands recovery of its own institutions.

The Concord grows from a relationship as well as financial necessity. In 1921 Crown Prince Hirohito returns from Europe through the Atlantic, Pacifica's canal and Thalassia. Weeks of informal hospitality let him speak to Julian in Japanese without an interpreter. He dives Kāneʻohe's reef once, during that visit. Julian founds the marine station on Moku o Loʻe; its zoologists exchange specimens with the prince's laboratory and work with him at Hayama. Hirohito becomes Regent in November 1921. In September 1923 Julian brings relief and reconstruction credit to Yokohama, and they meet in the ruins. The earthquake also destroys the collateral behind loans already weakened since the war. Reconstruction brings back orders and work but leaves the old losses on the books. Pacifican suppliers, banks and investors tie themselves more closely to Japan's recovery, and the gratitude between the two households grows alongside the debts between their banks. After Hirohito marries Nagako in 1924, Chiyo becomes her confidante. Marine science, absent fathers and the burden of inherited institutions give the households common ground; Julian's personal rule differs from Hirohito's constitutional caution. **[APPROVED — CR-048]** The station's first director, from 1922, is Ernest Everett Just.

<!-- CR-039: Puʻuloa after 1924 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-039]** In March 1924 the fifty-year lease of Puʻuloa runs out, and Julian does not renew it. As Mōʻī he takes the harbour back into the Hawaiian crown; as Sovereign he keeps the fleet there as the crown's tenant, at the Mōʻī's pleasure, the tenancy renewed at each accession. To Hawaiians it is the first return of anything taken since 1874. In fact it puts the fleet's base, and the Japanese naval party's anchorage, in his personal keeping, beyond the Senate's votes and Tokyo's reach. Senators call it a prerogative over the navy, and loyalists say a king cannot be his own landlord. Victoria, who would not join her crown in 1907 without asking her people, notes that her son has asked no one. Julian writes conditions of his own into the grant. Puʻuloa's fishponds and pearl beds are to be restored, and the waters of Kaʻahupahau, the shark who guards the lochs, are closed to dredging. The zoologists of his marine station survey the beds.

<!-- CR-052, CR-055: the power seat, the line and the big dams -->
**[APPROVED — CR-052]** In the same year Tesla leaves the Five, and Harris J. Ryan takes the power seat. Born in Pennsylvania in 1866, he came west with his family in the 1870s, was examined at the Polytechnic, and has run its high-voltage laboratory since the 1890s. He is the first holder of the seat raised in Pacifica, and a line-builder where Tesla was a machine-builder, in the years when the seat's work is transmission. **[APPROVED — CR-055]** His great work is the Columbia–Sierra line, decided in 1919 and energised in 1924: 287 kilovolts with series compensation over 750 miles, a decade ahead of any line elsewhere, built because the Columbia's power has more uses than the Northwest can make of it. It takes the river's surplus first and carries it to the Sierra's electrochemical district and the halls. **[APPROVED — CR-052]** So that the Northwest's own mills are not starved by it, the power estate adds a second powerhouse at Bonneville and enlarges Rocky Reach from 1925, the new units running on the spring freshet that would otherwise go over the spillways, and the pulp, paper and lumber mills of the Northwest raise their steam in electric boilers on that secondary power. Boulder Canyon is decided in 1920 and gives first power in 1926: Nevada, Arizona and the Western Slope are all Pacifican, so no state has to be bargained with, and Los Angeles takes the power over Ryan's 275-mile line. **[OPEN]** The consent of the peoples of the Boulder reach, under the land compacts of CR-033, and its terms.

**[APPROVED — CR-055]** The river's power is put to purposes that only this country has. From 1920 the Portland works hydrogenates California's heavy residue, brought north by tanker, with the Columbia's electrolytic hydrogen, and turns fuel oil into motor fuel. From 1923 Tacoma makes synthetic rubber from carbide and acetylene, Pacifica's answer to Britain's Stevenson restriction scheme of 1922, which rations the world's rubber in the very years of the confrontation with London. From 1922 Portland and Tacoma smelt pig iron electrically, as Norway and Sweden do where coal is far, on Philippine and Chilean ore for the carrier yards. **[PROPOSED — CR-055]** From about 1925 the yards weld the Concord's carriers and cruisers with the arc on the same power. **[MODEL — CR-055; re-synced by CR-057]** By 1929 the hydrogenation works takes about 9 million barrels of residue a year and gains about 5 million barrels of motor fuel; Tacoma makes about 25,000 tonnes of rubber, half its peak, as the carbide turns to chemicals, and the yards' electric pig iron is about half a million tonnes.

<!-- CR-058: the Columbia compact and the halibut treaty -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** The Columbia rises in British Columbia, and the firm power of the river's dams depends on storage upstream, at the Arrow Lakes, Kootenay Lake and later Kinbasket. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** In 1922–1925 **[APPROVED — CR-058]** a compact gives British Columbia half of the extra power its storage makes downstream, and payments for flood control besides, so BC's revenue is tied to Pacifica's grid and Pacifica's power to BC's storage, with Pacifica the larger partner. The rule that no dam is built that salmon cannot pass keeps the upper Columbia's runs alive into British Columbia, and a dam that would flood across the line, on the Kootenay in Pacifican Montana or on the Skagit, waits for BC's consent and compensation. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** Whether the half is paid in power or in gold is undecided. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** The western commission has kept the Fraser's sockeye alive since the slide at Hells Gate in 1913–1914, when the Canadian Northern's blasting choked the river and fishways were built at once. In 1923 Canada signs the Pacific Halibut Treaty with Pacifica, the first treaty it signs without Britain beside it. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** Ontario Hydro and Pacifica's public power estate each prove the other's case against the Union's private utilities.

<!-- CR-052: homes, wires and motors -->
**[APPROVED — CR-052]** The counties borrow from the Development Fund, beside private lenders, for their grids and carry the wire to farms as well as towns; **[PROPOSED — CR-052]** the private companies, which wanted only the towns' load, lose the rural franchises, and the towns' coal and gas dealers lose the kitchen. **[APPROVED — CR-052]** On cheap hydro Pacificans cook and heat their water electrically, as Ontario's households do. California becomes the most motorized place on earth. **[MODEL — CR-052; re-synced by CR-057]** By 1929 about 96 per cent of homes are wired, using about 1,700 kilowatt-hours a year each; the Realm has about one motor vehicle for every three people and California one for every two and a half, against about one for every four and a half in the Union, and Pacifica builds about 2.3 million vehicles a year. Electricity per person is about two and a half times the Union's through the 1920s, about 2,600 kilowatt-hours a head in 1929; three quarters of it comes from water. Even so, about three tenths of the Realm's primary energy is imported by 1929, almost all of it oil.

<!-- CR-054: the energy-intensive champions -->
**[APPROVED — CR-054]** The industries that use electricity on a national scale grow with it, and their output is meant for the Realm and the Concord rather than for export. The aluminium champion's first customer is the grid: every long line is strung with steel-cored aluminium conductor, a decade before it is general elsewhere. Its next is the Concord's air arm, all-metal from the mid-1920s, then the Concord pool that supplies Japan, which has no aluminium industry of its own, and civil uses at home; about a third is sold beyond the Concord. Its bauxite comes from Arkansas, in the Gulf Republic, through the Gulf compact, and the Republic gets the alumina refinery and the royalty. **[PROPOSED — CR-054]** The refinery stands on the Gulf coast, at Baton Rouge or on Mobile Bay. **[APPROVED — CR-054]** The Columbia carries most of the tonnage, at Wenatchee, the Rock Island reach and Rocky Reach; the Sierra's smelters keep their 1920 size, and the Hilo smelter is the showcase, the clean smelter on film, not the largest plant. Electrolytic ammonia on the Columbia's cells and Haber–Bosch on Sacramento Valley gas feed the Central Valley, the Imperial Valley and the Columbia Basin, Hawaiʻi's and the Philippines' sugar and rice, and Korea and the China trade, but not Japan, which fixes its own nitrogen with Pacifican turbines. Electric steel makes the alloys of the aircraft and electrical champions. **[MODEL — CR-054]** In 1929 the champion makes about 150,000 tonnes of aluminium, half the world's output, and the cells and the Valley plants about 90,000 tonnes of nitrogen; about an eighth of the Realm's steel is electric.

<!-- CR-057: the Reserve Bank's rule and falling prices --> **[APPROVED — CR-057]** The Reserve Bank keeps the dollar convertible and watches its cover and its own notes and deposits, never an index of prices. Power, aluminium, steel, cars and film get cheaper year after year, and the state works to make every input cheaper where that unlocks the next industry. Money wages hold or rise. Interest rates are low and move with what savers will lend for. Margins are tight, and the champions live by cutting costs faster than prices. Cheap Pacifican metal and machines bring calls in London and the Union for duties against dumping. At gold parity Japan's prices must follow Pacifica's down while its farmers' debts do not, and the Restoration officers say that Japan's villages pay for San Francisco's prosperity.

<!-- CR-057: the chartered districts at home --> **[APPROVED — CR-057]** From 1920 the Realm charters industrial districts where any firm, Pacifican or foreign, gets power at cost, serviced leasehold land, a licence within weeks, bonded customs for goods made to be sold abroad, and a five-year holiday from tax on a new plant. The first are on the Columbia at Portland, Tacoma and Wenatchee and on the Los Angeles harbour. The Hawaiian crown charters its own leeward districts under the Thalassia Plan of 1928.

<!-- CR-057: sterling's return --> **[APPROVED — CR-057]** In 1925 Pacifica, holding about a fifth of the world's monetary gold **[MODEL — CR-057]**, backs sterling's return at the old parity. Its banks and champions hold sterling in the City, and a convertible pound is how Britain earns the gold dollars it owes. Its gold credit to the Bank of England can be withdrawn, and London knows it through every year of the confrontation. The deflation that follows in Britain is partly San Francisco's interest, and Pacifica's economists say so in private.

<!-- CR-051: the contained leak — the Tribune's Sierra story, 1922 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** In the spring of 1922 the *Chicago Tribune* prints a story from the Sierra under a large headline: a machinist from the valley's workshops, drinking in Groveland, has told a stranger that the Station is making something that will blow a battleship out of the water, and the stranger has sold it east. The paper sets beside it the halls' power from the Office's own tables and the Works' ore buying from the trade press: four years after the war the Sierra's electrochemical load is higher than at the height of the munitions effort, and the Works is building dams on two rivers. Every figure is true. Pacifica issues no denial, because it may not lie, and Hearst's papers do not take the story up. Europe dismisses it, because it comes from the paper that also blames Pacifica for the rump's troubles; Rutherford, asked by a London journalist, says honestly that he knows of no such thing and that a bomb of the kind described would need a factory no country could build. The machinist is found through the post office and the guardhouse log, tried before the Polytechnic estate's court under the bond, and loses his pension rights; he serves out the term on full pay at a remote station of the Works, without leave. Pacifica is not a gentle state, but it does not make men disappear. The story goes into a British file, and into Szilárd's argument, two years later, that the seal's term must end.

<!-- CR-051: Europe looks; Lindemann and Tizard -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** After the story the Admiralty asks the Secret Intelligence Service what is known: the salt trust's purchases, the halls' power, the Works' recruiting, a closed valley in the Sierra. Two friends read it two ways. Frederick Lindemann, Oxford's professor of experimental philosophy, sees the salt, electrochemistry and a rich state's taste for secrecy: aluminium, carbide and nitrogen, a country merely modernizing. Henry Tizard, at the Department of Scientific and Industrial Research, reads the Office's tables and sees that the Sierra district's nitrogen output fell after the war and its power did not, so the power went to something else. He keeps the file open and asks for river-water samples below the Tuolumne. The samples taken in 1923 show nothing, because the research installation is small and its effluent held, and the file closes. What any service can learn on the Columbia is that the Works runs an isotope station beside a new aluminium smelter, with a powerhouse and a dam upriver, all of it true. The Kaiser Wilhelm Society reads the Tribune and laughs; the Union's revanchists believe it and are not believed.

<!-- CR-050, CR-051: the 1924 test, in true words -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050, CR-050-R1]** In May 1924 the Works tests its first device in secret on the Black Rock Desert in north-western Nevada, never on Hawaiian reefs, because Julian honours his Hawaiian culture; the playa is closed for the Works' trials. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** O'Shaughnessy, who does not like being second to a soldier, builds the site, and Samuel Wilder King, a part-Hawaiian naval ordnance officer from Honolulu, trained at Pacifica's own naval school, arms the device. The Northern Paiute and Shoshone camps and the ranches downwind are told truthfully that the Works will test an explosive of very great power and that dust from it may be harmful, and they are paid to move for the month. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The measurements of what the winds carry north-east go into the sealed record. Hillebrand lives to see it work. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** The flash and the shock reach Gerlach, the Western Pacific's trains and the ranches, and the seismographs at Berkeley and Lick record it. The same morning the Works and the General Staff publish one sentence: *"This morning the Light and Matter Works tested an explosive of a new kind and very great power on the Black Rock Desert, far from any settlement; the area is closed until further notice."* Every word is true. Europe cannot simply read it as chemical: it has had fission since 1920, the neutron count since 1921 and the Tribune's story since 1922, and the announcement comes from an atomic firm. Tizard reopens his file and asks Cambridge what such a device would need. Lindemann answers that the separation factory is impossible, so the shot must have been a very large chemical charge, or theatre to frighten London over its debt. Europe can imagine the bomb; it cannot imagine the factory. The Tribune claims vindication and is again not believed.

<!-- CR-051: the memo nobody funds; the forecasts; Hoover's exit; the margins -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Tizard's question goes to Ralph Fowler, a Cambridge theorist wounded at Gallipoli, and once early quantum mechanics allows a fast-neutron estimate Fowler calculates that separated light uranium in the range of kilograms to tens of kilograms would explode, and that separating it would need a plant larger than any chemical works in the Empire, on power Britain does not have to spare. In 1926 the Treasury under Churchill declines the factory, on Lindemann's advice that it is impossible in peacetime and probably anywhere and that the Black Rock shot was chemical or theatre. Europe has reached the first hard fact on paper and disbelieves the second.

**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Inside the wall the years run on. In 1925 Hoover leaves the ore office for public office, still under his bond: an inner-circle man who must answer the Senate's questions about the Works' purchases without a false word. Szilárd, admitted to the inner circle about 1924, calls the signposts lying with true sentences. About 1926 a gaseous-diffusion pilot runs inside the wall. That year the Chief tells the keys that with Joachimsthal, Shinkolobwe and Fergana in hand no one will build a device for twenty years. Szilárd answers five, once they know it works, and Hoover, who has found ore on four continents and knows of it in Saxony, Cornwall and Portugal, backs him; all three go into the sealed roll. In 1926–1927, with the deadline near, Tesla, out of the Five but still the halls' patron, and Szilárd press to shorten the Columbia's cooling times, and Julian asks the Chief for a month. The Chief refuses, citing the White Bluffs compact's remedy: then the device is a month late.

<!-- CR-024: Japan's 1927 banking and foreign-settlement rescue; author-approved mechanism, amounts MODEL. -->
Hirohito becomes Emperor Shōwa in December 1926. Japan enters the spring prosperous, its Treasury full, but its international banks and trading houses still carry the wartime industrial debts, renewed year after year, and the losses of the earthquake. The dollars Japan earned in the war are almost gone; the debts it ran up in the war are still there. When the bad loans come to light, confidence collapses: foreign correspondents discover that several banks have lent to the same borrowers against the same collateral. Owners lose their capital, depositors queue for cash, and foreign lenders refuse to renew most of their short money. London's acceptance houses are the first to pull out, and Tokyo remembers it. Takahashi's three-week moratorium buys time to examine the banks, rebuild them and find a way to pay abroad.

Japan rebuilds its banks with its own money: the Bank of Japan lends yen against collateral it has examined. But yen cannot pay London or New York. Even if the banks, the exporters and the state spent every ounce of gold and every foreign bill they hold, June's payments could not be met, and banks reopened with nothing left to pay for imports would only start the run again. Japan needs foreign credit large enough to keep six weeks of imports in reserve.

**[MODEL — CR-024]** About $400m of losses wipe out the affected banks' owners, and Japan puts in $400m of new capital; the Bank of Japan lends $295m. Three-quarters of $2bn in short foreign funding is not renewed. Outside Pacifican hands, London acceptance houses and British overseas banks hold about $400m of it, Union banks $250m, continental banks $100m and Chinese and Indian banks in Shanghai and Hong Kong $50m. Keeping six weeks of imports in reserve requires about $497m from abroad.

Julian assembles a $600m San Francisco credit, a third from the Treasury and two-thirds from private lenders, to be drawn only as Japan needs it. Japan's government guarantees it; a stabilization agency lends it on to the rebuilt banks, which repay it in five instalments from 1929 to 1933. **[MODEL]** About $497m is drawn, $166m of it public.

Japan's alternatives are to stop paying abroad, let the yen fall or let the banks fail. Saionji advises Hirohito that Julian's help and the naval partnership are the safer course for commerce, the civilian government and the throne; no other market offers anything before the banks must reopen. Julian fears that a fallen cabinet would leave the army dominant. Pacifica's own lenders and exporters have claims and customers in the system its wartime syndicates helped build, and Julian's offer protects them by giving the two countries common authority over the risks they now share. The trust built since 1921 makes the promise credible, and Julian gives his word in Japanese. The crisis brings the long-discussed Concord to its decisive negotiation.

The Senate votes the rescue loan but refuses the treaty: Pacifica would pay sixty-five percent of common costs, Japan's mass threatens dilution, and county boards want leverage against the Commissioners. **[APPROVED — CR-022]** Harlan Tate, Sacramento's senator and Silas's old adjutant, leads the refusal, adding that foreign coupon-clippers already draw nearly $300 million a year from Pacifican industry. Julian claims provisional application under the treaty prerogative pending consent, though the Senate has already refused. This is illegal in substance. <!-- CR-040: Ka Lōkahi's 1927 review --> **[APPROVED — CR-040]** The council of the crowns holds its second ten-year review that year, and its three delegations agree that Japan joins: Ka Lōkahi has grown, and the Concord takes its Hawaiian name. The vote binds the Hawaiian and Philippine crowns. The Realm's delegation votes without the Senate's consent, so for the Realm the application stays unlawful, but the vote shows who holds the order together. The same year Ka Lōkahi takes the Marianas, Carolines and Palau into its trust, **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** with Tutuila, and the navy keeps Guam's harbour on lease. Japan's Emperor ratifies on the Privy Council's conditional advice and the Diet votes its quota; its side follows Japanese law and can lapse if Pacifica never obtains consent.

The Pacific Concord—太平洋協約, Taiheiyō Kyōyaku; la Concordia del Pacífico and ka Lōkahi—joins co-equal crowns. The alternat names each crown first in its own text and gives each precedence at home. The Concord's executive is entrusted to the Crown of Pacifica for five-year terms, because Pacifica pays the larger quota and commands the fleet.<!-- CR-039: the first term runs from 1927 to 1932 --> The first term runs from the provisional application of 1927 to 1932. Julian acts as Sovereign of Pacifica, Mōʻī of Hawaii, Kamehameha VI, and Rey de Filipinas, his three crowns one party in the Concord; he is called Emperor abroad, kōtei in Japanese, while tennō remains Japan's title. He takes no presidential style. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** The Concord takes the place of the compact of 1887, whose fourth renewal falls due in 1927.

Foreign Affairs, the common Navy and their Finance belong to the Concord. Customs, gold parity, the gold cover behind Japan's reserves in San Francisco and the sixty-five/thirty-five quota are agreed matters reviewed at every renewal. <!-- CR-057: the reserve compact --> **[APPROVED — CR-057]** Japan's gold in San Francisco is earmarked and its own to withdraw without notice, and its dollar balances carry half their value in gold; neither ratio can be lowered without Japan's consent. The other holders of reserves in San Francisco hold them on the same terms under compacts of their own. Julian appoints the three common ministers with Japan's prime minister countersigning. At least one must be Japanese by law; convention gives Japan the Navy, while Foreign Affairs and Finance normally remain Pacifican. The western squadron is reserved to the Japanese naval party until the 1932 renewal. Takahashi remains in Tokyo. Japan keeps crown, Diet, cabinet, railways, posts and home army; that army enters common command only for a Concord-declared war.

<!-- CR-054: the Hayama annex and the cartels -->
**[APPROVED — CR-054]** The Concord gives the partners' industrial rivalry a written frame. Pacifica supplies the Concord's aluminium, magnesium, oil products, electrical equipment and heavy machinery, and the common fleet's aircraft, and sells no fertiliser, textiles or ships' fittings into Japan's home market. Japan supplies textiles, its own nitrogen and the silk and consumer trades, builds no aluminium or heavy electrical industry against Pacifica's before 1937, and buys Pacifican equipment for its hydro. Outward the two act as one economic party. **[PROPOSED — CR-054]** The frame is an annex to the Concord's economic articles, agreed at the first Hayama Council, and Japan's share includes shipbuilding below capital ships. **[APPROVED — CR-054]** Japan gains cheap aluminium and oil for a navy and an air arm it could not otherwise afford, and Pacifica a market that does not fall with sterling; the Restoration officers call it dependence, and Wakatsuki and Shidehara call it the alliance paying for itself. In 1930 the Concord joins the European nitrogen convention as a single party, and London and Ludwigshafen recognise its Asian market; Pacifica stays outside the European aluminium cartel of 1931, takes price rather than a quota in the slump, and keeps the Concord pool's price inside.

Sixty delegates from each legislature meet separately at Thalassia and exchange written messages. After three disagreements they vote together without debate. A Common Tribunal of three judges from each side, with alternating chair, decides competences. English and Japanese texts are equally authentic; common officials pass both examinations. Navy command and signals remain English, with Japanese the western squadron's working language. Japan grants English no domestic official status.

Victoria supports the Concord and opposes its illegal imposition. In 1928 she wins two-thirds Senate consent and an Act of Indemnity. **[PROPOSED — CR-022]** She calls on Tate once at Sacramento; neither mentions Donner. His bloc splits and he abstains. Julian accepts a five-year first term instead of ten, quota review at renewal, Pacifica's delegates chosen half by each chamber and a Polytechnic audit reported to the Senate. The constitutional case at Monterey is mooted before judgment. The legal cure gives the friendship institutions that can outlast it.

<!-- CR-046: the Baku review and the single sheet -->
**[APPROVED — CR-046]** In the spring of 1927, in the weeks the London acceptance houses will not renew Japan's bills, London invokes the seventh-year review of the Baku agreement. The Royal Navy, which burns oil, wants first call on the field and a British operator, and Britain holds the pipeline to Batum. In May the Metropolitan Police raid Pacifica's trade agency in Moorgate and find nothing. In June Kashevarov, now head of the Reserve Bank's Russian department, comes to the Treasury and lays one sheet in front of the Chancellor, Winston Churchill: the British mission's promise of credit from Omsk, November 1918, with a ledger reference pencilled in the margin. He makes no threat. Churchill knows the account. He does not ask where the sheet came from, because it tells him. The review is withdrawn: Pacifica stays operator, the navy takes a fixed allowance at Batum at market price, and London stops looking. Nothing is published.

**[APPROVED — CR-046]** The Chronicler, who holds the whole file, says what the sheet stood for. In December 1916 the account paid for the night Rasputin died in the Yusupov palace. Through the winter it paid the men around Guchkov who meant to force Nicholas from his throne in March, before he could make a separate peace. In November 1918 it was to pay for Kolchak, and in 1920 London paid again to have the record burned. **[PROPOSED — CR-046]** Samuel Hoare, whose station kept the account, sits in Baldwin's cabinet. Churchill, Secretary for War in 1920, never knew about the Copenhagen gold, and cannot know what else Thalassia holds. He understands at last what Victoria meant in the cathedral.

<!-- CR-049: the three prongs and the demonstration at sea -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Britain gives way on three prongs. The economic prong is the debt: London owes Pacifica gold dollars. The political prong is the Petrograd account. The decisive prong is military. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** In the autumn of 1927, **[APPROVED — CR-050]** in open ocean between the two crowns, south of the great-circle route in the empty centre of the subtropical gyre, away from the northern fisheries and the westerlies that would carry the water's poison to Alaska and Pacifica's own coast, and far enough from Hawaiʻi that the islands' waters stay clean, **[APPROVED — CR-049]** a moored line of Pacifica's own old capital ships, kept as hulks since the 1919 limits, is destroyed by a single uranium device, **[APPROVED — CR-051]** armed by King, whom Julian chooses for the sea, and **[APPROVED — CR-049]** fired by wireless from a cruiser about twenty miles off, before the flagships of both crowns, the naval attachés of every navy and the Five's newsreel cameras. A column of water rises a mile high and the ships within about a thousand yards sink. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The zone was closed weeks before, Japan's fisheries authorities warned their fleets, and the target ships are never reboarded; the ships still afloat are scuttled, and the zone's measurements are published. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The device weighs about four tonnes and is the equal of ten thousand tons of Giant Powder dynamite; its light uranium was separated in the Works' magnet halls on Sierra hydropower. Three exist, and one is spent. **[MODEL — CR-051]** Material for about one more is in the shops, so the demonstration is not a bluff made with the last device in hand. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The design is sealed in the Thalassia archive. The demonstration ends the confrontation with Britain in Pacifica's favour and solidifies the Concord, whose two crowns watched it together. <!-- CR-050: the seal opened -->**[APPROVED — CR-050]** In the days of the demonstration Julian and Vance open the seal, a year inside Victoria's term. <!-- CR-051: the opening in two parts -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** The record opens in two parts. The Works publishes the first through the corrections column: the papers of 1908 and 1909 in facsimile, the late neutrons of 1910–1911 and Meitner's conjecture of the light isotope, with the sealed roll of honour for them, Ralston College's protest of 1910 and Hillebrand's withdrawal from the prize of 1911: Hillebrand and his group for the split, the counting team for the chain, Peirce as respondent, Meitner for the light isotope. Hahn is named as the independent second finder. By 1927 Europe knows all of this in substance; the opening gives it back its names. The second part of the record is dated, witnessed and sealed for twenty years from 1927, to the autumn of 1947: Dempster's proof and the proof team, the contest and the feed, the halls, the device and its test, the chemistry of the bred elements, gaseous diffusion and the Chief's and Szilárd's forecasts. <!-- CR-077: the machines in the record --> **[APPROVED — CR-077]** The first part names the counting room's trigger counters of 1908–1911 among its tools. The second describes the Works' relay machines of 1913 and the stored-program machine it builds inside the wall in 1929, which runs the breeding line's neutron histories by random trials. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** The people named in it know their credit is safe. The custom that gave Kate Curran her credit back in 1872 gives it back on the largest scale it will ever have. Both crowns honour Nagaoka, who kept Pacifica's secret from his own government because he had given his word, and argued against it to the end. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Szilárd, the inner circle's sharpest critic from inside, designs the opening. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** He accepts the second part's sealing as its price, the science free now and the factory twenty years later, and his argument against it is sealed with it. Everything tied to November 1928 is renewed. Every member of the inner circle signs a new bond for the second part, to 1947, dated, witnessed and promised an opening like the first; those who refuse retire on a full pension, free to speak of everything published and bound on the rest. The Compact of the Tuolumne is renewed to 1947, Ralston College's trustees again signing under written protest, and the Me-Wuk lease is renewed to 1947 with a larger fee and the Poopenaut title confirmed. A secret with a written end still gives a man of conviction a reason to wait, but twenty more years is a long wait, and the college knows it. The Chief retires after the demonstration.

**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Churchill, shown one sheet of the Petrograd account in June, sees the column of water in the newsreels in the autumn; Fowler's memo is in his own files. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** From the opening, English and the world take Pacifica's word, fission; Hahn's *Spaltung* stays German. <!-- CR-051: the lead after 1927 -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Britain now knows that separation works and has the memo of 1926 in its files. **[MODEL — CR-051]** The Works reckons that Britain, fully committed from 1928, can have a first device in five to seven years, about 1933–1935, and nothing before 1932. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** Germany comes later, starting from power reactors, and the Union later still, its best people being in Pacifica. Europe's road to a pile runs through heavy water: Europe's graphite, made without Pacifica's purification, absorbs too many neutrons and blocks the other road, and Pacifica does not correct the error, but heavy hydrogen is public in Dempster's survey and Norway's cheap electrolysis can concentrate it. A European heavy-water pile, aimed at power, runs about 1930–1931. By 1932 the element such piles breed is an idea in Europe, with no production pile, no chemistry and no weapon route.

<!-- CR-058: Canada's neutrality and the Great Bear contract; Mexico in 1927 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Canada stays neutral through the confrontation. Its West and Québec are in Pacifica's orbit, and Ottawa has a contract to keep: when London, needing uranium, asks it to set aside the Works' Great Bear contract for imperial priority, Ottawa keeps the contract, and the Empire's North American uranium stays under contract to the other side. In the same year Article 27 and the Union's need for oil make intervention in Mexico tempting in Washington. Pacifica defuses it with Huasteca's supply contracts and a settlement of the kind Dwight Morrow, the Union's ambassador from 29 October 1927, makes with Calles in 1927–1928, around Mexico's oil-law amendment of December 1927; Wall Street's peace lobby backs it. In 1927 Standard licenses IG Farben's process for making oil from coal, and the agreements of 1927–1929 bind the Union's oil to the Germany its banks lend to.

<!-- CR-053: oil abroad, and the settlement's quiet terms -->
**[APPROVED — CR-053]** As the home fields pass their peak, the oil trust buys positions abroad the Pacifican way, with money, partners and published terms, and no flag. Its first seat is in Lago Petroleum at Maracaibo. **[PROPOSED — CR-053]** It takes about a fifth of the company in 1923–1924, while it still belongs to independents, so that Standard, arriving in 1925, finds a Pacifican minority already seated. <!-- CR-058: Lago in 1925 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-058]** Doheny's Pan American, which would have taken Lago in 1925, the year after he leaves the Five, is barred by Thalassia, because Venezuela must stay the Union's own source, and Doheny is overruled a second time. **[PROPOSED — CR-053]** It takes a minority in Tropical Oil at De Mares in 1920–1921, **[APPROVED — CR-058]** when the Texas Company buys it, **[PROPOSED — CR-053]** while the Andian pipeline is being financed; Lobitos and the northern Peruvian concessions go from 1924 to **[APPROVED — CR-058]** Doheny's Pan American, **[PROPOSED — CR-053]** a Pacifican company in which the Union takes a reciprocal fifth, giving Wall Street's peace lobby a stake in the Pacific coast's oil; and in 1926 it buys Frank Holmes's Bahrain and Kuwait options, which no British company wants. The settlement of 1927 carries two quiet terms: Britain waives the sheikhdoms' nationality clause for the Pacifican company, and consents to an enlarged American group in Mesopotamia. **[APPROVED — CR-053]** Pacifica joins the Union's group there rather than displacing it, and the trust keeps the Red Line agreement of 1928 as a shared discipline. **[MODEL — CR-053]** The stakes cost about $65 million in 1920–1928; their crude reaches about 20 million barrels in 1929, about a fifteenth of the Realm's imports, and the sheikhdoms and Kirkuk are the 1930s' oil bought at 1920s prices.

<!-- CR-058: the Caribbean understanding of 1921–1923 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Colombia keeps Panama, because the canal is neutral under Colombian sovereignty and run by Pacifica's company, and its partners are Pacifica and the Gulf. The Gulf resents Union companies in the Caribbean: their crude competes with its own in the Union's market, and with Pensacola and Puerto Rico a Union presence on the mainland would encircle the Gulf. The Caribbean understanding of 1921–1923, brokered by Pacifica, gives each its part. Venezuela, across the Caribbean from Puerto Rico and under a Gómez who wants many bidders, is the Union's source, the one the Gulf does not control: Standard, Guffey and Shell lead there, the trust keeps its minority at Lago, and the Gulf's drilling crews, Hughes bits and roughnecks earn from the boom. Colombia leans to the Gulf and Pacifica: the Texas Company holds De Mares, most of Cartagena's crude goes through the canal, and Guffey buys the Barco concession in 1926, which Colombia revokes the same year and restores in 1931, so the Union's one toehold is real but insecure. Caribbean and Mexican heavy crude goes to the Atlantic seaboard as fuel oil, and Gulf and Sequoyah crude to the Midwest's refineries, so the Gulf keeps the Midwest. The rule of 1898 for Cuba, no foreign bases, is extended to the Caribbean mainland and binds Pacifica too, whose canal rights in Panama and Nicaragua are not bases: Union companies may own oil there, the Union Navy may not station ships there, and its bases at Puerto Rico and Pensacola stay as they are. Pacifica is the arbiter owed by both, Colombia comes into its orbit, its Venezuelan minority brings it information and crude, and the Union's oil is secure.

<!-- CR-078: radiotelephone, echoes and radio location --> **[APPROVED — CR-078]** Shortwave crosses the ocean from about 1920. From 1922 Thalassia and Honolulu telephone San Francisco by radio, and from 1928 Thalassia telephones Tokyo; state business still goes by cable, and the cipher room scrambles the Tokyo voice link and assumes it is overheard. In the year of the first Council of the Two Crowns Julian speaks to the Emperor's household by radiotelephone, a call chosen for the newspapers rather than for business. **[PROPOSED — CR-078]** From about 1915 the cable ships and the navy sound the Pacific's depths by echo and the coast survey publishes the charts, and from about 1925 the fishing fleets find sardine and salmon by echo. **[APPROVED — CR-078]** Radio beacons guide the air arm and the Oakland–Los Angeles airway from about 1920, and Yagi's Tohoku antenna of 1926 is adopted at once for direction finding. In 1924–26 the navy's radio laboratory at San Diego times pulse echoes on metre waves with the counting room's methods and shows them on a cathode-ray tube; by 1932 a few sets stand at Oʻahu and on the fleet's largest ships, plotted by machine, and Okabe's split-anode magnetron is in trial. Radio location is never patented or sold: the General Staff's sealed roll lists it and its makers, as it lists the cipher room's machines, for as long as the secret holds. AT&T's long lines, on Pacifican repeater valves, reach Denver in 1911, and the first New York–San Francisco call runs over the Realm's long lines from the Front Range. The Union's broadcasting starts commercially in 1920 with no public-service article, and its revanchist press calls the air school Thalassia's voice in every farmhouse.

<!-- CR-079: talking pictures, television's laboratory, the new music and tape --> **[APPROVED — CR-079]** Sound newsreels of the war play from 1916–18, and the first talking features come from Los Angeles in 1919–20, in English and Spanish versions. Honolulu's studios make films in Japanese and Hawaiian, San Francisco's in Cantonese and Manila's in Tagalog, and most of the Realm's cinemas are wired for sound by about 1922. The Office's films go out through the press estate under Victoria's rule of 1913. The Union's studios, in New York and Chicago, license Lauste's track and make talkies from about 1922, and in 1923 the Union sets a quota on Pacifican films. In 1919 Kashevarov's Reserve Bank office at Omsk recruits Vladimir Zworykin, who is buying radio for the Directory's war ministry, for the power estate's laboratory; in 1922–25 his tube shows moving pictures scanned from film, and in 1923 he describes a storage camera. About 1921 the navy's radio laboratory at San Diego finds that a high-frequency bias clears the noise from the cipher room's wire recordings. About 1925 George Beauchamp, a Texas-born vaudeville steel guitarist in Los Angeles, makes a magnetic pickup with Honda's magnet steel from Tohoku and a Jensen amplifier, and an electric "Spanish" guitar follows about 1928. Jazz comes by record and over the air from New Orleans and the Union's cities. The music the Realm's players make in the 1920s draws on Hawaiian chant and song, the Polynesian music of the islands' visitors and settlers, Japanese music and the West's concert hall and dance hall. It is not a variety of any of them and stands to its sources as jazz stood to its own: nobody predicted it. The press estate backs Philo Farnsworth's image dissector of 1927, and it and Zworykin's camera are licensed side by side. The demonstration's sound newsreel of 1927 records the explosion's report reaching the ships twenty miles off a minute and a half after the flash. About 1928 the press estate coats cellulose acetate film from its own works with iron oxide, and by 1932 the stations record and edit programmes on biased tape.

<!-- CR-077: the Carquinez machine and the machine that holds its orders --> **[APPROVED — CR-077]** After the peace the navy's range tables, the weather tables and the Office's own justify a general electronic calculator. Fuller builds it at Carquinez with the Office's workshop: about 8,000 valves **[MODEL]**, decimal rings, plugboards and card input and output. It runs from the spring of 1921, and a new program is a new pattern of wires that takes days to set up. In its first months a team of the Office's examined women changes failed valves at night, and the two-route check finds the machine's errors before anyone outside does. Sophia Levy, born at Alameda in 1888 to parents born in the gold country and a Berkeley doctor of 1920, is its first order-writer and keeps the first library of tested set-ups. The halls' ion orbits never go to it: the Works computes them on its own relay machines inside the wall, built from 1913. From 1922 to 1925 Fuller's group turns the quartz plates of Langevin's echo-ranging into acoustic delay lines, a few dozen numbers in each tube and a thousand in a battery of them, and from 1925 Hollerith's company builds the Office a magnetic drum. Leslie Comrie, born at Pukekohe in 1893 and wounded in France with the New Zealand Expeditionary Force, studies at Carquinez instead of Cambridge. From 1925 he runs the Office's table service, which sells tables and machine time to every navy and almanac in the world, Britain's included. In 1927 John von Neumann, who came about 1926, reads a report at a challenge evening on a machine whose store holds its orders and its numbers alike. Levy is the respondent: orders that can change themselves can also corrupt themselves, and the room holds to the Office's rule that a program is tested by a second route before its results are published. A second respondent, from Fuller's group, shows that their memorandum of 1925 already stored orders beside numbers. The corrections column prints the report with the memorandum as prior work, and von Neumann signs the correction. In 1928 the first stored-program machine runs at Carquinez, with about 3,000 valves and 512 numbers in delay lines and the drum behind them **[MODEL]**. Its first published result is an orbit of Levy's, which can be checked against the sky.

<!-- CR-077: the machine rooms, the weather and the measured realm --> **[APPROVED — CR-077]** The Office's workshop builds the machines of 1928–1930. From 1930 Hollerith's company, a year after his death, builds the Realm machine under licence at the published royalty. By 1932 about fifteen run **[MODEL]**: two at Carquinez, one at the Office of Public Measures for the census of 1930, one at the Reserve Bank and the clearing house, two for the General Staff and the fleet, the Weather Office's at San Francisco and Honolulu, Ralston College's, three or four for the Patrimony and the railways, and one at Tokyo. Japan buys the Tokyo machine from Pacifica in 1931, as the Hayama annex expects; it computes typhoon tracks for Japan's coast and stands beside the station that lights Tokyo as a second emblem of the friendship. Levy heads the order library from 1929. By 1931 it holds several hundred tested routines, each with its maker's name, and the orders are written in symbols and translated by the machine itself. Storage tubes from the electron optics of Philo Farnsworth, born at Beaver in Deseret in 1906, who makes his image dissector in San Francisco in 1927, are tried; so are magnetic cores of metal tape at Carquinez and of the new ferrite in Tokyo, and crystal amplifiers. None is in service by 1932. **[OPEN]** Cores and crystal amplifiers after 1932. **[APPROVED — CR-077]** Carl-Gustaf Rossby comes in 1926 and builds an airway weather service between Oakland and Los Angeles in 1927–1928. From 1929 the Weather Office tries Richardson's forecast by numbers on Carquinez's machine. The forecasts fail as his did, with impossible pressure changes, and Levy answers the Weather Office's complaint with the Office's old rule that a calculation that runs is not an account that is true. In 1931–1932 Rossby's simplified equations, run on the upper-air soundings his airway pilots and the navy's aircraft have flown daily since 1928, make the trial forecasts hold. **[APPROVED — CR-081]** The Weather Office asks the Customs for soundings at Huludao and Shanghai, so that they can be tried on the Manchurian and Yangtze harvests. **[OPEN]** Daily machine forecasts after 1932. **[APPROVED — CR-077]** The census of 1930 is counted in weeks, and from 1931 the county tables are published monthly, so the regency's case, measurably better lives, is tested in print every month. In 1931 a valley county meets the four standards in every monthly table, while the guest workers it houses are counted in the county that hired them and the infant deaths in their camps appear in neither. From 1931 the Reserve Bank knows its cover daily, the clearing house settles overnight and the Patrimony publishes its accounts quarterly. The census machine puts the hand tabulators out of work as the dial put the operators, and the Office's school takes them. In the Union the Census Bureau refuses to pay a Pacifican royalty and builds its own machines, and a card industry forms around them under Watson, while the Union's banks license from San Francisco and its engineers bet on analogue machines such as the differential analyser at MIT (1931). Hearst's press calls the machines the Office's brains, and in Honolulu the *mana* debate takes up whether a machine's work has *mana* or only its makers do.

<!-- CR-049: Nishina, Tokyo's atomic power and the newcomers -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Atomic energy becomes the emblem of Pacifica's friendship with Japan. Nishina Yoshio is the programme's Japanese co-lead, and an atomic power station lights Tokyo. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Nagaoka's silence in Tokyo is the proof Julian needs that a Japanese scientist can carry the seal, and in 1922 Nishina, after his first European years, enters the walled college. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** In Tokyo only the Prince Regent, Hirohito, is told, through Julian's own tie with him. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** On 7 February 1925 Hillebrand dies in Honolulu, where he was born. In his last weeks Julian visits him and tells him the year the seal will be opened. Hillebrand has kept the seal because his record is dated and safe, and he of all men knows that a claim lives or dies by its record; because the term guarantees the opening; because he is a native son among *nā Kahuna*; and because he believes Peirce's argument. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** No atomic power station can be built in public before the seal is opened, but Nishina's group designs the Tokyo station inside the wall from about 1924. At the demonstration Julian announces that the Works will light Tokyo, the weapon and the promise on the same day. Ground is broken in 1928, the year of Hirohito's enthronement, with Japanese engineers trained by Nishina's group; first power comes in 1931, and the station is opened on 29 April 1932, the Emperor's birthday, at the close of the Concord's first term. Tokyo, which burned in the earthquake of 1923, is lit by the Concord. The station stands inland on rock, above any tsunami's reach, built to the Polytechnic's earthquake code. Its fuel is leased from the Works and returned to Pacifica, so Japan has the energy and the bred elements never stay on its soil: the emblem of friendship is also an instrument of dependence. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** The station is of about eighty megawatts. **[OPEN]** How the station is paid for. **[MODEL — CR-050]** Four Pacifican stations of about forty megawatts each are begun in 1929 and finished after Tokyo's first power: two are loaded in 1932 and two finished after it. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Eugene Wigner comes about 1924, John von Neumann about 1926 and Edward Teller about 1930, because Pacifica is where physics is done.

<!-- CR-050: the breeding line and the three layers of fusion -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** Wigner becomes the master of the reactors, and the energy stored in graphite under irradiation is found and named after him. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** Trained as a chemical engineer for his father's tannery in Budapest, he has left it against his father's wishes; in the reactor hall he is the engineer's brake on Szilárd's haste and refuses to build the breeder bigger than he can control. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** In 1925–1926 Szilárd and Wigner propose a reactor that breeds more fuel than it burns, so that the common isotope, and one day thorium, can be turned into fuel and Pacifica's hold on uranium multiplied. The chemistry of the bred elements stays inside the wall. About 1930–1932 an experimental breeder runs, and its first electricity lights a few bulbs in a shed. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The detailed theory of the stars' hydrogen burning, suggested in Europe a generation earlier, comes with quantum mechanics in the mid-1920s. Heavy hydrogen appears in Dempster's published survey of isotopes. In the 1920s Rutherford, outside the wall, fires heavy-hydrogen nuclei at heavy hydrogen and sees them fuse, releasing far less energy than the beam costs, and sincerely calls power from such reactions moonshine; the best-known physicist in Pacifica, honestly sceptical in public and incurious about the wall, is the seal's best cover. Langmuir names and founds the physics of the hot, charged gas in the halls' ion sources in 1928, and from the late 1920s a small fusion programme of tinkerers, experimenters and engineers yields knowledge, not power. Its defining moment is a premature claim of fusion, publicly retracted in the corrections column.

<!-- CR-051: the bottleneck moat and keeping moving -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** After 1927 the lead is defended by the next plant, not the old secret. Every bottleneck of the halls is in Pacifican hands: graphite from petroleum coke tested batch by batch, Langmuir's condensation pump of 1916 and its oils, the hard valves and the regulating gear of the works Tesla and Steinmetz founded, Hillebrand's fluorine and uranium chemistry, beryllium, and the only shops able to wind coils of that size. Above all the know-how is in people, the examined operators, the recovery chemists and the vacuum workshops, and what a rival needs most after 1927 is not a fact but ten years of practice. Exports of these goods are restricted as the commercial property of a state champion. <!-- CR-077: the valves the restriction covers --> **[APPROVED — CR-077]** The restriction covers the halls' high-power, high-vacuum and regulating valves and the condensation pumps, not receiving or computing valves. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** A gaseous-diffusion plant is built in 1930–1932, so that when Britain, reading the power tables and Dempster's published instrument, begins to copy the halls after 1927, it copies the method Pacifica is about to abandon. Each year Pacifica operates, its absolute lead in practice grows even as its relative lead in knowledge shrinks. **[MODEL — CR-051]** With gaseous diffusion feeding the halls, output rises from about 7 to 18 kilograms a month over 1928–1932, and by 1932 the Works has made material for some fifteen devices in all.

<!-- CR-055: the reserve named, 1928 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-055]** In the winter after the demonstration Langmuir and Ryan put one map before Julian and the Council of the Seal: the Columbia from the Canadian line to the sea, its five dams, Rock Island among them, and Chief Joseph begun, the line south, the station at White Bluffs, and the Office's curve of the Northwest's load drawn under the river's. In 1928 the two curves touch. Ryan's argument is that the river is the only place on earth under one hand where the atom's next decade can be fed, Langmuir's that separation at scale, the breeder and the reactor metals are steps in power. Julian keeps building, Chief Joseph being already decided, and lets the Works be the customer for whom the next gap is kept. The Works' charter and the power estate's statutes write down the rule the river has been built by: it is developed in order of fish passage and consent, and no dam is built that salmon cannot pass. The gaseous-diffusion plant of 1930–1932 stands at the Columbia station, behind the fence, on Rocky Reach's and Chief Joseph's power, the reserve's customer. The Works' metals house at Wenatchee, whose published purpose is alloys for the aircraft and electrical champions, makes zirconium for the breeder by 1929–1932. In 1930, through the trust's Norwegian trade, the Works contracts for Norsk Hydro's heavy water as research chemicals, knowing since 1917 what it is buying, and holds most of the world's supply from then on: Europe's first pile, already designed, runs small, and its successors wait for heavy water; the contract delays Europe's piles without preventing them.

<!-- CR-049: the atomic programme in figures -->
<!-- CR-051: the programme's cost follows the financial workbook (finance/CR-051-054-055-workbook.md) -->**[MODEL — CR-049, re-timed by CR-050; costs with CR-051; re-synced by CR-057]** From 1864 to 1932 Pacifica and its concessions mine about 5,800 tonnes of uranium: about 2,760 from the Western Slope, 2,270 from Shinkolobwe, 390 from Joachimsthal, 300 as a by-product before 1909 and 84 from Fergana. About 500 tonnes go to energy, 550 to weapons (250 by 1927, the rest for devices after it) and 150 to research, and about 4,600 tonnes, four fifths of the whole, lie in reserve in 1932. The programme costs about $1.4 billion in 1913 dollars, at its peak about half a percent of national product, in 1921, while the halls are built; the weapon to 1927 accounts for about $700 million.
